Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Saṃyuttanikāye

In the Saṃyutta Nikāya

Saḷāyatanavaggaṭīkā

The Subcommentary on the Chapter on the Six Sense Bases

1. Saḷāyatanasaṃyuttaṃ

1. The Connected Discourses on the Six Sense Bases

1. Aniccavaggo

1. The Chapter on Impermanence

1. Ajjhattāniccasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on Internal Impermanence

1. Cakkhatīti [V 2.0001][M 2.0281] cakkhu, ñāṇaṃ, yathāsabhāvato ārammaṇassa jānanena samavisamaṃ ācikkhantaṃ viya pavattatīti attho. Tathā maṃsacakkhu. Tampi hi rūpadassane cakkhatīti cakkhu. Buddhānaṃyeva cakkhūti buddhacakkhu, asādhāraṇato hi sattasantānesu sassatucchedadiṭṭhi anulomikañāṇayathābhūtañāṇānañceva kāmarāgānusayādīnañca yāthāvato vibhāvitañāṇaṃ āsayānusayañāṇaṃ indriyaparopariyattañāṇañca. Heṭṭhimā tayo maggā catusaccadhammesu vuttākārena pavattiyā dhamme cakkhūti dhammacakkhu, tathā tesaṃ phalāni taṃtaṃpaṭipakkhesu paṭippassaddhipahānavasena [V 2.0002] pavattanato. Samantato sabbadhammesu cakkhukiccasādhanato samantacakkhu, sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ. Dibbavihārasannissayena laddhabbato devānaṃ dibbacakkhu viyāti taṃ dibbacakkhu, abhiññāviseso. Ālokaṃ vaḍḍhetvā rūpadassanato “ālokapharaṇenā”ti vuttaṃ. “Idaṃ dukkhaṃ ariyasaccanti me, bhikkhave, pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu cakkhuṃ udapādī”tiādinā (saṃ. ni. 5.1081; mahāva. 15) nayena [M 2.0282] āgatattā catusaccaparicchedakañāṇaṃ “paññācakkhū”ti vuttaṃ. Tadidaṃ “vipassanāñāṇa”nti vadanti, “vipassanāmaggaphalapaccavekkhaṇañāṇānī”ti apare.

1. ‘It sees’ (cakkhati), hence ‘eye’ (cakkhu), that is, knowledge; the meaning is that it proceeds as if pointing out an object as regular or irregular by knowing it according to its intrinsic nature. Similarly with the fleshly eye. For that too is called ‘eye’ because it ‘sees’ when seeing forms. The eye of the Buddhas alone is the Buddha-eye, for it is unique among the mental continuities of beings, being the knowledge that has accurately clarified the views of eternalism and annihilation, conformity knowledge, knowledge of things as they are, and the latent tendencies such as sensual lust; and it is the knowledge of dispositions and latent tendencies, and the knowledge of the superiority and inferiority of faculties. The lower three paths are the Dhamma-eye in regard to the Four Noble Truths, as they proceed in the manner described; likewise their fruits, because they proceed by way of tranquillizing and abandoning their respective opposites. Because it accomplishes the function of an eye with regard to all phenomena all-around, the all-around eye is the knowledge of omniscience. Because it is to be obtained through reliance on a divine abiding, it is like the divine eye of the devas; thus it is the divine eye, a specific kind of direct knowledge. Because it sees forms by extending light, it is spoken of as ‘by the suffusion of light.’ The knowledge that discerns the four truths is called the ‘eye of wisdom,’ as stated in the passage: ‘Bhikkhus, concerning things unheard before, the eye arose in me...’ (SN 56.11; Vin I 11). This they call ‘insight knowledge’; others say it refers to the knowledges of insight, path, fruition, and reviewing.

Paccayabhūtehi etehi abhisambharīyantīti sambhārā, upatthambhabhūtā catusamuṭṭhānikarūpā. Saha sambhārehīti sasambhāraṃ. Mahābhūtānaṃ upādāya pasīdatīti pasādo. Akkhikūpake akkhipaṭalehīti ubhohi akkhidalehi. Sambhavoti āpodhātumeva sambhavabhūtamāha. Idha “terasa sambhārā”ti vuttaṃ. Aṭṭhasāliniyaṃ (dha. sa. aṭṭha. 596) pana saṇṭhānena saddhiṃ “cuddasa sambhārā”ti āgataṃ. Tattha saṇṭhānanti vaṇṇāyatanameva parimaṇḍalādisaṇṭhānabhūtaṃ. Visuṃ vacanaṃ pana nesaṃ tathābhūtānaṃ atathābhūtānañca āpodhātuvaṇṇāyatanānaṃ yathāvutte maṃsapiṇḍe vijjamānattā. Sambhavassa catudhātunissitehi saha vuttassa dhātuttayanissitatā yojetabbā. Diṭṭhimaṇḍaleti abhimukhaṃ ṭhitānaṃ paṭibimbapaññāyanaṭṭhānabhūte cakkhusaññitāya diṭṭhiyā pavattiṭṭhānabhūte maṇḍale. Sanniviṭṭhanti etena cakkhupasādassa anekakalāpagatabhāvo dassito. Tathā hi so satta akkhipaṭalāni abhibyāpetvā vattati. Yasmā so satta akkhipaṭalāni byāpetvā ṭhitehi attano nissayabhūtehi katūpakāraṃ taṃnissiteheva āyuvaṇṇādīhi anupālitaparivāritaṃ tisantatirūpasamuṭṭhāpakehi utucittāhārehi upatthambhiyamānaṃ hutvā tiṭṭhati. Rūpadassanasamatthanti attānaṃ nissāya pavattaviññāṇassa vasena rūpāyatanadassanasamatthaṃ. Vitthārakathāti tassa cakkhuno sotādīnañca hetupaccayādivasena ceva lakkhaṇādivasena ca vitthārakathā.

Because they are brought together by these conditions, they are called ‘constituents’; they are the supporting material phenomena born of the four causes. ‘With constituents’ means together with the constituents. Because it becomes clear in dependence on the great elements, it is called ‘sensitivity.’ ‘In the eye socket’; ‘by the eye membranes’ means by both eye-layers. ‘Source’ refers to the water element itself as the ground of origin. Here it is said, ‘thirteen constituents.’ In the Aṭṭhasālinī (Dhs-a 596), however, it is given as ‘fourteen constituents,’ including shape. There, ‘shape’ means the color base itself, which has a configuration of roundness, etc. The separate mention is due to the existence, in the aforesaid lump of flesh, of both the water element and color base that are of that nature and those that are not. The dependence on the three elements should be connected with the source, which was stated along with its dependence on the four elements. ‘In the circle of vision’ means in the circle that is the locus for the arising of the eye, which is called ‘vision,’ and is the place where the reflection of those standing opposite appears. ‘Established’: by this is shown the fact that the eye-sensitivity consists of numerous groups of matter. For it exists pervading the seven eye-membranes. Because it stands, having received assistance from its own supporting bases which pervade the seven eye-membranes, being maintained and surrounded by life, color, etc., which are dependent on them, and being propped up by temperature, consciousness, and nutriment, which generate the three continuities of matter. ‘Capable of seeing forms’ means capable of seeing the form-object by means of the consciousness that arises dependent on it. ‘The detailed explanation’ means the extensive discussion of the eye and other faculties in terms of causes, conditions, characteristics, etc.

Sammasanacāracittanti vipassanāya pavattiṭṭhānabhūtaṃ vipassitabbaṃ cittaṃ. Keci “vipassanupagatakiriyamayacitta”nti vadanti, taṃ tesaṃ matimattaṃ. Tīṇi lakkhaṇāni dassetvā vipassanaṃ ussukkāpetvā arahattassa pāpanavasena desanāya pavattattā.

‘The mind that is the sphere of comprehension’ is the mind to be subjected to insight, which is the basis for the arising of insight. Some say it is ‘a functional consciousness connected with insight,’ but that is merely their opinion. This is because the teaching proceeds by showing the three characteristics, rousing diligence for insight, and leading to the attainment of arahantship.

Ajjhattāniccasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Discourse on Internal Impermanence is concluded.

2-3. Ajjhattadukkhasuttādivaṇṇanā

2-3. Commentary on the Discourses beginning with the Internal Suffering Discourse

2-3. Dve [V 2.0003][M 2.0283] lakkhaṇānīti dukkhānattalakkhaṇāni. Ekaṃ lakkhaṇanti anattalakkhaṇaṃ. Sesānīti vuttāvasesāni lakkhaṇāni. Tehīti yehi dutiyatatiyāni suttāni desitāni, tehi. Sallakkhitānīti sammadeva upadhāritāni. Ettakenāti dvinnaṃ ekasseva vā lakkhaṇassa kathanena.

2-3. ‘Two characteristics’ means the characteristics of suffering and not-self. ‘One characteristic’ means the characteristic of not-self. ‘The rest’ means the remaining characteristics that were mentioned. ‘By these’ means by which the second and third suttas were taught. ‘Well noted’ means rightly discerned. ‘By this much’ means by the statement of two characteristics or of just one.

Ajjhattadukkhasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the discourses beginning with ‘Internal Suffering’ is concluded.

4-6. Bāhirāniccasuttādivaṇṇanā

4-6. Commentary on the External Impermanence Discourse and Others

4-6. Vuttasadisovāti “dve lakkhaṇānī”tiādinā vuttasadiso eva. Nayoti atidesanayo.

4-6. ‘Like what has been said’: it is just like what has been said beginning with the words, ‘two characteristics.’ ‘The method’: this is the method of extension.

Bāhirāniccasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the External Impermanence Discourse and others is finished.

7-12. Ajjhattāniccātītānāgatasuttādivaṇṇanā

7-12. Commentary on the Discourses on Internal Impermanence, Past and Future, and Others

7-12. Sallakkhetvāti atītānāgatānaṃ avijjamānattā gāhassa daḷhatāya sallakkhetvā. Paccuppannesu vijjamānattā balavatā taṇhādigāhena vipassanāvīthiṃ paṭipādetuṃ kilamantānaṃ vineyyānaṃ vasena.

7-12. ‘Having discerned’: one discerns because the past and future are non-existent and because of the firmness of the grasp. This is said for the sake of those disciples to be trained who are struggling to establish the course of insight into present phenomena, since these exist and are subject to the powerful grasp of craving and so on.

Ajjhattāniccātītānāgatasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Discourses on Internal Impermanence, Past, and Future, and Others is finished.

Aniccavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Chapter on Impermanence is finished.

2. Yamakavaggo

2. The Chapter on Pairs

1-4. Paṭhamapubbesambodhasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-4. Commentary on the First Discourse Before Enlightenment and Others

13-16. Dvīsupi suttesu āyatanānaṃ vasena desanā ekarasāvāti “paṭhamadutiyesū”ti ekajjhaṃ paduddhāro kato. Āhito ahaṃmāno etthāti attā, attabhāvo. Attānamadhi ajjhattaṃ, tappariyāpannattā tattha bhavāni ajjhattikāni, tesaṃ ajjhattikānaṃ. Ajjhattañca [M 2.0284] nāma ajjhattajjhattaṃ[V 2.0004], niyakajjhattaṃ, gocarajjhattaṃ, visayajjhattanti catubbidhaṃ. Tattha ajjhattajjhattaṃ ajjhatte bhavanti ajjhattikanti āha “ajjhattajjhattavasena ajjhattikāna”nti. Tesaṃ cakkhādīnaṃ ajjhattesupi ajjhattikabhāvo adhikasinehavatthutāyāti āha “chandarāgassa adhimattabalavatāyā”ti. Idāni tattha tamatthaṃ paṭiyoginā saddhiṃ udāharaṇavasena dassento “manussānaṃ hī”tiādimāha. Taṃ uttānameva. Bāhirānīti ajjhattikato bahi bhavāni.

13-16. Since in both discourses the teaching has a single flavor by way of the sense bases, the explanation of the term is made together as ‘in the first and second.’ The self (attā), personal existence (attabhāvo), is that wherein the conceit ‘I’ is placed. ‘Internal’ (ajjhattaṃ) means with reference to the self; because they are included therein, things that exist there are internal; of those internal things. And the internal is fourfold: internal as internal, one's own internal, internal as resort, and internal as object. Therein, concerning ‘internal as internal,’ he says ‘of the internal by way of internal as internal’ because things that are within are internal. The internality of the eye and so on, even among internal things, is due to their being the basis for excessive affection; thus he says, ‘because of the exceeding strength of sensual desire.’ Now, showing that meaning along with its counterpart by way of an example, he says, ‘for humans…,’ and so on. That is plain. ‘External’ means things that are outside the internal.

Paṭhamapubbesambodhasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the First Discourse Before Enlightenment and Others is finished.

5-6. Paṭhamanoceassādasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the First Discourse on Gratification and Others

17-18. Nikkhantāti lokato nikkhantā. Visaṃyuttā saṃyogahetūnaṃ kilesānaṃ pahīnattā no saṃyuttā. No adhimuttāti na ussukkajātā. Vimariyādī…pe… cetasāti vigatakilesavaṭṭamariyādatāya nimmariyādīkatena cittena. Catusaccameva kathitaṃ cakkhādīnaṃ assādādino kathitattā.

17-18. ‘Departed’: departed from the world. ‘Disjoined’: not joined, because the defilements that are the cause of fetters have been abandoned. ‘Not intent’: not having become eager. ‘With a mind that has transcended limits…(pe)…’: with a mind made limitless by the removal of the limit that is the round of defilements. The Four Truths alone have been spoken of, since gratification and so on concerning the eye and so on have been spoken of.

Paṭhamanoceassādasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the First Discourse on Gratification and Others is finished.

7-10. Paṭhamābhinandasuttādivaṇṇanā

7-10. Commentary on the First Discourse on Rejoicing and Others

19-22. Vaṭṭavivaṭṭameva kathitaṃ abhinandanānaṃ uppādanirodhānañca vasena desanāya pavattattā. Anupubbakathāti ādito paṭṭhāya padatthavaṇṇanā. Nesanti suttānaṃ.

19-22. The round and the ending of the round alone are spoken of, because the teaching proceeds by way of the arising and cessation of rejoicings. ‘A gradual exposition’ means the explanation of the meaning of the words from the beginning. ‘Of these’ means of the discourses.

Paṭhamābhinandasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the First Discourse on Rejoicing and Others is finished.

Yamakavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Chapter on Pairs is finished.

3. Sabbavaggo

3. The Chapter on the All

1. Sabbasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on the All

23. Sabba-saddo [M 2.0285] pakaraṇavasena katthaci sappadesepi pavattatīti tato nivattanatthaṃ anavasesavisayena sabba-saddena visesetvā vuttaṃ “sabbasabba”nti, sabbameva hutvā sabbanti attho. Āyatanabhāvaṃ sabbaṃ āyatanasabbaṃ, sesadvayepi eseva nayo.

23. Since the word ‘all’ sometimes occurs even with a partial meaning depending on the context, in order to exclude that, it is specified by the word ‘all’ in the sense of ‘without remainder’ and is stated as ‘the all-all.’ The meaning is: being entirely all, it is all. The all that is the state of the sense bases is ‘the all of the sense bases’; this same method applies to the remaining two as well.

Tassa [V 2.0005] avisayābhāvato na addiṭṭhamidhatthi kiñcīti. Idhāti nipātamattaṃ, idha vā sadevake loke, dassanabhūtena ñāṇena adiṭṭhaṃ nāma kiñci natthīti attho. Yadi evaṃ anumānavisayaṃ nu kho kathanti āha “atho aviññāta”nti. Aññesaṃ apaccakkhampi aviññātaṃ tassa kiñci natthīti adiṭṭhaṃ aviññātaṃ natthi. Paccuppannaṃ atītameva ñeyyaṃ gahitaṃ, anāgataṃ nu kho kathanti āha – “ajānitabba”nti, tassa kiñci natthīti ānetvā sambandho. Jānituṃ ñātuṃ asakkuṇeyyaṃ nāma tassa kiñci natthīti dassento āha “sabbaṃ abhiññāsī”tiādi.

Because for him there is nothing that is not its object, it is said: ‘there is nothing here unseen.’ ‘Here’ is just a particle; or it means ‘here in this world with its devas.’ The meaning is that there is nothing at all that has not been seen by the knowledge that has become vision. If so, what about the object of inference? Thus he says, ‘and what is uncognized.’ For him there is nothing uncognized, even what is not directly perceptible to others; thus there is nothing unseen or uncognized. The knowable present and past have been included. But what about the future? Thus he says, ‘or unknowable.’ The connection is made by supplying: ‘for him there is nothing.’ Showing that for him there is nothing at all that cannot be known or understood, he says, ‘He has directly known all,’ and so on.

Sakalassa sakkāyadhammassa pariggahitattā sakkāyasabbaṃ. Sabbadhammesūti pañcannaṃ dvārānaṃ ārammaṇabhūtesu sabbesu dhammesu. Yasmā chasupi ārammaṇesu gahitesu padesasabbaṃ nāma na hoti, tasmā “pañcārammaṇamatta”nti vuttaṃ. Padesasabbaṃ sakkāyasabbaṃ na pāpuṇāti tassa tebhūmakadhammesu ekadesassa asaṅgaṇhanato. Sakkāyasabbaṃ āyatanasabbaṃ na pāpuṇāti lokuttaradhammānaṃ asaṅgaṇhanato. Āyatanasabbaṃ sabbasabbaṃ na pāpuṇāti. Yasmā āyatanasabbena catubhūmakadhammāva pariggahitā, na lakkhaṇapaññattiyo, yasmā sabbasabbaṃ dassentena buddhañāṇavisayo dassito, tasmā “sabbasabbaṃ na pāpuṇātī”ti etthāpi “kasmā…pe… natthitāyā”ti sabbaṃ ñātārammaṇeneva pucchāvissajjanaṃ kataṃ. “Āyatanasabbepi idha vipassanupagadhammāva gahetabbā abhiññeyyaniddesavasenapi sammasanacārasseva icchitattā”ti vadanti.

It is ‘the all of personal existence’ (sakkāyasabbaṃ) because the entire phenomenon of personal existence is included. ‘In all phenomena’ means in all phenomena that have become the objects of the five doors. Since, when the six objects are included, there is no so-called ‘partial all,’ it is therefore said, ‘only the five objects.’ The ‘partial all’ does not extend to the ‘all of personal existence’ because it does not include a portion of the phenomena of the three planes. The ‘all of personal existence’ does not extend to the ‘all of the sense bases’ because it does not include the supramundane phenomena. The ‘all of the sense bases’ does not extend to the ‘all-all.’ Since by the ‘all of the sense bases’ only the phenomena of the four planes are included, not concepts and designations, and since by showing the ‘all-all’ the range of the Buddha’s knowledge is shown, therefore concerning the statement ‘does not extend to the all-all,’ here too the question and answer about ‘why…(pe)…because of its non-existence’ is made only with reference to the knowable object. Some say: ‘Here, in the ‘all of the sense bases’ too, only phenomena that are amenable to insight should be taken, because the practice of contemplation is intended, as shown by the exposition of what is to be directly known.’

Paṭikkhipitvāti “idaṃ sabbaṃ nāma na hotī”ti evaṃ paṭikkhipitvā. Tassāti “aññaṃ sabbaṃ paññāpessāmī”ti vadantassa. Vācāya vattabbavatthumattakamevāti vañjhāputtagaganakusumādivācā viya etassa vācāya kevalaṃ vattabbavatthukameva bhaveyya, na attho, vacanamattakamevāti attho[M 2.0286]. Atikkamitvāti anāmasitvā aggahetvā. Taṃ kissa hetūti vighātāpajjanaṃ kena hetunā. Yathā taṃ avisayasminti yathā aññopi koci avisaye vāyamanto, evanti attho. Aṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana yasmā pāḷiyaṃ “taṃ kissa hetū”ti vuttakāraṇameva upanayanavasena dassetuṃ “yathā ta”ntiādi vuttaṃ. Kāraṇopanayanañca kāraṇamevāti “yathāti kāraṇavacana”nti vuttanti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Tenevāha “evaṃ imasmimpi avisaye”tiādi.

‘Having rejected’: having rejected it thus: ‘This is not the all.’ ‘Of him’: of one who says, ‘I will make known another all.’ ‘It would be a mere matter for speech’: just as with speech about a barren woman’s son, a sky-flower, and so on, for this person’s speech there would be only a matter to be spoken of, not a reality; the meaning is, it would be a mere statement. ‘Having gone beyond’: not having touched, not having grasped. ‘For what reason is that?’: for what reason would he reap vexation? ‘Just as it is not his domain’: the meaning is, just as anyone else striving in what is not his domain. In the commentary, however, since in the Pāli text the reason has already been stated with ‘For what reason is that?,’ the words ‘just as it is,’ and so on, are stated to show this by way of an illustration. And since the illustration of a reason is itself a reason, it should be understood that ‘just as’ is said to be a word indicating a reason. Therefore he says, ‘So too in this case, it not being his domain,’ and so on.

Sabbasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Discourse on the All is finished.

2. Pahānasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Pahāna Sutta

24. Sabbassāti dvārārammaṇehi saddhiṃ dvārappavattassa. Pahānāyāti tappaṭibaddhachandarāgapahānavasena [V 2.0006] pajahanāya. Cakkhusamphassanti cakkhusannissitaphassaṃ. Mūlapaccayanti mūlabhūtaṃ paccayaṃ katvā, sahajātavedanāya cakkhusamphassapaccayabhāve vattabbameva natthi. Eseva nayoti apadesena “sotasamphassaṃ mūlapaccayaṃ katvā”tiādinā vattabbanti dasseti. Manoti bhavaṅgacittaṃ manodvārassa adhippetattā. Ārammaṇanti dhammārammaṇaṃ. Sahāvajjanakajavananti sahamanodvārāvajjanakaṃ javanaṃ. Taṃpubbakattā manoviññāṇaphassavedanānaṃ mūlapaccayabhūtā sabbesveva cakkhudvārādīsu vuttittā tadanurūpato “bhavaṅgasahajāto samphasso”ti vuttaṃ. Sahāvajjanavedanāya javanavedanā “vedayita”nti adhippetā, bhavaṅgasampayuttāya pana vedanāya gahaṇe vattabbameva natthi. Bhavaṅgato amocetvā bhavaṅgacittena saddhiṃyeva āvajjanaṃ gahetvā manodvārāvajjanaṃ bhavaṅgaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Yā panettha desanāti yā ettha “pahānāyā”tiādinā pavattadesanā satthu anasiṭṭhi āṇā. Ayaṃ paṇṇatti nāma tassa tassa atthassa pakārato ñāpanato. Ettha sabbaggahaṇena sabbe sabhāvadhammā gahitā, paññatti pana katamāti vicāraṇāya taṃ dassetuṃ “yā panetthā”tiādi vuttanti daṭṭhabbaṃ.

24. ‘Of all’ means of what has occurred at the sense door, together with the sense doors and sense objects. ‘For abandoning’ means for abandoning by way of abandoning the desire and lust connected with that. ‘Eye-contact’ means contact based on the eye. ‘Root condition’ means having made it a root condition; as for co-nascent feeling, there is nothing to be said about eye-contact being its condition. ‘This is the method’ shows that it should be stated by way of example, ‘having made ear-contact the root condition,’ and so on. ‘Mind’ means the life-continuum consciousness, because the mind-door is intended. ‘Object’ means the mental object. ‘Together with adverting and impulsion’ means impulsion together with mind-door adverting. Because it precedes them, it is the root condition for mind-consciousness, contact, and feeling. Since this is stated for all the sense doors beginning with the eye door, it is said accordingly: ‘contact co-nascent with the life-continuum.’ The feeling of impulsion along with the feeling of adverting is intended by ‘felt.’ But regarding the taking of feeling associated with the life-continuum, there is nothing to be said. Without being released from the life-continuum, having taken adverting together with the life-continuum consciousness, mind-door adverting should be seen as the life-continuum. ‘The instruction here’ means the teaching set forth here beginning with ‘for abandoning’ is the Teacher’s command, not an admonition. This is called a designation because it makes known the meaning of this and that by way of its mode. Here, by the taking of ‘all,’ all phenomena with intrinsic nature are included. But to show, in the investigation ‘what, then, is the designation?,’ it is said, ‘but here,’ etc.—this should be understood.

Pahānasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Pahāna Sutta is finished.

3. Abhiññāpariññāpahānasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Abhiññāpariññāpahāna Sutta

25. Abhiññāti [M 2.0287] abhiññāya. Ya-kāralopavasenāyaṃ niddeso “sayaṃ abhiññā”tiādīsu (dī. ni. 1.28, 405; ma. ni. 1.154) viya, tathā “pariññā”ti etthāpi. Sabbanti āyatanasabbaṃ. Tañhi abhiññeyyaṃ. Abhijānitvāti abhiññāya jānitvā. Parijānitvāti tīraṇapariññāya aniccādito parijānitvā. Pajahanatthāyāti pahānapariññāya anavasesato pajahanāya.

25. ‘Abhiññā’ means ‘for direct knowledge’ (abhiññāya). This exposition is by way of the elision of the letter ‘y,’ as in such cases as ‘sayaṃ abhiññā’ (having directly known for himself) (DN 1:28, 405; MN 1:154); similarly in the case of ‘pariññā,’ which stands for ‘pariññāya’ (for full understanding). ‘All’ means the all of the sense bases, for that is to be directly known. ‘Having directly known’ (Abhijānitvā) means having known by direct knowledge. ‘Having fully understood’ (Parijānitvā) means having fully understood by means of the full understanding that is discernment, starting with impermanence. ‘For the purpose of abandoning’ (Pajahanatthāya) means for abandoning without remainder by means of the full understanding that is abandoning.

Abhiññāpariññāpahānasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Abhiññāpariññāpahāna Sutta is finished.

4. Paṭhamaaparijānanasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the First Sutta on Not Fully Understanding

26. “Abhijāna”ntiādinā ettha bhagavā paṭhamaṃ kaṇhapakkhaṃ dassetvā sukkapakkhaṃ dasseti veneyyajjhāsayavasena. Tenettha vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ.

26. By ‘Not directly knowing,’ etc., here the Blessed One first shows the dark side and then the bright side in accordance with the disposition of those to be trained. Thus the round of existence and the rolling back of the round are explained here.

Paṭhamaaparijānanasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Sutta on Not Fully Understanding is finished.

5. Dutiyaaparijānanasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Second Sutta on Not Fully Understanding

27. Cakkhuviññāṇaviññātabbadhammo [V 2.0007] nāma rūpāyatanamevāti āha “heṭṭhā gahitarūpamevā”ti. Idha anāpāthagataṃ “cakkhuviññāṇaviññātabbā dhammā”ti vuttattā. Heṭṭhā āpāthagatampi anāpāthagatampi gahitameva “ye ca rūpā”ti anavasesato vuttattā. Te hi vedanāsaññāsaṅkhārakkhandhā saha cakkhuviññāṇena viññātabbattā. Tathā hi cakkhuviññāṇaṃ tehi ekuppādaṃ ekavatthukaṃ ekanirodhaṃ ekārammaṇameva. Sesapadesūti sesesu “yañca sotaṃ ye ca saddā”tiādinā āgatesu kaṇhapakkhe pañcasu, sukkapakkhe chasupi padesu. Eseva nayoti yvāyaṃ “heṭṭhā gahitarūpameva gaṇhitvā”ti attho vutto. Eso eva tatthapi atthavaṇṇanānayo.

27. The thing called ‘a phenomenon cognizable by eye-consciousness’ is just the form base; thus it is said, ‘only the form taken below.’ Here, because it is said ‘phenomena cognizable by eye-consciousness,’ what has not come into range is included. Below, both what has come into range and what has not come into range are taken, because it is said without remainder, ‘and whatever forms.’ For those aggregates—feeling, perception, and volitional formations—are cognizable together with eye-consciousness. For indeed, eye-consciousness has with them a single arising, a single base, a single cessation, and a single object. ‘In the remaining passages’ means in the remaining passages that occur, beginning with ‘the ear and sounds,’ in the five of the dark side and in the six of the bright side. This is the method: this is the meaning that is stated, ‘having taken only the form taken below.’ This very same method of explaining the meaning applies there as well.

Dutiyaaparijānanasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Second Sutta on Not Fully Understanding is finished.

6. Ādittasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Fire Discourse

28. Gayānāmikāya [M 2.0288] nadiyā avidūre pavatto gāmo gayā nāma, tassaṃ gayāyaṃ viharatīti samīpatthe cetaṃ bhummavacanaṃ. Gayāgāmassa hi āsanne gayāsīsanāmake piṭṭhipāsāṇe bhagavā tadā vihāsi. Tenāha “bhagavā tattha viharatī”ti.

28. A village named Gayā is situated not far from the river named Gayā. The expression ‘dwelling at Gayā’ is a locative used in the sense of proximity. For at that time the Blessed One was dwelling near the village of Gayā on a flat rock named Gayāsīsa. Therefore it is said: ‘the Blessed One was dwelling there.’

Tatrāti “bhikkhū āmantesī”ti ye bhikkhū āmantesi, yathā cāyaṃ desanā tesaṃ sappāyā jātā, tatra tasmiṃ atthadvaye vibhāvetabbe ayaṃ anupubbikathā samudāgamato paṭṭhāya anupaṭipāṭikathā. Itoti imasmā kappato. Kirāti anussavanatthe nipāto. Pāramitāparibhāvanāya paripākagate. Ñāṇeti bodhiñāṇe. Kaniṭṭhaputto vemātikabhātā bhagavato. Veḷubhittikuṭikāhi parikkhipitvā bahiddhā, anto pana paṭasāṇīhi.

‘There’: this refers to the bhikkhus he addressed when he said, ‘he addressed the bhikkhus,’ and to how this teaching was suitable for them. When this twofold meaning is to be explained, this is the gradual discourse, the sequential discourse from the very beginning. ‘From here’ means from this aeon. `Kirā` is a particle in the sense of hearsay. `When it had reached maturity`: through the cultivation of the perfections. `In the knowledge`: in the knowledge of awakening. The youngest son was the Blessed One’s stepbrother. Having enclosed it on the outside with huts with bamboo walls, and on the inside with cloth curtains.

Sabbesaṃ sattānaṃ. Puññacetanaṃ anto abbhantare paveseti. Bhagavāpi tassa puttoti katvā “aññe tayo puttā”ti vuttaṃ. Avippakiritvāti parājayena avippakiriya apalāyitvā. Pidahīti dātuṃ na sakkomīti tathā akāsi. Saccavāditāya gaṇhiṃsūti rājakulassa saccavāditāya attano varaṃ gaṇhiṃsu.

‘Of all beings.’ ‘He causes the meritorious volition to enter inside.’ Because the Blessed One, too, was considered his son, it is said, ‘the other three sons.’ ‘Without being scattered’ means not having been scattered by defeat, not having fled. ‘He covered it up’: he acted thus because he thought, ‘I am not able to give.’ ‘They took because of his truthfulness’: because of the truthfulness of the royal family, they took their boon.

Vinivattitunti paṭiññāya nivattituṃ. Antarāti tumhehi paricchinnakālassa antarā eva matā. Aṭṭhavīsatihatthaṭṭhānaṃ usabhaṃ nāma. Usabhe aṭṭhavīsatihatthappamāṇe ṭhāne. Dānagge byāvaṭoti pasuto.

‘To turn back’ means to go back on a promise. ‘In between’ means they died within the time determined by you. A place of twenty-eight cubits is called an `usabha`. `In an usabha`: in a place measuring twenty-eight cubits. ‘Engaged in giving’ means diligent.

Soti [V 2.0008] bhagavā. Tathārūpañhi buddhānaṃ desanāpāṭihāriyaṃ, yathā desanāya gahito attho paccakkhato vibhūto hutvā upaṭṭhāti. Tenāha “imesaṃ…pe… desessāmī”ti. Sanniṭṭhānanti cirakālaparicitādittaaggikānaṃ ādittapariyāyadesanāva sappāyāti nicchayamakāsi. Padittanti padīpitaṃ ekādasahi aggīhi ekajālībhūtaṃ. Tenāha “sampajjalita”nti. Dukkhalakkhaṇaṃ kathitaṃ cakkhādīnaṃ ekādasahi aggīhi ādittabhāvena dukkhamatāya dukkhassa kathitattā.

‘He’: the Blessed One. For such is the miracle of teaching of the Buddhas that the meaning grasped through the teaching, having become directly evident, presents itself. Therefore he said: ‘To these ... I will teach.’ ‘Conclusion’: he made the decision that the teaching of the Discourse on Burning is suitable for those long familiar with blazing fires. ‘Ablaze’: lit, made into a single flame by the eleven fires. Therefore he said, ‘fully ablaze.’ The characteristic of suffering is stated, because suffering is stated through the fact that the eye, etc., by being ablaze with the eleven fires, have the nature of suffering.

Ādittasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Āditta Sutta is finished.

7. Addhabhūtasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Addhabhūta Sutta

29. Adhisaddena [M 2.0289] samānattho addhasaddoti āha “addhabhūtanti adhibhūta”ntiādi. Sesaṃ vuttanayameva.

29. The word `addha` has the same meaning as the word `adhi`; thus it is said: '`addhabhūta` means `adhibhūta`,' and so on. The rest is by the method already stated.

Addhabhūtasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Addhabhūta Sutta is finished.

8. Samugghātasāruppasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Samugghātasāruppa Sutta

30. Maññitaṃ nāma taṇhāmānadiṭṭhīhi gahetabbaṃ maññitaṃ. Sabbasmiṃ maññitanti sabbamaññitaṃ, tassa samugghāto setughāto, tadāvahaṃ sabbamaññitasamugghātasāruppaṃ. Aṭṭhakathāyaṃ pana “maññitaṃ nāma cakkhādīsu eva uppajjati, nāññasmi”nti dutiyanayo vutto. Anucchavikanti anurūpaṃ avilomaṃ. Idhāti idheva sāsane aññattha tadabhāvato. “Taṇhā-mānadiṭṭhimaññitāna”nti vuttaṃ maññitattayaṃ saparasantānesu paṭipakkhavasena yojetvā dassetuṃ “cakkhuṃ ahanti vā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha “cakkhuṃ aha”nti iminā ajjhattavisayaṃ diṭṭhimaññitañca dasseti attābhinivesāhaṃkāradīpanato. “Mama”nti iminā taṇhāmaññitaṃ mānamaññitampi vā, pariggahamukhenapi seyyādito mānuppajjanato. Sesapadadvayepi iminā nayena maññitavibhāgo veditabbo. Ahanti attāva, so ca cakkhusmiṃ tadadhīnavuttittā “paro”ti na maññati. Mama kiñcanapalibodho cakkhusmiṃ sati labbhanato, asati na maññati tathāmaññitassa paccayaghātato. Sesapadadvayepi eseva nayo.

30. Conceiving (`maññitaṃ`) is that which is to be grasped by craving, conceit, and views. `Sabbamaññitaṃ` means conceiving in all. Its uprooting is the breaking of the bridge. That which brings this about is what is suitable for the uprooting of all conceiving. In the commentary, however, a second method is stated: 'Conceiving arises only in regard to the eye and so on, not in regard to anything else.' `Anucchavikaṃ` means suitable, not contrary. `Idha` means right here in this dispensation, because it is absent elsewhere. In order to show the three kinds of conceiving that have been mentioned—craving-conceiving, conceit-conceiving, and view-conceiving—by connecting them with one's own and others' continuities of existence by way of opposition, it is said, 'the eye is I,' and so on. Therein, by 'the eye is I,' it shows view-conceiving regarding an internal object, because it indicates adherence to self and egoism. By 'mine,' it shows craving-conceiving or also conceit-conceiving, since conceit can also arise by way of possession, from 'better,' and so on. The division of conceivings in the remaining two phrases should be understood in this way. 'I' is the self. Since its existence is dependent on the eye, one does not conceive it as 'other.' Because the encumbrance of 'mine' is obtained when the eye exists, one does not conceive when it is absent, due to the destruction of the condition for such conceiving. This is the same method for the remaining two phrases.

Ahaṃ cakkhuto niggatoti “aha”nti vattabbo ayaṃ satto cakkhuto niggato tattha sukhumākārena upalabbhanato. Mama kiñcanapalibodho cakkhuto niggato tasmiṃ sati eva upalabbhanato[V 2.0009]. Sesadvayepi eseva nayo. Tattha paroti paro satto. Mama cakkhūti na maññati yassa taṃ cakkhu, tassa “aha”nti vattabbasseva abhāvato. Mamattabhūtanti mama kāraṇaṃ. Sesaṃ uttānameva. Evametasmiṃ sutte cakkhurūpa-cakkhuviññāṇa-cakkhusamphassa-sukhadukkhādukkhamasukhavasena satta vārā cakkhudvāre, tathā sotadvārādīsūti cha sattakā dvecattālīsa. Puna sakkāyavasena “sabbaṃ na maññatī”tiādinā vuttaṃ, tena tecattālīsa. Puna tebhūmakavaṭṭaṃ “loko”ti gahetvā “na kiñci loke upādiyatī”ti [M 2.0290] vuttaṃ, tena catucattālīsa honti. Evaṃ sabbathāpi catucattālīsāya ṭhānesu arahattaṃ pāpetvā vipassanā kathitāti veditabbā. “Catucattālīsādhikasatesū”ti kesuci potthakesu likhanti, sā ca pamādalekhā.

'I apart from the eye': 'I' is said because this being is found in a subtle form apart from the eye. 'The encumbrance of mine apart from the eye': this is found only when that (the eye) exists. This is the same method for the remaining two. Therein, 'other' means another being. One does not conceive 'my eye' of that which is another's eye, because for that one there is no basis for saying 'I.' `Mamattabhūtaṃ` means 'on account of me.' The rest is clear. Thus in this sutta, by way of eye, form, eye-consciousness, eye-contact, and pleasure, pain, and neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling, there are seven cases at the eye door. Similarly for the ear door and so on, there are six sets of seven, making forty-two. Again, by way of personality view, it is said, 'he conceives nothing,' etc., which makes forty-three. Again, taking the round of the three planes of existence as 'the world,' it is said, 'he does not cling to anything in the world,' which makes forty-four. Thus it should be understood that insight is taught as leading to arahantship in all forty-four instances. In some books they write 'one hundred and forty-four,' but that is a scribal error.

Samugghātasāruppasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Samugghātasāruppa Sutta is finished.

9. Paṭhamasamugghātasappāyasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the First Sutta on What is Conducive to Uprooting

31. Upakārabhūtā tadāvahattā. Tatoti maññitākārato. Tanti maññanāvatthuṃ. Aññenākārenāti yathā maññati aniccādiākārato, aññena aniccādinā ākārena hoti. Aññathābhāvaṃ vipariṇāmanti uppādavayatāya aññathābhāvaṃ jarāya maraṇena ca dvedhā vipariṇāmetabbaṃ. Taṃ upagamanena aññathābhāvī, evaṃbhūto hutvāpi jīraṇabhijjanasabhāvesu. Bhavesu satto loko uparipi bhavaṃyeva abhinandati. Heṭṭhā gahitameva saṅkaḍḍhitvāti “cakkhuṃ na maññatī”tiādinā heṭṭhā gahitameva khandhadhātuāyatanāti khandhādipariyāyena ekato gahetvā punapi maññanāvatthuṃ dasseti. Avasāne “tato taṃ hotī”ti vuttapadena saddhiṃ sabbavāresu aṭṭha aṭṭha hontīti “aṭṭhacattālīsāya ṭhānesū”ti vuttaṃ.

31. It is helpful because it brings that about. `Tato` means: from the mode of conceiving. `Taṃ` means: the object of conceiving. `Aññena ākārena` means: it becomes with a mode—that of impermanence and so on—different from the way one conceives it. `Aññathābhāvaṃ vipariṇāmaṃ` means: alteration into another state; it is to be altered in two ways: by arising and passing away, and by aging and death. By approaching that, it becomes altered; yet even having become so, being in a state of decay and dissolution, the world, attached to states of existence, still delights in existence above. Having drawn down what was grasped below: This refers to what was taken up below with the words 'he does not conceive the eye,' etc. Having gathered this together by way of the aggregates, elements, and sense bases, it again shows the object of conceiving. At the end, together with the phrase 'from that, it becomes otherwise,' there are eight items in all cases, thus it is said 'in forty-eight instances'.

Paṭhamasamugghātasappāyasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Sutta on What is Conducive to Uprooting is finished.

10. Dutiyasamugghātasappāyasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Second Sutta on What is Conducive to Uprooting

32. Dassetvāti ettha lakkhaṇe ayaṃ tvā-saddo, hetumhi vā. Anādisattasantānagatagāhattayalakkhitā hi satthu, tiparivaṭṭadesanā taṃnimittaṃ yāvadeva tappahānāya pavattitabhāvato. Arahattaṃ pāpetvā vipassanā kathitāti aṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttaṃ “saha vipassanāya cattāropi maggā kathitā”ti.

32. Regarding `dassetvā`: here the suffix `-tvā` is in the sense of a characteristic, or in the sense of a cause. For the Teacher's teaching of the three rounds, which is marked by the three kinds of grasping found in the beginningless continuity of beings, proceeds for that reason, solely for the sake of abandoning them. The statement in the commentary, 'insight was taught as leading to arahantship,' means that 'along with insight, all four paths were taught.'

Dutiyasamugghātasappāyasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Second Sutta on What is Conducive to Uprooting is finished.

Sabbavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on All is finished.

4. Jātidhammavaggavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Chapter on Things Subject to Birth

33-42. Nibbattanasabhāvanti [V 2.0010][M 2.0291] hetupaccayehi uppajjanasabhāvaṃ. Uppādānantaraṃ buddhippattiyā jīraṇasabhāvaṃ. Yattha cakkhādayo, tattheva visabhāgasamuṭṭhānalakkhaṇena byādhino uppattipaccayabhāvena byādhisabhāvaṃ. Maraṇasabhāvanti vināsasabhāvaṃ. Sokasabhāvanti ñātibyasanādinā ḍayhamānadukkhasabhāvaṃ. Saṃkilesikasabhāvanti taṇhādivasena saṃkilissanasabhāvaṃ.

33-42. The nature of arising means the nature of originating from causes and conditions. After arising, upon reaching maturity, there is the nature of aging. Where there are the eye and so on, right there is the nature of illness, which has the characteristic of the origination of disharmonious elements and is a condition for the arising of disease. The nature of death means the nature of destruction. The nature of sorrow means the nature of suffering, being burned by such things as the misfortune of relatives. The nature of being subject to defilement means the nature of being defiled by way of craving and so on.

Jātidhammavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on Things Subject to Birth is concluded.

5. Sabbaaniccavaggavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Chapter on All as Impermanent

43-52. Ñātapariññā āgatā visayavasena tabbisayassa dhammassa jotitattā. Itarā dve tīraṇapahānapariññāpi āgatā evāti veditabbā, tāsaṃ abhiññeyyadhammavisayattā ñāṇassa ca tīraṇapahānapariññāsambhavato. Pariññeyyapade tīraṇapariññāva āgatā, pahātabbapade pahānapariññāva āgatāti yojanā. Itarāpi dve gahitāyeva tāhi vinā atthasiddhiyā abhāvato. Paccakkhaṃ kātabbaṃ ārammaṇato asammohato paṭivijjhanena. Avuttāpi gahitāyeva parijānanassa yāvadeva pahānatthattā. Ekasabhāvena vinābhāvo anekaggaṭṭho. Upasaṭṭharogena viya anto eva abhihatasabbatā upahataṭṭho.

43-52. Full understanding as the known is included by way of the domain, because the phenomenon that is its object has been illuminated. It should be understood that the other two—full understanding by investigation and full understanding by abandoning—are also included, because their domain is phenomena to be directly known and because knowledge can arise from full understanding by investigation and by abandoning. The connection is that in the term 'to be fully understood,' it is full understanding by investigation that is included; in the term 'to be abandoned,' it is full understanding by abandoning that is included. The other two are also implied, for without them the meaning is not established. It is to be directly realized by penetrating the object without delusion. Though unstated, they are implied, since understanding is for the very purpose of abandoning. Being devoid of a single nature is the meaning of 'manifold.' Like a disease that has attacked, the meaning of 'afflicted' is being struck down right within.

Sabbaaniccavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on All as Impermanent is concluded.

Paṭhamo paṇṇāsako.

The First Fifty.

6. Avijjāvaggavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Chapter on Ignorance

53-62. Catūsu saccesu aññāṇaṃ tappaṭicchādakasammoho. Avindiyaṃ vindati, vindiyaṃ na vindatīti katvā vijjāya paṭipakkhova avijjā. Vijjāya uppannāya anavasesato avijjā pahīyati, taṃ dassento “vijjāti arahattamaggavijjā”ti [M 2.0292] āha. Na kevalaṃ [V 2.0011] aniccānupassanāvaseneva maggavuṭṭhānaṃ, atha kho itarānupassanāvasenapīti dassento “dukkhā…pe… pahīyatiyevā”ti āha. Sabbatthāti uparisuttante sandhāyāha. Tato aparepi taṃatthalakkhaṇavasena kathitasuttantepi. Tānipi hi tathā bujjhanakapuggalānamajjhāsayena vuttānīti.

53-62. Ignorance is not knowing the four truths, the delusion that conceals them. Because it finds what is not to be found and does not find what is to be found, ignorance is the very opposite of knowledge. When knowledge has arisen, ignorance is abandoned without remainder. To show this, he said: 'Knowledge means the knowledge of the path to arahantship.' To show that emergence from the path does not occur only by way of contemplation of impermanence but also by way of other contemplations, he said: '...of suffering...pe... it is indeed abandoned.' He said 'in all' with reference to the subsequent suttas, and also to other suttas spoken after that in terms of the characteristic of that meaning. For those too were spoken in accordance with the disposition of the persons who were to awaken thus.

Avijjāvaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on Ignorance is concluded.

7. Migajālavaggo

7. The Migajāla Chapter

1. Paṭhamamigajālasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the First Migajāla Sutta

63. Cakkhuviññāṇaṃ dassanakiccanti vuttacakkhuviññāṇena passitabbanti āha “iṭṭhārammaṇabhūtā”ti. Kamanīyāti kāmetabbā. Manaṃ appāyantīti manāpāti āha “manavaḍḍhanakā”ti. Piyāyitabbasabhāvā piyarūpā. Kāmūpasaṃhitāti kāmapaṭisaṃyuttā. Ālambitabbatā eva cettha upasaṃhitatāti “ārammaṇaṃ katvā”tiādimāha. Taṇhāsaṅkhātā nandī taṇhānandī, na tuṭṭhinandīyeva “nandiṃ caratī”tiādīsu viya. Gāmantanti gāmasamīpaṃ. “Anupacāraṭṭhāna”nti vatvā taṃ dasseti “yatthā”tiādinā.

63. Because eye-consciousness has the function of seeing, he said 'pleasing objects,' meaning 'what is to be seen by the aforesaid eye-consciousness.' 'Desirable' means to be desired. 'Agreeable' is so called because they delight the mind; thus he said 'mind-enhancing.' 'Lovely forms' have a nature to be cherished. 'Connected with sensual pleasure' means associated with sensual desire. Here, 'connected' just means 'to be taken as an object'; thus he said 'having made it an object,' and so on. Delight reckoned as craving is 'delight in craving,' not just the delight of satisfaction as in such passages as 'he dwells in delight.' 'Village-end' means near a village. Having said 'a place not frequented,' he illustrates this with 'where,' and so on.

Etthāti yathānīhate pāṭhe. “Imaṃ ekaṃ pariyāyaṃ ṭhapetvā”ti vuttaṃ tassa “pantānī”tipadena saṅgahitattā. Appasaddāni appanigghosānīti ettha appa-saddo abhāvattho.

'Here' means in the text as it has been handed down. 'Setting aside this one passage' is said because that is included by the word 'remote.' In 'of few sounds, of little noise,' the word 'few' has the meaning of absence.

Paṭhamamigajālasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Migajāla Sutta is concluded.

6. Samiddhilokapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Sutta of Samiddhi's Questions about the World

68. Āyācanasuttato paṭṭhāyāti migajālavagge dutiyasuttato paṭṭhāya. Paṭhamasutte pana dutiyakavihārādibhāvo vuttoti.

68. 'Starting from the Āyācanasutta' means starting from the second sutta in the Migajāla Chapter. But in the first sutta, the state of the second dwelling and so on has been stated.

Samiddhilokapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Sutta of Samiddhi's Questions about the World is concluded.

7. Upasenaāsīvisasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Sutta on Upasena and the Serpent

69. Gahetvāti [V 2.0012][M 2.0293] pārupanassa sithilakaraṇena vīmaṃsato mocetvā. Leṇacchāyāyāti purimadisāya leṇacchāyāyaṃ. Patitvāti leṇacchadanato bhassitvā. Phuṭṭhavisoti catūsu āsīvisesu so phuṭṭhaviso. Tenāha “tasmā”tiādi. Pariyādiyamānamevāti khepentameva, vināsentamevāti attho. Yathāparicchedenāti attano visaparicchedānurūpaṃ. Aññathābhāvanti vaḍḍhabhāvādipakatijahanaṃ. Sabhāvavigamoti vināso.

69. 'Having taken' means having freed it after examining it by loosening the garment. 'In the shade of the cave' means in the shade of the cave on the eastern side. 'Having fallen' means having slipped from the roof of the cave. 'A touch-poison snake' means that among the four kinds of venomous snakes, this one is of the touch-poison type. Therefore he said 'therefore,' and so on. 'Being consumed' just means being expended, being destroyed; that is the meaning. 'According to its limit' means in accordance with the limit of its own poison. 'Alteration' means the abandoning of its natural state of growth and so on. 'Disappearance of its nature' means destruction.

Upasenaāsīvisasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Sutta on Upasena and the Serpent is concluded.

8. Upavāṇasandiṭṭhikasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Sutta to Upavāṇa on the Immediately Apparent

70. Rūpaṃ paṭisaṃviditaṃ karoti ñātapariññāvasena. Rūparāganti nīlādibhede rūpadhamme rāgaṃ. Paṭisaṃviditaṃ karoti “ayaṃ me rāgo appahīno”ti. Etena sekkhānaṃ paccavekkhaṇā kathitā. Tena vuttaṃ “evampi kho, upavāṇa, sandiṭṭhiko dhammo hotī”tiādi. Rūparāgaṃ paṭisaṃviditaṃ karoti “natthi me ajjhattaṃ rūpesu rāgo”ti pajānāti. Asekkhānaṃ hāyaṃ paccavekkhaṇā.

70. 'He fully knows form' means by way of full understanding as the known. 'Lust for form' means lust for the phenomena of form in their varieties such as blue, etc. He fully knows it thus: 'This lust of mine is unabandoned.' By this, the reviewing of the trainees is described. Therefore it was said: 'In this way, Upavāṇa, the Dhamma is immediately apparent,' and so on. He fully knows lust for form, understanding: 'There is no lust for forms internally in me.' For this is the reviewing of the non-trainees.

Upavāṇasandiṭṭhikasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Sutta to Upavāṇa on the Immediately Apparent is concluded.

9. Paṭhamachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the First Discourse on the Six Bases for Contact

71. Phassākarānanti channaṃ phassānaṃ ākarānaṃ uppattiṭṭhānānaṃ, cakkhādīnanti attho. Naṭṭho nāma ahanti vadati, yo channaṃ phassāyatanānaṃ samudayādiṃ yathābhūtaṃ pajānāti, so vusitavā, itaro avusitavā ahañca tādisoti. Ayamevāti ayaṃ cakkhusmiṃ “netaṃ mamā”tiādinā tiṇṇaṃ gāhānaṃ abhāvo eva.

71. “Of the sources of contact” means of the sources, the places of their arising, of the six contacts; that is, the eye, etc. One is called “lost” (naṭṭho); he is one who says “I am.” In contrast, he who understands as it actually is the origin, etc., of the six bases for contact has lived the holy life; the other has not lived the holy life, and I am such a one. “This very” means this very absence of the three kinds of grasping—“This is not mine,” etc.—with regard to the eye.

Paṭhamachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Discourse on the Six Bases for Contact is concluded.

10. Dutiyachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Second Discourse on the Six Bases for Contact

72. Apunabbhavoti [M 2.0294] punabbhavābhāvo.

72. “No renewed becoming” means the absence of renewed becoming.

11. Tatiyachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Third Discourse on the Six Bases for Contact

73. Panassasanti [V 2.0013] ekaṃsena naṭṭhoti ayamettha atthoti āha “atinaṭṭho”ti, dhurato eva naṭṭhoti attho.

73. “He is lost” (panassati): the meaning here is “he is definitely lost.” Thus it is said, “utterly lost” (atinaṭṭho), which means lost from the very beginning.

Tatiyachaphassāyatanasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Third Discourse on the Six Bases for Contact is concluded.

Migajālavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Migajāla Chapter is concluded.

8. Gilānavaggo

8. The Chapter on the Sick

1-5. Paṭhamagilānasuttādivaṇṇanā

1-5. Commentary on the First Discourse on the Sick Person, etc.

74-78. Appaññātoti nāmagottato ceva sīlādiguṇehi ca appaññāto avissuto. Theramajjhimabhāvaṃ appattatāya navo.

74-78. “Little known” means unknown by name and clan, and also by virtues such as morality; not famous. He is “new” because he has not attained the state of an elder or a middle-ranking bhikkhu.

Paṭhamagilānasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the First Discourse on the Sick Person, etc., is concluded.

7. Dutiyaavijjāpahānasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Second Discourse on Abandoning Ignorance

80. Aniccādivasena abhinivisanaṃ abhiniveso, so eva dhammasabhāvaṃ atikkamitvā parato āmasanato parāmāso, so eva gāho. Tena abhinivesaparāmāsaggāhena gaṇhituṃ na yuttā aniccādisabhāvattā. Saṅkhārā eva pavattiyā kāraṇabhāvato saṅkhāranimittāni. Yo saṅkhāresu apariññātābhinivesena passitabbo attākāro, so na hotīti añño anattākāro, tato aññato passati. Pariññātābhinivesoti tīraṇapariññāya paricchijja ñātamicchābhiniveso. Pariññātābhinivesoti vā pariññātavipassanābhiniveso. Vipassanāti arūpasattakavasena vipassanāya parijānitabbā.

80. Adherence (abhinivesa) is insistence by way of impermanence, etc. That very adherence, by transgressing the true nature of phenomena and fondling what is other, becomes misapprehension (parāmāsa); that itself is grasping (gāha). Because their nature is impermanent, etc., it is not proper to grasp them with that adherence, misapprehension, and grasping. Formations themselves, because they are the cause of occurrence, are the signs of formations. The semblance of a self that might be seen in formations with uncomprehended adherence does not exist; thus it is other, a semblance of not-self. From that, one sees it as other. “Comprehended adherence” is the adherence of desire that is known, having been defined by the full understanding of judgment. Or, “comprehended adherence” is the adherence of comprehended insight. They are to be fully known by means of insight by way of the seven formless items.

Dutiyaavijjāpahānasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Second Discourse on Abandoning Ignorance is concluded.

8. Sambahulabhikkhusuttavaṇṇanā

8. Commentary on the Discourse to a Number of Bhikkhus

81. Kevalanti [M 2.0295] itaralakkhaṇehi avomissaṃ.

81. “Exclusively” means unmixed with other characteristics.

10. Phaggunapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Discourse on Phagguna's Question

83. Taṇhāya [V 2.0014] pahīnāya diṭṭhimānāpi pahīnā evāti “taṇhāpapañcassa chinnattā chinnapapañce”ti vuttaṃ. Dutiyapadepi eseva nayo. Idha sattavohāro cakkhādīsu vijjamānesu eva hoti, tasmā parinibbutānañca vohāro cakkhādīsu sannissayeneva, nāññathāti atikkantabuddhehi pariharitāni cakkhusotādīni pucchāmīti pucchati “atthi nu kho bhante”tiādinā. Cakkhusotādivaṭṭaṃ vaṭṭe pavatteyya.

83. When craving is abandoned, views and conceit are also abandoned; thus it is said, “with proliferation cut off because the proliferation of craving has been cut off.” The same principle applies to the second phrase as well. Here, the designation “a being” occurs only when the eye, etc., exist. Therefore, the designation of those who are fully extinguished also depends on the eye, etc., and not otherwise. Thus, he asks about the eye, ear, etc., which were employed by Buddhas who have passed beyond, by asking, “Is there, venerable sir?” etc. The round of the eye, ear, etc., would revolve in the cycle of existence.

Phaggunapañhāsuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Discourse on Phagguna's Question is concluded.

Gilānavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Chapter on the Sick is concluded.

9. Channavaggo

9. The Channa Chapter

1. Palokadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Discourse on Things that Crumble

84. Aniccalakkhaṇameva kathitaṃ, tañca pariyāyena, aniccalakkhaṇe kathite itaralakkhaṇāni kathitāneva honti byabhicārabhāvato.

84. Only the characteristic of impermanence is stated, and that by implication. When the characteristic of impermanence is stated, the other characteristics are also stated because they do not deviate from it.

2. Suññatalokasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Discourse on the World as Empty

85. Attano idanti attaniyanti āha “attano santakenā”ti.

85. “This is one's own” means “belonging to oneself.” Thus it is said, “with what is one's own.”

3. Saṃkhittadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Discourse on the Dhamma in Brief

86. Vuttanayeneva veditabbanti khandhiyavagge khandhavasena āgataṃ, idha āyatanavasenāti ayameva viseso.

86. It should be understood in the way already stated. In the Chapter on the Aggregates it was presented by way of the aggregates, whereas here it is by way of the sense bases. This is the only difference.

4. Channasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Discourse to Channa

87. Sabbanimittehi paṭisallīyati etenāti paṭisallānaṃ, phalasamāpatti. Jīvitahārakasatthaṃ [V 2.0015] jīvitassa haraṇato, sattānañca sasanato hiṃsanato[M 2.0296]. Paricaritoti payirupāsito. Tena yathānusiṭṭhaṃ paṭipajjinti dīpeti.

87. “Seclusion” (paṭisallānaṃ) is so called because by this one is secluded from all signs; it is the attainment of fruition. A “life-taking weapon” is so called because it takes life and because it wounds and harms beings. “Served” (paricarito) means attended upon. By this he shows that they practice as instructed.

Anupavajjanti parehi na upavaditabbaṃ. Taṃ panettha āyatiṃ appaṭisandhibhāvato hotīti āha “appavattika”nti. “Netaṃ mamā”tiādīni vadanto arahatte pakkhipitvā kathesi. Puthujjanabhāvameva dīpento vadati akatakiccabhāvadīpanena. Kiñcāpi thero pucchitaṃ pañhaṃ arahatte pakkhipitvā kathesi, “na samanupassāmī”ti pana vadanto kiñci nipphattiṃ na kathesi, tasmā “idampi manasi kātabba”nti idaṃ ānetvā sambandho.

“Blameless” (anupavajjaṃ) means not to be blamed by others. That, in this case, occurs because of the absence of future relinking; thus it is said to be “without continuation.” Saying “This is not mine,” etc., he spoke, including this in arahantship. He speaks revealing only the state of a worldling by showing the state of one whose task is undone. Although the elder, in answering the question, included it in arahantship, yet by saying “I do not see,” he did not declare any attainment. Therefore, the connection is made by bringing in the phrase “this too should be kept in mind.”

Kilesapassaddhīti kilesapariḷāhavūpasamo. Bhavatthāya puna bhavatthāya. Ālayanikanti pariyuṭṭhāneti bhavantare apekkhāsaññite ālaye nikantiyā ca pariyuṭṭhānappattiyā. Asati avijjamānāya. Paṭisandhivasena aññabhavato idhāgamanaṃ āgati nāma. Cutivasena gamananti cavanavasena ito gati. Anurūpagamanaṃ gati nāma tadubhayaṃ na hoti. Cutūpapāto aparāparabhavanavasena cuti, upapajjanavasena upapāto, tadubhayampi na hoti. Evaṃ pana cutūpapāte asati nevidha na idha loke. Na huraṃ na paraloke hoti. Tato eva na ubhayattha hoti. Ayameva anto ayaṃ idhaloke paraloke ca abhāvoyeva dukkhassa pariyosānaṃ. Ayamevāti yathāvutto eva – ettha etasmiṃ pāṭhe paramparāgato pamāṇabhūto attho.

“Calming of defilements” is the pacification of the fever of defilements. “For the sake of existence”: again for the sake of existence. “In the obsession with attachment and craving”: because of reaching a state of obsession with craving for attachment, which is known as expectation for another existence. “When it is absent”: being non-existent. Coming here from another existence by way of relinking is called “coming” (āgati). Going from here by way of passing away is called “going” (gati). A corresponding going is also called “going.” Neither of these two occurs. Passing away and reappearance are passing away by way of successive existences and reappearing by way of arising; neither of these two occurs. Thus, when passing away and reappearance are absent, one is neither here in this world, nor beyond in the other world. For that reason, one is not in both. This itself is the end; this very absence in this world and the other world is the termination of suffering. “This very” means just as was stated. Here, in this reading, the meaning that has come down through tradition is the authority.

Ye panāti sammavādino sandhāya vadati. Antarābhavaṃ icchanti “evaṃ bhavena bhavantarasambandho yujjeyyā”ti. Niratthakaṃ antarābhavassa nāma kassaci abhāvato. Cutikkhandhānantarañhi paṭisandhikkhandhānaṃyeva pātubhāvo. Tenāha “antarābhavassa…pe… paṭikkhittoyevā”ti. Tattha bhāvoti atthitā. Abhidhamme kathāvatthuppakaraṇe (kathā. 505-507) paṭikkhittoyeva. Yadi evaṃ “antarenā”ti idaṃ kathanti āha “antarenā”tiādi. Vikappato aññaṃ vikappantaraṃ, tassa dīpanaṃ “antarenā”ti vacanaṃ. Na antarābhavadīpanaṃ tādisassa anupalabbhanato payojanābhāvato ca. Yattha hi vipākaviññāṇassa paccayo, tatthassa nissayabhūtassa vatthussa sahabhāvīnañca [M 2.0297] khandhānaṃ sambhavoti saddhiṃ attano nissayena viññāṇaṃ uppajjatevāti nāssa uppattiyā desadūratā veditabbā. “Neva idha na hura”nti vuttadvayato aparaṃ vikappena “na ubhaya”nti, tatthapi na hotiyevāti adhippāyo. “Antarenā”ti vā “vinā”ti iminā samānattho nipāto, tasmā nevidha, na huraṃ, ubhayaṃ vināpi nevāti attho.

“But those who”: this is said with reference to those of right view. They desire an intermediate state, thinking, “In this way, the connection between one existence and another would be justified.” It is meaningless, because for anyone there is no such thing as an intermediate state. For immediately after the aggregates of death, the aggregates of relinking appear. Hence it is said: “The intermediate state... is indeed rejected.” Therein, “state” (bhāva) means existence. In the Abhidhamma, in the treatise Kathāvatthu, it is indeed rejected. If so, why the word “in between” (antarena)? He says: “in between,” etc. The word 'in between' indicates another alternative interpretation. It is not an explanation of an intermediate state, because such a thing is not found and because it serves no purpose. For wherever there is a condition for resultant consciousness, there the supporting base and the co-arisen aggregates come to be. Thus consciousness arises together with its own support, so no spatial distance should be understood for its arising. From the two statements “neither here nor beyond,” by another alternative it is said “not in both.” The intention is that it does not exist there either. Or the particle “in between” (antarena) has the same meaning as “without” (vinā). Therefore the meaning is: neither here, nor beyond, and not even without both.

Āharīti [V 2.0016] chinnavasena gaṇhi. Tenāha “kaṇṭhanāḷaṃ chindī”ti. Pariggaṇhantoti sammasanto. Parinibbuto dīgharattaṃ vipassanāyaṃ yuttapayuttabhāvato. “Anupavajjaṃ channena bhikkhunā satthaṃ āharita”nti, kathesīti asekkhakāle byākaraṇaṃ viya katvā kathesi.

“He took up” (āhari): he took it up as if to cut. Therefore he said, “He cut his throat.” “Grasping” (pariggaṇhanto) means comprehending. “Fully extinguished” (parinibbuto): because of being long devoted to the practice of insight meditation. “It is blameless for the bhikkhu Channa that the knife was taken up,” he said, speaking as if making a declaration at the time of being a non-trainee.

Imināti “upavajjakulānī”ti iminā vacanena. Theroti sāriputtatthero. Evanti evaṃ pubbakālesu saṃsaṭṭhavihārī hutvā ṭhito pacchā arahattaṃ pāpuṇissatīti āsaṅkanto pucchati. Sesaṃ uttānameva.

“By this” means by this statement “families liable to reproach.” “The elder” means the Elder Sāriputta. “Thus”: he asks, suspecting, “Having been one who lived in close association in former times, will he later attain arahantship?” The rest is clear.

Channasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Discourse to Channa is concluded.

5-6. Puṇṇasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-6. Commentary on the Puṇṇasutta and Others.

88-89. Tanti cakkhurūpadvayaṃ. Tenāha “cakkhuñceva rūpañcā”ti. Nandisamudayāti nandiyā samudayataṇhāya sesakāraṇehi nandiyā samuditi samodhānaṃ nandisamudayo, tasmā nandisamudayā. Tenāha “taṇhāya samodhānenā”ti. Pañcakkhandhasaṅkhātassa dukkhassa samodhānena samuditi pavattiyevāti saha samudayena dukkhassa dassitattā “vaṭṭaṃ matthakaṃ pāpetvā”ti vuttaṃ. Nirodhūpāyena saddhiṃ nirodhassa dassitattā “vivaṭṭaṃ matthakaṃ pāpetvā”ti. Pucchānusandhiādīsu aññataro na hotīti āha – “pāṭiyekko anusandhī”ti.

88-89. The pair is the eye and form. Hence he said: ‘the eye and form.’ As to ‘from the origin of delight’: the origin of delight is the coming together of delight due to the craving that is its origin along with other causes; therefore, ‘from the origin of delight.’ Hence he said: ‘by the coming together of craving.’ Since suffering, designated as the five aggregates, has been shown together with its origin—for its arising is its proceeding in conjunction with its cause—it is said: ‘having brought the round to its culmination.’ Since cessation has been shown together with the means to cessation, it is said: ‘having brought the rolling back to its culmination.’ Because this is not one of the connections such as the connection of a question, he said: ‘a special connection.’

Caṇḍāti kodhanā. Duṭṭhāti dosavantoti attho. Kibbisāti pāpā. Kakkhaḷāti dāruṇā. Ghaṭikamuggarenāti ekasmiṃ pakkhe ghaṭikaṃ dassetvā katena rassadaṇḍena. Sattānaṃ sasanato satthaṃ, tato eva jīvitassa haraṇato hārakañcāti satthahārakaṃ. Indriyasaṃvaro “damo”ti vutto manacchaṭṭhānaṃ indriyānaṃ damanato. Paññā “damo”ti vuttā kilesavisevitānaṃ damanato vūpasamanato. Uposathakammaṃ “damo”ti [M 2.0298] vuttaṃ kāyadvārādīhi uppajjanakaasamassa damanato. Khanti “damo”ti veditabbā akkhantiyā damanato vūpasamanato. Tenāha “upasamoti tasseva vevacana”nti.

‘Fierce’ means irascible. ‘Wicked’ means hateful; this is the meaning. ‘Corrupt’ means evil. ‘Harsh’ means cruel. ‘With a hammer-mallet’ means with a short club made to look like a mallet on one side. ‘Weapon-bearer’ (satthahārakaṃ): it is a ‘weapon’ (satthaṃ) because it disciplines beings, and a ‘bearer’ (hārakaṃ) because it takes life. Restraint of the sense faculties is called ‘taming’ because it is the taming of the six sense faculties with the mind as the sixth. Wisdom is called ‘taming’ because it is the taming and pacifying of the defilements. The Uposatha observance is called ‘taming’ because it is the taming of the lack of calm liable to arise through the body door and so on. Patience should be known as ‘taming’ because it is the taming and pacifying of impatience. Hence he said: ‘“Calm” is a synonym for that.’

Etthāti sunāparantajanapade. Ete dveti ayaṃ puṇṇatthero tassa kaniṭṭhoti ete dve bhātaro. Āhacca aṭṭhāsi uḷāraṃ buddhārammaṇaṃ pītiṃ uppādetvā. Satta sīhanāde naditvāti mammacchedakānampi akkosaparibhāsānaṃ khamane santosābhāvadīpanaṃ, pāṇippahārassa, leḍḍuppahārassa, daṇḍappahārassa, satthappahārassa, jīvitavoropanassa, khamane santosābhāvadīpanañcāti [V 2.0017] evaṃ satta sīhanāde naditvā. Catūsu ṭhānesu vasitattā pāḷiyaṃ vasanaṭṭhānaṃ anuddesikaṃ katvā “sunāparantasmiṃ janapade viharati”icceva vuttaṃ.

‘Here’ means in the Sunāparanta country. ‘These two’ means this Elder Puṇṇa and his younger brother; these two brothers. ‘He stood resolute’ means having aroused sublime joy with the Buddha as its object. ‘Having roared seven lion’s roars’ means demonstrating his state of contentment in tolerating even heart-rending insults and abuse, in tolerating blows with the fist, blows with clods, blows with a stick, blows with a weapon, and the taking of his life. Thus he roared seven lion’s roars. Because he had dwelled in four places, the Pāli text does not specify a dwelling place, saying only: ‘he dwells in the Sunāparanta country.’

Catūsu ṭhānesūti abbuhatthapabbate, samuddagirivihāre, mātulagirimhi, makuḷakārāmavihāreti imesu catūsu ṭhānesu. Tanti caṅkamaṃ āruyha koci bhikkhu caṅkamituṃ samattho natthi mahatā samuddaparissayena bhāvanāmanasikārassa anabhisambhuṇanato. Uppātikanti uppātakaraṃ mahāsaṅkhobhaṃ uṭṭhapetvā. Sammukheti anilapadese. Paṭivedesunti pavedesuṃ.

‘In four places’ means on Abbuhattha Mountain, at Samuddagiri Monastery, on Mātula Mountain, and at Makuḷakārāma Monastery; in these four places. ‘There’: having ascended the walking path, no bhikkhu is able to walk there because of the great weariness from the ocean and because of being unable to accomplish the mind-work of meditation. ‘Calamitous’ means having stirred up a great commotion that causes calamity. ‘Face to face’ means in a windless place. ‘They informed’ means they reported.

Āraddhakālato paṭṭhāyāti maṇḍalamāḷassa kātuṃ pathavīmitakālato pabhuti. Saccabandhena pañcasatāni paripūretuṃ “ekūnapañcasatāna”nti vuttaṃ. Gandhakuṭinti jetavanamahāvihāre mahāgandhakuṭiṃ.

‘From the time it was begun’ means from the time the ground was measured for making the circular hall. In order to complete the five hundred by the bond of truth, it was said: ‘of the four hundred and ninety-nine.’ ‘The Perfumed Chamber’ means the Great Perfumed Chamber in the Jetavana Mahāvihāra.

Saccabandhanāmoti saccabandhe pabbate ciranivāsitāya “saccabandho”tveva laddhanāmo. Arahattaṃ pāpuṇīti pañcābhiññāparivāraṃ arahattaṃ adhigacchi. Tenāha “maggenevassa abhiññā āgatā”ti.

‘Named Saccabandha’ means he obtained the name ‘Saccabandha’ because he had lived for a long time on Saccabandha Mountain. ‘He attained arahantship’ means he reached arahantship accompanied by the five direct knowledges. Hence it is said: ‘His direct knowledges came by means of the path itself.’

Tasmiṃ sannipatitā mahājanā keci sotāpannā, keci sakadāgāmino, keci anāgāmino, keci arahanto ahesuṃ. Tatthāpi keci tevijjā, keci chaḷabhiññā, keci paṭisambhidappattā ahesuṃ. Taṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “mahājanassa bandhanamokkho jāto”ti. Ye pana tattha saraṇagamanapañcasīladasasīlasamādānena laddhānuggahā, tesaṃ devatānañca vasena “mahantaṃ buddhakolāhalaṃ ahosī”ti vuttaṃ.

Among the great crowd gathered there, some were stream-enterers, some once-returners, some non-returners, and some arahants. Among them, too, some possessed the three true knowledges, some the six direct knowledges, and some had attained the analytical knowledges. In reference to this it was said: ‘The great crowd was released from bondage.’ But on account of those who there received the benefit of undertaking the going for refuge, the five precepts, and the ten precepts, and on account of the devas, it was said: ‘There was a great acclamation for the Buddha.’

Aruṇaṃ [M 2.0299] pana mahāgandhakuṭiyaṃyeva uṭṭhapesi devatānuggahatthañceva kulānudayāya ca. Apāyamagge otārito “koci lokassa sajitā atthi, tassa vasena pavattisaṃhārā honti, tenevāyaṃ pajā sanāthā hoti, taṃ yuñjati ca tasmiṃ tasmiṃ kamme”ti micchāgāhehi. Paricaritabbaṃ yāci “ettha mayā ciraṃ vasitabba”nti.

He raised the dawn-offering in the Great Perfumed Chamber itself, both for the benefit of the devas and for the welfare of his clan. He was led down the path to the plane of misery by wrong grasp, thinking: ‘There is some creator of the world. By his power occurrence and dissolution happen. Through him this generation has a protector, and he yokes one to this or that deed.’ He asked to be served, thinking: ‘I must dwell here for a long time.’

Chaṭṭhanti bāhiyasuttaṃ. Taṃ uttānameva heṭṭhā vuttanayattā.

The sixth is the Bāhiya Sutta. It is plain because of the method stated below.

Puṇṇasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Puṇṇasutta and Others is finished.

7-8. Paṭhamaejāsuttādivaṇṇanā

7-8. The Commentary on the First Sutta on Agitation and Others.

90-91. Ejati [V 2.0018] chaḷārammaṇanimittaṃ kampatīti ejā. Tenāha “calanaṭṭhenā”ti. Ābādhanaṭṭhena pīḷanaṭṭhena. Anto dosanaṭṭhenāti antocitte eva padussanaṭṭhena. Nikantanaṭṭhenāti chindanaṭṭhena. Heṭṭhā gahitamevāti heṭṭhā maññitasamugghātasāruppasutte āgatameva. Vuttanayameva maññitasamugghātasutte.

90-91. ‘Agitation’ (ejā) is so called because ‘it agitates’ (ejati), that is, it quakes (kampati) in regard to the signs of the six sense objects. Hence he said: ‘in the sense of shaking.’ In the sense of afflicting, in the sense of oppressing. ‘In the sense of internal corruption’ means in the sense of corrupting within the mind itself. ‘In the sense of cutting down’ means in the sense of severing. ‘What was taken up below’ means what has come in the suitable sutta below on the uprooting of conceit. The method has been stated in the Sutta on the Uprooting of Conceit.

Paṭhamaejāsuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the First Sutta on Agitation and Others is finished.

9-10. Paṭhamadvayasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. The Commentary on the First Sutta on the Dyad and Others.

92-93. Dvayanti dukaṃ. Pāḷiyaṃ āmeḍitalopena niddesoti āha “dve dve koṭṭhāse”ti. Evametanti evaṃ aniccādibhāvena etaṃ cakkhurūpañcāti dvayaṃ. Calatīti anavaṭṭhānena pacalati. Byathatīti jarāya maraṇena ca pavedhati. Hetu ceva uppattinimittattā. Sahagatīti sahappavatti, tāya gahetabbattā “saṅgatī”ti phasso vutto. Esa nayo sesapadadvayepi. Yasmā ca saṃgacchamānadhammavimuttā saṅgati nāma natthi, tathā sannipātasamavāyā, tesaṃ vasena nibbatto phasso tathā vuccatīti. Tenāha “iminā”tiādi.

92-93. ‘Dyad’ (dvaya) means a pair (dukaṃ). The explanation in the Pāli is by elision of the reduplication; thus he said, ‘two portions.’ ‘Thus this’ means: in this way, on account of impermanence, etc., this eye and form are a dyad. ‘It trembles’ means it shakes because of instability. ‘It is afflicted’ means it quakes on account of aging and death. It is a cause because it is a condition for arising. ‘Accompanied’ means co-arising; because it is to be grasped by that, contact is called ‘association’ (saṅgati). This is the method for the remaining two pairs of terms as well. And since there is no such thing as association apart from the phenomena that associate, nor are there conjunctions and combinations, contact, which is produced on account of them, is so called. Hence he said: ‘by this,’ etc.

Vatthūti [M 2.0300] cakkhu nissayapaccayādibhāvena. Ārammaṇanti rūpaṃ ārammaṇapaccayādibhāvena. Sahajātā tayo khandhā vedanādayo, te sahajātādipaccayabhāvena. Ayaṃ hetūti ayaṃ tividho hetū. Phassenātiādīsu ayaṃ saṅkhepattho – yasmā rūpārammaṇe phasse attano phusanakiccaṃ karonte evaṃ vedanā anubhavanakiccaṃ, saññā sañjānanakiccaṃ karoti, tasmā “phassena phuṭṭhamevā”tiādivuttameva atthaṃ idāni puggalādhiṭṭhānena dassetuṃ “phuṭṭho”tiādi vuttaṃ. Pañceva khandhā bhagavatā samatiṃsāya ākārehi vuttā. Kasmāti āha “katha”ntiādi. Rukkhasākhāsu rukkhavohāro viya ekekadhammepi khandhavohāro hotiyeva. Tenāha bhagavā – “viññāṇaṃ viññāṇakkhandho”ti (yama. khandhayamaka 2).

‘The basis’ is the eye, by way of dependence condition, etc. ‘The object’ is form, by way of object condition, etc. The three co-arisen aggregates—feeling and the rest—are so by way of co-nascence condition, etc. ‘This is the cause’ means this threefold cause. Regarding ‘by contact,’ etc., this is the concise meaning: since, when contact is performing its function of contacting a form object, feeling performs its function of experiencing and perception performs its function of perceiving, therefore, to show the same meaning already stated in ‘touched by contact,’ etc., now from the standpoint of a person, ‘touched,’ etc., is said. The five aggregates have been spoken of by the Blessed One in thirty ways. Why? He asks: ‘How?’ etc. Just as there is the designation ‘tree’ for the branches of a tree, so too there is the designation ‘aggregate’ for each single phenomenon. Hence the Blessed One said: ‘Consciousness is the consciousness aggregate’ (Yama. Khandhayamaka 2).

Paṭhamadvayasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the First Sutta on the Dyad and Others is finished.

Channavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Channavagga is finished.

10. Saḷavaggo

10. The Chapter of the Six

1. Adantaaguttasuttavaṇṇanā

1. The Commentary on the Sutta on the Untamed and Unguarded

94. Adamitāti [V 2.0019] damaṃ nibbisevanabhāvaṃ anītā. Agopitāti satisaṅkhātāya vatiyā na rakkhitā. Apihitāti satikavāṭena na pihitā. Catūhipi padehi indriyānaṃ anāvaraṇamevāha. Adhikaṃ vahantīti adhivāhā, dukkhassa adhivāhā dukkhādhivāhā. Nirayesu uppajjanakaṃ nerayikaṃ. Ādi-saddena sesapāḷiṃ saṅgaṇhāti.

94. ‘Untamed’ (adamitā) means not brought to the state of thoroughly practicing discipline. ‘Unguarded’ (agopitā) means not protected by the fence called mindfulness. ‘Unclosed’ (apihitā) means not shut by the gate of mindfulness. With all four terms he speaks only of the non-restraint of the sense faculties. ‘They bear much’ (adhikaṃ vahanti), thus they are ‘bearers of much’ (adhivāhā); bearers of suffering are ‘bearers of suffering’ (dukkhādhivāhā). That which causes arising in the hells is ‘hellish’ (nerayikaṃ). With the word ‘etc.’ he includes the rest of the Pāli text.

Saḷevāti cha-kārassa sa-kāro, ḷa-kāro padasandhikaro. Yatthāti nimittatthe bhummaṃ. Anavassutā atintāti rāgena atemitā.

Regarding saḷeva: the letter ‘sa’ is for the letter ‘cha,’ and the letter ‘ḷa’ is for euphonic combination. ‘Where’ (yattha) is a locative in the sense of a condition. ‘Not oozing, not drenched’ means not moistened by lust.

Assāditanti assādaṃ itaṃ upagataṃ. Tenāha “assādavanta”nti. Sukhadukkhanti iṭṭhāniṭṭhaṃ. Anvayatīti anvayo, hetu. Phassoti anvayo etassāti phassanvayanti āha – “phassahetuka”nti. “Aviruddha”iti vibhattilopena niddeso.

‘Tasted’ (assāditaṃ) means gone to or approached gratification. Hence he said: ‘possessing gratification.’ ‘Pleasure and pain’ means desirable and undesirable. ‘It follows’ (anvayati), thus ‘consequence’ (anvayo), a cause. ‘Contact is the consequence of this,’ thus ‘having contact as its consequence’; hence he said: ‘caused by contact.’ The explanation of ‘unopposed’ (aviruddha) is by elision of the case ending.

Papañcasaññāti [M 2.0301] taṇhādisamadhūpasaṃhatasaññā. Tenāha “kilesasaññāya papañcasaññā nāma hutvā”ti. Papañcasaññā etesaṃ atthīti papañcasaññā, itarītarā narā. Papañcayantāti saṃsāre papañcaṃ cirāyanaṃ karontā. Saññinoti gehassitasaññāya saññāvanto. Manomayaṃ vitakkanti kevalaṃ manasā sambhāvitaṃ micchāvitakkaṃ. Irīyatīti iriyaṃ paṭipattiṃ irīyati paṭipajjati.

‘Perception of proliferation’ (papañcasaññā) is perception connected with craving and other defilements. Hence he said: ‘having become what is called perception of proliferation through the perception of defilements.’ Those for whom this perception of proliferation exists are ‘those with perception of proliferation,’ that is, various kinds of people. ‘They proliferate’ means they make proliferation last long in saṃsāra. ‘Perceivers’ means those possessing perception connected with the household life. ‘A thought that is mind-made’ means a wrong thought produced simply by the mind. ‘He comports himself’ means he undertakes or practices the practice of deportment.

Suṭṭhu bhāvitoti suṭṭhubhāvaṃ subhāvanaṃ ito bhāvitabhāvito. Phuṭṭhassa cittanti tena yathāvuttaphassena phuṭṭhaṃ assa cittaṃ. Na vikampate kvacīti kismiñci iṭṭhāniṭṭhārammaṇe na kampati. Pāragāti pāragāmino bhavatha.

‘Well developed’: because of being well developed, well cultivated, he is ‘developed and cultivated.’ ‘His mind being touched’ means his mind is touched by that aforesaid contact. ‘It does not waver anywhere’ means it does not quake in regard to any desirable or undesirable object. ‘Go to the far shore’ means ‘you should become goers to the far shore.’

Adantaaguttasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Sutta on the Untamed and Unguarded is finished.

2. Mālukyaputtasuttavaṇṇanā

2. The Commentary on the Mālukyaputta Sutta

95. Apasādetīti [V 2.0020] tajjeti. Ussādetīti ukkaṃseti. Ayaṃ kira thero mālukyaputto. Pamajjitvāti yonisomanasikārassa ananuyujjanena pamajjitvā.

95. ‘He disparages’ means he rebukes. ‘He extols’ means he praises. This elder, it seems, was Mālukyaputta. ‘Having been negligent’ means having been negligent by not applying wise attention.

Yatrāti paccattaṃ vacanālaṅkāre. Nāmāti asambhāvane apasādanapakkhe, ussādanapakkhe pana sambhāvane. Kiṃ jātanti kiṃ tena mahallakabhāvena jātanti mahallakabhāvaṃ tiṇāyapi amaññamāno vadati. Tenāha “yadi…pe… anuggaṇhanto”ti. Anuggaṇhantoti acintento. Mādisānaṃ bhagavato ovādo upakārāvahoti etarahi ovādañca pasaṃsanto.

‘Where’ (yatra) is an emphatic particle, a verbal ornament. ‘Indeed’ (nāma) is in the sense of improbability in the case of disparagement, but in the sense of probability in the case of extolling. ‘What is born of it?’ means ‘What is born of that old age?’—he speaks thus not considering old age to be worth even a blade of grass. Hence he said: ‘if…pe… not accepting.’ ‘Not accepting’ means not considering. ‘For those like me, the Blessed One’s advice is beneficial’—thus now praising the advice.

“Adiṭṭhā adiṭṭhapubbā”tiādinā parikappavasena vuttanidassanaṃ “yathā etesu chandādayo na honti, evamitaresupi pariññātesū”ti nayapaṭipajjanatthaṃ, tesampi imehi samānetabbattā. Tena vuttaṃ “supinakūpamā kāmā”ti (ma. ni. 1.234; 2.46; pāci. 417; cūḷava. 65).

The illustration beginning ‘unseen, not seen before,’ which is stated by way of conception, is for the purpose of grasping the method: ‘Just as there are no desires, etc., in these, so too in others that are fully understood,’ because they too are to be equated with these. Therefore it is said: ‘Sensual pleasures are like a dream’ (MN I 364; MN I 520; etc.).

Cakkhuviññāṇena diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattanti cakkhuviññāṇassa rūpāyatanaṃ yattako gahaṇākāro, tattakaṃ. Kittakaṃ pamāṇanti attasaṃvediyaṃ parassa na [M 2.0302] disitabbaṃ, kappanāmattaṃ rūpaṃ. Tenāha “cakkhuviññāṇaṃ hī”tiādi. Rūpeti rūpāyatane. Rūpamattamevāti nīlādibhedaṃ rūpāyatanamattaṃ, na nīlādi. Visesanivattanattho hi ayaṃ matta-saddo. Yadi evaṃ, eva-kāro kimatthiyo? Cakkhuviññāṇañhi rūpāyatane labbhamānampi nīlādivisesaṃ “idaṃ nīlaṃ nāma, idaṃ pītaṃ nāmā”ti na gaṇhāti. Kuto niccāniccādisabhāvatthanti saṃhitassapi nivattanatthaṃ evakāraggahaṇaṃ. Tenāha “na niccādisabhāva”nti. Sesaviññāṇehipīti javanaviññāṇehipi.

‘In the seen by eye-consciousness, there will be only the seen’ means: for eye-consciousness, the form base is just as much as is its mode of grasping. ‘What is its measure?’ It is to be experienced by oneself, it cannot be shown to another; form is a mere concept. Hence he said: ‘For eye-consciousness…’ etc. ‘In form’ means in the form base. ‘Merely form’ means just the form base with its distinctions of blue, etc., not the blue, etc. For this word ‘mere’ has the meaning of excluding specifics. If so, for what purpose is the word ‘only’? For although eye-consciousness apprehends the form base, it does not grasp the specifics of blue, etc., as ‘This is called blue, this is called yellow.’ How then could it grasp the state of the nature of permanence, impermanence, etc.? The word ‘only’ is included to exclude even what is connected with it. Hence he said: ‘not the nature of permanence, etc.’ ‘By the remaining consciousnesses too’ means by the impulsion consciousnesses too.

Diṭṭhaṃ nāma cakkhuviññāṇaṃ rūpāyatanassa dassananti katvā. Tenāha “rūpe rūpavijānana”nti.
