'Showing the danger from the defilements' means showing that the defilements are to be feared: if, when defilements have arisen, the death-consciousness were to be obstructed by an unwholesome mind, rebirth would definitely be in hell or the animal realm. Having said, 'Or it is said thus according to the occasion,' he explains its meaning by saying, 'For in the case of the eye-door,' etc. 'Or a lustful mind' means a mind impassioned by lust. How is there experience of the object's flavor with a mind of hate? The arising of painful feeling itself should be seen as its experience of the object's flavor. 'Of this occasion' means of the time of death.

Ubhinnaṃ nāsacchiddānaṃ majjhe ṭhita-aṭṭhitudanaṃ saha khuraṭṭhena chindanaṃ. Daṇḍakavāsīti dīghadaṇḍakā mahāvāsi. Nipajjitvā niddokkamananti iminā pacalāyikaniddaṃ paṭikkhipati. Tattha hi kadāci antarā micchāvitakkānaṃ sallakānaṃ avasaro siyā, nattheva nipajjitvā mahāniddaṃ okkantakāle. Vitakkānanti micchāvitakkānaṃ.

The piercing of the bone situated in the middle of the two nostrils, cutting it together with the nasal cartilage. 'A staff-axe' means a long-handled great axe. 'Lying down and succumbing to sleep': by this he rejects drowsy sleep. For in that case there may sometimes be an opportunity for the darts of wrong thoughts, but not at all when one has lain down and entered deep sleep. 'Of thoughts' means of wrong thoughts.

Ādittapariyāyasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Fire Sermon is finished.

9-10. Paṭhamahatthapādopamasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the First Discourse with the Simile of the Hand and Foot, etc.

236-237. Navamaṃ [M 2.0324] “paññāyatī”ti vuccamāne bujjhanakānaṃ ajjhāsayavasena vuttanti āha – “dasame na hotīti vuccamāne”tiādi. Ettakameva hi dvinnaṃ suttānaṃ visesoti.

236-237. He says that the ninth, 'is discerned,' is said according to the inclination of those who are to be awakened—and in the tenth, 'does not become,' etc. This much, indeed, is the difference between the two discourses.

Paṭhamahatthapādopamasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Discourse with the Simile of the Hand and Foot, etc., is finished.

Samuddavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter on the Ocean is finished.

19. Āsīvisavaggo

19. The Chapter on Venomous Snakes

1. Āsīvisopamasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Āsīvisopama Sutta

238. Ye [V 2.0041] bhikkhū tadā bhagavantaṃ parivāretvā nisinnā, tesu keci ekavihārino, keci attadutiyā, keci attatatiyā, keci attacatutthā, keci attapañcamā hutvā araññāyatanesu viharantīti vuttaṃ – “ekacārika…pe… pañcacārike”ti. Samānajjhāsayatā sabhāgavuttino. Kammaṭṭhānānuyuñjanassa kārake. Tato eva tattha yuttapayutte. Puggalajjhāsayena kāraṇabhūtena. Paccayabhūtanti apassayabhūtaṃ. “Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave”ti ārabhitvā yāva “tiṇṇo pāraṅgato thale tiṭṭhati brāhmaṇo”ti ayaṃ mātikānikkhepo. Tesaṃ mātikāya vitthārabhājanaṃ. Vāsanā bhavissatīti vāsanāvahaṃ bhavissati. Sineruṃ ukkhipanto viyātiādi imissā desanāya anaññasādhāraṇatāya sudukkarabhāvadassanaṃ.

238. It is said that among those monks who were at that time sitting surrounding the Blessed One, some were dwelling alone in forest resorts, some with a second, some with a third, some with a fourth, and some with a fifth. This is stated as: “wandering alone…pe… wandering with a fifth.” They had a similar disposition and suitable conduct. They were practitioners who applied themselves to the meditation subject. Hence, they were yoked and applied to it. By personal disposition, which is the cause. As a condition means as a support. The exposition of the matrix is from “Suppose, monks,” up to “crossed over, gone beyond, he stands on high ground, a brahmin.” For them, this is the detailed analysis of the matrix. “There will be a latent tendency” means it will be productive of a latent tendency. “Like one lifting Mount Sineru,” etc., shows the state of extreme difficulty of this teaching on account of its uncommon nature.

Mañcaṭṭhesu mañcasamaññā viya mukhaṭṭhaṃ visaṃ “mukha”nti adhippetaṃ. Sukkhakaṭṭhasadisabhāvāpādanato “kaṭṭha”nti vuccatīti kaṭṭhaṃ mukhaṃ etassāti kaṭṭhamukho, daṃsanādinā kaṭṭhasadisabhāvakaro sappo. Atha vā kaṭṭhasadisabhāvāpādanato kaṭṭhaṃ visaṃ vā mukhe etassāti kaṭṭhamukho. Iminā nayena sesapadesupi attho veditabbo. Ime cattāroti ime visakiccabhedena cattāro. Idāni taṃ nesaṃ visakiccabhedaṃ dassetuṃ “tesū”tiādi vuttaṃ. Ayasūlasamappitaṃ viyāti abbhantare ayasūlaṃ anuppavesitaṃ viya. Pakkapūtipanasaṃ viyāti paccitvā kālātikkame kuthitapanasaphalaṃ [M 2.0325] viya. Caṅgavāreti rajakānaṃ khāraparissāvane surāparissāvane vā. Anavasesaṃ chijjanena asanipātaṭṭhānaṃ viya. Mahānikhādanenāti mahantena nikhādanena.

Just as in the case of things on a bed there is the designation “bed,” so the poison in the mouth is intended by “mouth.” Because it causes a state similar to dry wood, it is called “wood”; one that has a wood-mouth is a kaṭṭhamukha, a snake that by its bite, etc., makes things like wood. Or else, because it causes a state similar to wood, the poison in its mouth is wood; hence kaṭṭhamukha. In this way, the meaning should be understood in the remaining passages too. “These four” means these four by the distinction of their venom’s action. Now, to show that distinction of their venom’s action, “among them,” etc., was said. “Like being pierced by an iron spike” means like an iron spike has been inserted inside. “Like a ripe, putrid jackfruit” means like a jackfruit that has become rotten after ripening and the passage of time. “In a straining basket” refers to what is used by washermen for straining lye or liquor. Like a place struck by a thunderbolt, by being cut without remainder. “By a great gnashing” means by a great gnashing.

Visavegavikārenāti visavegagatena vikārena. Vātenāti tassa sappassa sarīraṃ phusitvā uggatavātena. Nāsavāte pana vattabbameva natthi. Puggalapaṇṇattivasenāti tesaṃyeva soḷasannaṃ sappānaṃ āgatavisotiādipuggalanāmassa vasena catusaṭṭhi honti paccekaṃ catubbidhabhāvato. Āgatavisoti āgacchaviso, sīghataraṃ abhiruhanavisoti attho. Ghoravisoti kakkhaḷaviso, duttikicchaviso. Ayaṃ sītaudakaṃ viya hoti gāḷhadubbinimmocayabhāvena. Udakasappo hi ghoraviso hoti yevāti vuttaṃ – “udakasappādīnaṃ visaṃ viyā”ti. Paññāyatīti gaṇḍapiḷakādivasena paññāyati. Aneḷakasappo nāma mahāāsīviso. Nīlasappo nāma sākhavaṇṇo [V 2.0042] rukkhaggādīsu vicaraṇakasappo. Iminā upāyenāti yoyaṃ kaṭṭhamukhesu daṭṭhavisānaṃyeva “āgataviso no ghoraviso”tiādinā catubbidhabhāvo vutto, iminā upāyena. Kaṭṭhamukhe daṭṭhavisādayoti kaṭṭhamukhesu daṭṭhaviso, phuṭṭhaviso, vātavibhoti tayo, pūtimukhādīsu ca daṭṭhavisādayo cattāro cattāro veditabbo.

“By the distortion from the venom’s force” means by the distortion that has gone with the venom’s force. “By the wind” means by the wind that has arisen after touching that snake’s body. But regarding the breath from the nose, there is nothing to be said. “By way of the designation of individuals” means that by way of the individual names of those sixteen snakes themselves, such as “venom that has come,” there are sixty-four, because of each being fourfold. “Venom that has come” means arriving venom; the meaning is venom that spreads very quickly. “Fierce venom” means harsh venom, venom difficult to cure. This is like cold water because of its nature of being deep and hard to remove. For a water snake indeed has fierce venom, as it is said: “like the venom of water snakes, etc.” “It is discerned” means it is discerned by way of boils, pustules, etc. The snake called aneḷaka is a great venomous serpent. The snake called nīla is a snake of branch-color that moves about in treetops, etc. “By this method” means by this method, which is the fourfold state spoken of for the venoms of those bitten by kaṭṭhamukha snakes, beginning with “venom that has come, not fierce venom.” “Bitten venom, etc., of the kaṭṭhamukha” means: for the kaṭṭhamukha snakes, there are three—bitten venom, touch venom, and wind venom. And for the pūtimukha snakes, etc., four each should be understood: bitten venom, etc.

Ekekanti catusaṭṭhiyo ekekaṃ. Catudhāti aṇḍajādivibhāgena catudhā vibhajitvā. Chapaṇṇāsānīti chapaṇṇāsādhikāni. Gatamaggassāti yathāvuttasaṅkhyāgatamaggassa paṭilomato saṃkhippamānā anukkamena cattārova honti. Kulavasenāti kaṭṭhamukhādijātivasena.

“Each one” means each of the sixty-four. “Fourfold” means having divided them fourfold by the division of egg-born, etc. “Two hundred and fifty-six” means two hundred and fifty-six. “Of the path that has been taken”: being summarized in reverse order of the path taken by the aforesaid number, they become just four in sequence. “By family” means by way of the species such as kaṭṭhamukha, etc.

Sakalakāye āsiñcitvā viya ṭhapitavisāti hi tesaṃ phuṭṭhavisatā, vātavisatā vuccati. Evanti “āsittavisā”tiādinā. Etthāti āsīvisasadde vacanattho niruttinayena veditabbo. Uggatatejāti udaggatejā, attano visatejena nesaṃ kurūradabbatā vā. Dunnimmaddanavisāti mantāgadehi animmaddanīyavisā. Cattāro āsīvisāti ettha iti-saddo ādiattho. Tenettha avasesapāḷiṃ saṅgaṇhāti.

Indeed, their nature as touch-venom and wind-venom is so called because their venom is as if sprinkled and placed all over the body. “Thus” refers to the phrase “sprinkled venom,” etc. “Here” means the meaning of the word āsīvisa should be understood by the method of etymology. “Of risen power” means of lofty power; or their cruelty and ferocity is due to the power of their own venom. “Having venom hard to suppress” means having venom that cannot be suppressed by chants and antidotes. “Four venomous snakes”: here the word iti has the sense of “etc.” By this, it includes the remaining Pāli text.

Āsīvisesūti [M 2.0326] ime āsīvisā daṭṭhavisā evāti veditabbā. Sarīraṭṭhakesuyevāti tena purisena tesaṃ kassaci aniṭṭhassa akatattā āyusesassa ca vijjamānattā naṃ na daṃsiṃsūti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Pucchi yathābhūtaṃ pavedetukāmo. Durupaṭṭhāhāti durupaṭṭhānā. Sotthimaggoti sotthibhāvassa upāyo.

“Among the venomous snakes” means these venomous snakes should be known as having only biting venom. Regarding 'only to the bones of the body': it should be seen that they did not bite him because that man had not done anything undesirable to any of them and because a remnant of his life-span existed. He asked, wishing to make known what had actually happened. “Difficult to attend to” means difficult to attend to. “The path to safety” means the means to a state of safety.

Antaracaroti antaraṃ caro sukhasattu vissāsaghātī. Tenāha “vadhako”ti. Idāni tāsaṃ pesane kāraṇaṃ dassetuṃ “paṭhama”ntiādi vuttaṃ. Abhimukhagataṃ viya abhimukhagataṃ. Īdisīpi hi vacoyutti loke nirūpīyati santiyaṃ purisaṃ ṭhapetīti viya. Tasmā abhimukhagatanti abhimukhaṃ tena sampattanti attho. Vaṅkasaṇṭhānaṃ phalakaṃ rukkhamūle āgatāgatānaṃ nisīdanatthāya atthataṃ.

“An internal traitor” means one who moves within, an enemy of happiness, a destroyer of confidence. For that reason he is called “a murderer.” Now, to show the reason for sending them, “first,” etc., was said. “As if gone towards” means gone towards. For such a usage of words is also found in the world, like “he establishes a person in peace.” Therefore, “gone towards” means reached by him face-to-face; that is the meaning. A plank of crooked shape spread out at the foot of a tree for those who come and go to sit on.

Arittahattho puriso santāreti etāyāti santāraṇī. Orimatīrato uttaraṇāya setu uttarasetu. Ekena dvīhi vā gantabbo rukkhamayo setu rukkhasetu. Jaṅghasatthena gamanayoggo setu jaṅghasetu. Sakaṭena gantuṃ sakkuṇeyyo sakaṭasetu. Na kho esa brāhmaṇo paramatthato. Tadattho pana ekadesena sambhavatīti tathā vuttanti dassento “ettakānaṃ paccatthikānaṃ bāhitattā”ti āha. Desananti [V 2.0043] uddesadesanaṃ. Vinivattentoti paṭisaṃharanto. Na laddho vatāsīti na laddho vata āsi.

A man with full hands (arittahattho) crosses over (santāreti) with it (etāya), thus it is a “means of crossing” (santāraṇī). A bridge for crossing from this shore is a crossing-bridge. A wooden bridge is a bridge made of wood passable by one or two people. A footbridge is a bridge suitable for going on foot. A cart-bridge is a bridge that can be crossed by a cart. This is not, however, a brahmin in the ultimate sense. But showing that the meaning is partially possible, it is said thus: “because so many adversaries have been driven out.” “Teaching” means a teaching in brief. “Turning back” means withdrawing. “Alas, it was not obtained” means “alas, it was not obtained.”

Rājā viya kammaṃ sattesu issariyassa vattāpanato. Rājā…pe… puthujjano vaṭṭadukkhasaṅkhātāparādhatāya. Ñāṇapalāyanenāti mahābhūtehi nibbinditvā virajjitvā vimuccitukāmatāvasena uppannañāṇapalāyane maggādhigamasiddheneva ñāṇapalāyanena. Evañhettha upamāsaṃsandanaṃ matthakaṃ pāpitameva hoti.

Like a king, kamma is so called because it exercises lordship over beings. A king…pe… an ordinary person is so called because of the fault designated as the suffering of the round. “By refuge in knowledge” means by the refuge in knowledge that is accomplished by the very attainment of the path, a refuge in knowledge that has arisen by way of the desire to be liberated through disenchantment and dispassion towards the great elements. Thus here the connection of the simile has indeed been brought to its consummation.

Yatheva hītiādinā ekadesanāsamudāyassa nidassanaṃ āraddhaṃ. Yathāvuttavacanaṃ aṭṭhakathācariyānaṃ vacanena samattheti “patthaddho bhavatī”tiādinā. Tattha kaṭṭhamukhena vāti vā-saddo upamattho. Yathā kaṭṭhamukhena sappena daṭṭho patthaddho hoti, evaṃ pathavīdhātuppakopena so [M 2.0327] kāyo kaṭṭhamukheva hoti, kaṭṭhamukhagato viya patthaddho hotīti attho. Atha vā vā-saddo avadhāraṇattho. So “pathavīdhātupakopena vā”ti evaṃ ānetvā sambandhitabbo. Ayañhettha attho – kaṭṭhamukhena daṭṭhopi kāyo pathavīdhātuppakopeneva patthaddho hoti, tasmā pathavīdhātuyā aviyutto so kāyo sabbadā kaṭṭhamukhagato viya hotīti. Vā-saddo vā aniyamattho. Tatrāyamattho – kaṭṭhamukhena daṭṭho kāyo patthaddho hoti vā, na vā mantāgadavasena. Pathavīdhātuppakopena pana mantāgadarahito so kāyo kaṭṭhamukhagato viya hoti ekantapatthaddhoti. Tattha kāyoti pakatikāyo. Pūtikoti kuthito. Santattoti sabbaso tatto mahādāhappatto. Sañchinnoti sabbaso chinno cuṇṇavicuṇṇabhūto. Yadā kāyo patthaddhādibhāvappatto hoti, tadā puriso kaṭṭhamukhādisappassa mukhe vattamāno viya hotīti attho.

With “Just as, indeed,” etc., an illustration of a collection of partial teachings is begun. He supports the aforesaid statement with the words of the commentary masters, with “becomes stiff,” etc. Here, the word vā in “by a kaṭṭhamukha snake” has the sense of comparison. Just as one bitten by a kaṭṭhamukha snake becomes stiff, so by the aggravation of the earth element that body is as if bitten by a kaṭṭhamukha; the meaning is that it becomes stiff as if it had entered the mouth of a kaṭṭhamukha. Or else, the word vā has the sense of emphasis. It should be connected by taking it thus: 'by the aggravation of the earth element indeed.' The meaning here is this: even a body bitten by a kaṭṭhamukha becomes stiff solely due to the aggravation of the earth element. Therefore, that body, not separate from the earth element, is always as if in the mouth of a kaṭṭhamukha. Or, the word vā has the sense of indefiniteness. Here the meaning is: a body bitten by a kaṭṭhamukha may or may not become stiff, depending on chants and antidotes. But when the earth element is aggravated, that body, devoid of chants and antidotes, becomes absolutely stiff, as if in the mouth of a kaṭṭhamukha. Here, “body” means the natural body. “Putrid” means rotten. “Scorched” means completely burnt, having reached a state of great heat. “Shattered” means entirely broken, having become dust and fragments. When the body reaches a state of stiffness, etc., then a person is as if situated in the mouth of a kaṭṭhamukha snake or the like; that is the meaning.

Visesatoti kaṭṭhamukhādivisesato ca pathavīādivisesato ca. Anatthaggahaṇatotiādi acetanesupi bhūtesu sacetanesu viya anatthādīnaṃ paccakkhatāya nibbedajananatthaṃ āraddhaṃ. Tattha āsayatoti pavattiṭṭhānato. Etesanti mahābhūtānaṃ. Sadisatāti vammikāsayasusiragahanasaṅkāraṭṭhānāsayatāya ca sadisatā.

“Specifically” means both by the specifics of the kaṭṭhamukha snake, etc., and by the specifics of the earth element, etc. “By grasping what is not the goal,” etc., is begun for the purpose of generating disenchantment through the direct perception of what is not the goal, etc., in inanimate elements as if they were animate. Therein, “by resort” means by place of occurrence. “Of these” means of the great elements. “Similarity” means similarity due to being a resort like an anthill, a hollow, a thicket, and a rubbish heap.

Paccattalakkhaṇavasenāti visuṃ visuṃ lakkhaṇavasena. Pathavīādīnaṃ kakkhaḷabhāvādi, taṃsamaṅgino puggalassa kakkhaḷabhāvāpādanādinā vikāruppādanato visavegavikārato sadisatā veditabbā.

“By way of their own specific characteristics” means by way of their individual characteristics. The similarity of the earth element’s characteristic of hardness, etc., to the distortion from the venom’s force should be understood from its producing a distortion—such as producing hardness, etc.—in a person endowed with it.

Anatthāti [V 2.0044] byasanā. Byādhinti kuṭṭhādibyādhiṃ. Bhave jātābhinandinoti bhavesu jātiyā abhinandanasīlā. Pañcavokāre hi jātiyā abhinandanā nāma mahābhūtābhinandanā eva.

“Calamity” means disaster. “Disease” means an ailment such as leprosy. “Delighting in birth in existence” means having the nature of delighting in birth in existences. For in the five-constituent existence, delighting in birth is indeed delighting in the great elements.

Durupaṭṭhānatarānīti duppaṭikāratarāni. Durāsadāti durupasaṅkamanā. “Upaṭṭhāmī”ti upasaṅkamituṃ na sakkonti. Parijānāma kammanāmāni, upakārā nāma natthi. Anantadosūpaddavatoti aparimāṇadosūpaddavahetuto. Ekapakkhalanti ekadukkhaṃ.

“Harder to attend to” means harder to remedy. “Hard to approach” means difficult to approach. “I will attend” means: they are unable to approach. We fully understand the names of the actions, but there is no help. “Because of endless faults and troubles” means because it is a cause of immeasurable faults and troubles. “A single stumble” means a single suffering.

Rūpakkhandho [M 2.0328] bhijjamāno cattāro arūpakkhandhe gahetvāva bhijjati arūpakkhandhānaṃ ekanirodhattā. Vatthurūpampi gahetvāva bhijjanti pañcavokāre arūpakkhandhesu bhinnesu rūpakkhandhassa avaṭṭhānābhāvato. Ettāvatāti lobhuppādanamattena. Paññā nāma attabhāve uttamaṅgaṃ paññuttarattā kusaladhammānaṃ, sati ca kilesuppattiyaṃ paññāya anuppajjanato vuttaṃ – “ettāvatā paññāsīsaṃ patitaṃ nāma hotī”ti. Yoniyo upaneti tadupagassa kammapaccayassa bhāve. “Jātibhayaṃ, jarābhayaṃ, maraṇabhayaṃ, corabhaya”ntiādinā āgatāni pañcavīsati mahābhayāni, “hatthampi chindatī”tiādinā āgatāni dvattiṃsa kammakāraṇāni āgatāneva honti kāraṇassa samavaṭṭhitattā. Nandīrāgo saṅkhārakkhandhoti saṅkhārakkhandhapariyāpannattā vuttaṃ.

The aggregate of form, when breaking up, breaks up only by taking the four formless aggregates with it, because the formless aggregates have a single cessation. Even the physical basis is taken when they break up in the five-constituent existence, because when the formless aggregates have broken up, the aggregate of form has no place to stand. “By this much” means by the mere arising of greed. Wisdom is the highest part in an individual existence because wisdom is supreme among wholesome qualities; and because wisdom does not arise when a defilement arises, it is said: “By this much, the head of wisdom is said to have fallen.” One applies it wisely, due to the presence of the kamma-condition that is suitable for it. The twenty-five great fears that are mentioned beginning with “the fear of birth, the fear of aging, the fear of death, the fear of thieves,” and the thirty-two kinds of punishment that are mentioned beginning with “he cuts off even a hand,” are already present because the cause is established. “Delight-and-lust is the aggregate of formations” is said because it is included in the aggregate of formations.

Pāḷiyaṃyeva āgatā “cakkhuto cepi naṃ, bhikkhave”tiādinā. Kiñci alabhitvāti tasmiṃ suññagāme corānaṃ gayhūpagassa alābhavacaneneva tassa purisassa attano paṭisaraṇassa alābho vutto eva hotīti na uddhaṭo, purisaṭṭhāniyo bhikkhu, corā pana bāhirāyatanaṭṭhāniyā. Abhinivisitvāti vipassanābhinivesaṃ katvā. Ajjhattikāyatanavasena desanāya āgatattā vuttaṃ “upādārūpakammaṭṭhānavasenā”ti.

It is found in the Pāli itself, beginning: “Bhikkhus, if even from the eye....” “Not having obtained anything”: just as by the very statement about not obtaining anything fit to be seized by robbers in that empty village, that man's own lack of a refuge is thereby stated and not elaborated on. The bhikkhu is in the position of the man, while the robbers are in the position of the external sense bases. “Having entered” means having made an application of insight. Because the teaching was given by way of the internal sense bases, it is said, “by way of the meditation subject of derived materiality.”

Bāhirānanti bāhirāyatanānaṃ. Pañca kiccānīti corehi tadā kātabbāni pañca kiccāni. Hatthasāranti attano santake hatthehi gahetabbasārabhaṇḍaṃ. Pātanādivasena hatthaparāmāsaṃ karonti. Pahāraṭhāneti pahaṭaṭṭhāne. Ṭhānaso tasmiṃ eva khaṇeti vadanti. Attano sukhāvahaṃ kusaladhammaṃ pahāya dukkhāvahena akusalena samaṅgitā sukhāvahaṃ bhaṇḍaṃ pahāya bahi nikkhamanaṃ viyāti vuttaṃ sukhanissayattā tassa. Hatthaparā…pe… āpajjanakālo guṇasarīrassa tadā pamādena bādhitattā. Pahāra…pe… kālo tato daḷhataraṃ guṇasarīrassa bādhitattā. Pahāraṃ…pe… assamaṇakālo guṇasarīrassa maraṇappattisadisattā. Avasesajanassa dāsaparibhogena paribhuñjitabbatā aññathattappattigihibhāvāpattiyā nidassanabhāvena vuttā. Yaṃ “chasu dvāresu ārammaṇe [V 2.0045] āpāthagate”ti vuttaṃ, tameva ārammaṇaṃ nissāya samparāyiko dukkhakkhandho veditabboti yojanā.

'Of the external' means of the external sense bases. 'Five duties' means the five duties to be performed by the robbers at that time. 'Hand-treasure' means valuable goods in one's possession that can be taken by hand. They commit handling by way of felling, etc. 'At the place of striking' means at the spot where one is struck. They say: 'On the spot, at that very moment.' Having abandoned a wholesome quality that brings oneself happiness and having become endowed with an unwholesome quality that brings suffering is like abandoning beneficial goods and going outside; this is said because that wholesome quality is a basis for happiness. Hand-handling... the time of falling into an offense, because the body of virtue is then afflicted by negligence. Striking... the time... because the body of virtue is more severely afflicted than that. Striking... the time of being a non-ascetic, because it is similar to the body of virtue reaching death. For the remaining people, it is stated as an illustration of being used with a slave's usage, because of having fallen into a different state, the state of a householder. What was said, 'When an object has come into the range of the six doors,' should be understood thus: relying on that very object, the mass of suffering pertaining to the future life is to be experienced. This is the connection.

Rūpādīnīti [M 2.0329] rūpasaddagandharasāni. Tesanti yathāvuttabhūtupādārūpānaṃ. Lahutādivasenāti tesaṃ lahutādivasena. Duruttaraṇaṭṭhoti uttarituṃ asakkuṇeyyabhāvo oghaṭṭho. Vuttanayenāti “sampayuttā vedanā vedanākkhandho”tiādinā vuttanayena. Catumahābhūtādīhīti ādisaddena upādānakkhandhādīnaṃ gahaṇaṃ. Cittakiriyadassanatthanti cittapayogadassanatthaṃ. Vuttavāyāmamevāti “sammāvāyāmo”ti yo ariyamagge vutto. Bhaddekarattādīnīti “ajjeva kiccaṃ ātappa”ntiādinā (ma. ni. 3.272, 275, 276) vuttāni bhaddekarattasuttādīni.

'Forms, etc.' means forms, sounds, odors, and tastes. 'Of these' means of the aforesaid primary and derived materiality. 'By way of lightness, etc.' means by way of their lightness, etc. 'The meaning of being difficult to cross' is the state of being uncrossable; this is the meaning of 'flood.' 'By the stated method' means by the method stated beginning, 'Associated feeling is the aggregate of feeling….' 'By the four great elements, etc.': by the word 'etc.' the aggregates of clinging, etc., are included. 'For the purpose of showing the mind's activity' means for the purpose of showing the mind's application. 'The aforesaid effort' means that which is called 'right effort' in the noble path. 'The Bhaddekaratta, etc.' refers to the Bhaddekaratta Suttas, etc., stated beginning, 'Today itself the task should be undertaken….'

Kuṇṭhapādoti chinnapādova hutvā gativikalo. Mānasaṃ bandhatīti tasmiṃ citte kiccaṃ nibandhati. “Ayaṃ ariyamaggo mayhaṃ oghuttaranupāyo”ti tattha cittassa sanniṭṭhānaṃ puna tattha pavattanaṃ vīriyārambho cittabandhanaṃ.

'Lame-footed' means, having a severed foot, one is impaired in movement. 'It binds the mind' means it fastens a task to that mind. The settling of the mind on that, thinking, 'This noble path is my means for crossing the flood'; its recurring there; the undertaking of energy—this is the binding of the mind.

Tassa nāmarūpassa ime nandīrāgādayo taṇhāvijjādayoti katvā paccayo dhammāyatanekadeso. Ariyamagganibbānataṇhāvajjo idha dhammāyatanekadesoti ca. Soḷasahākārehīti pīḷanādīhi soḷasahi ākārehi. Satipaṭṭhānavibhaṅge āgatanayena saṭṭhinayasahassehi. Desanāpariyosāne…pe… patiṭṭhahiṃsūti vipañcitaññū evettha gahaṇavasena adhigatavisesā paricchinditā. Te hi tadā dhammapaṭiggāhakabhāvena satthu santike sannisinnā. Ugghaṭitaññūnaṃ pana neyyānañca visesādhigamo aṭṭhakathāyaṃ na ruḷhoti idha na gahitoti.

A part of the mind-object base is the condition for that name-and-form, namely, these things such as delight and lust, craving and ignorance. And here a part of the mind-object base is the noble path, Nibbāna, and what is other than craving. 'By sixteen aspects' means by the sixteen aspects such as oppression. By sixty thousand methods, according to the method given in the Analysis of the Foundations of Mindfulness. 'At the conclusion of the discourse... they were established': here only those of expansive wisdom are specified, as they attained a special distinction by way of comprehension. For at that time they were seated near the Teacher as receivers of the Dhamma. However, the attainment of distinction by those of quick understanding and by those who are to be led is not established in the commentary, so it is not included here.

Āsīvisopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Āsīvisopama Sutta is completed.

2. Rathopamasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Simile of the Chariot Sutta

239. Susaṃvutindriyassa bhojane mattaññuno satassa sampajānassa viharato kilesanimittaṃ dukkhaṃ anavasaranti sukhasomanassabahulatā vuttā. Yavati tena phalaṃ missitaṃ viya hotīti yoni, ekantikaṃ kāraṇaṃ. Assāti bhikkhuno. Paripuṇṇanti avikalaṃ. Anavasesaṃ āsave khepetīti āsavakkhayo, aggamaggo. Yaṃ yanti dandhaṃ majjhaṃ javoti javādīsu yaṃ yaṃ gamanaṃ. Rakkhaṇatthāyāti kilesacorehi rakkhaṇatthāya[M 2.0330]. Veganiggahaṇatthāyāti kilesahetukassa [V 2.0046] indriyavegassa niggaṇhanatthāya. Nibbisevanatthāyāti visevanassa vipphanditassa nirodhanāya. Kilesūpasamatthāyāti kilesānaṃ uparūpari samanatthāya vūpasamanatthāya.

239. For one who dwells with well-restrained faculties, knows moderation in eating, is mindful and clearly comprehending, painful signs of defilements do not find an opportunity; an abundance of happiness and gladness is spoken of. Because by it the fruit is, as it were, mixed, it is called 'source' (yoni), which means the exclusive cause. 'Of him' (assā) means of the bhikkhu. 'Complete' (paripuṇṇaṃ) means undiminished. 'He destroys the taints without remainder': this is the destruction of the taints, the supreme path. 'Whatever way they go—slow, medium, or swift': this refers to any kind of going among the various speeds. 'For the sake of protection' means for protection from the thieves that are the defilements. 'For the sake of restraining vehemence' means for restraining the vehemence of the faculties caused by the defilements. 'For the sake of non-agitation' (nibbisevanatthāya) means for the cessation of agitation and trembling. 'For the sake of calming the defilements' means for the successive calming and pacifying of the defilements.

Samaṇarati nāma samathavipassanāmaggaphalasukhāni. Dantānampi sindhavānaṃ sārathino payogena siyā kāci visevanamattāti tadabhāvaṃ dassento “nibbisevane katvā pesento”ti vuttaṃ. Ñāṇaṃ gacchatīti asaṅgamanaṃ pavattati pageva indriyabhāvanāya katattā, kilesaniggahassa sukheneva siddhattā. Dāyakassa ajjhāsayo vaso uḷāro lāmako. Deyyadhammassa pamāṇaṃ vaso appakaṃ bahukañca.

The ascetic's delight consists of the happiness of serenity, insight, path, and fruition. Even for trained thoroughbreds, there might be some degree of agitation through the charioteer's effort; showing the absence of this, it is said, 'sending them forth having made them non-agitated.' 'Knowledge proceeds' means it proceeds without impediment, precisely because the cultivation of the faculties has already been done and the subduing of the defilements is easily accomplished. The donor's disposition is the controlling factor: superior or inferior. The amount of the gift to be given is the controlling factor: small or large.

Mahāmaṇḍapaṭṭhānaṃ nāma lohapāsādassa purato eva mahābhikkhusaṅghassa sannipātakāle tesaṃ pahonakavasena mahāmaṇḍapassa kattabbaṭṭhānaṃ. Gahaṇamānanti parivisagahaṇabhājanaṃ.

The Mahāmaṇḍapaṭṭhāna is the name for the place in front of the Brazen Palace where a great pavilion should be built, sufficient for the great community of bhikkhus when they assemble. 'Gahaṇamāna' means a vessel for receiving what is served.

Pakatiyā bhattakārakicce adhigato upaṭṭhākupāsako. Mudusamakharasaññitehi, dahanapacanabhajjanasaññitehi vā tīhi pākehi.

The attendant lay devotee was naturally skilled in the task of preparing food. With three kinds of cooking, designated as soft, even, and hard; or designated as boiling, roasting, and frying.

Parisodhetvāti rañño anucchavikabhāvaṃ parisodhetvā. Bhūmiyaṃ chaḍḍesi “bhikkhūnaṃ adatvā mayā mukhe pakkhitta”nti. Mukhena gaṇhi tassa niddosabhāvaṃ dassetuṃ. Mattaṃ jānāpetuṃ pamajji. “Tvaṃ kuto āgato”ti pucchane pamādaṃ āpajji. Anubhāgoti avasiṭṭhabhāgo. Idamatthiyanti idaṃ payojanaṃ. Itaranti pāḷiyaṃ anāgatampi āhāre pamāṇajānanaṃ.

'Having purified' means having purified the king's unbecoming behavior. He threw it on the ground, thinking: 'Without giving it to the bhikkhus, I have put it in my mouth.' He took it with his mouth to show his blamelessness. He wiped the ladle to make known the amount. He was negligent in asking, 'Where have you come from?' 'Remainder' means the remaining portion. 'For this purpose' means this is the aim. 'The other' means knowing the measure in regard to food, even though it is not mentioned in the Pāḷi.

Sayanaṃ seyyā, kāmabhogīnaṃ seyyā kāmabhogiseyyā. Dakkhiṇapassena sayāno nāma natthi dakkhiṇahatthena kātabbakiccakaraṇato. Tejussadattāti idaṃ anutrāsasseva sīhassa sayananti dassetuṃ vuttaṃ. Dve purimapāde ekasmiṃ, pacchimapāde ekasmiṃ ṭhāne ṭhapetvāti hi idaṃ pādānaṃ avikkhittabhāvakaraṇadassanaṃ.

Lying down (sayanaṃ) is a posture (seyyā); the posture of those who enjoy sensual pleasures is the kāmabhogiseyyā. For them, there is no such thing as lying on the right side, because they must perform tasks with their right hand. 'Endowed with majesty' (tejussadatā): this is said to show that this is the posture of a fearless lion. For placing the two front paws in one place and the two hind paws in one place shows that the paws are kept unscattered.

Catutthajjhānaseyyāti catutthajjhānikaṃ phalasamāpattiṃ vadati. Yebhuyyena hi tathāgatā phalasamāpattiṃ samāpajjitvāva sayanti. Sā ca nesaṃ catutthajjhānato vuṭṭhāya, kilesaparinibbānassa ca katattāti vadanti[M 2.0331]. Idha sīhaseyyā āgatā pādaṃ accādhāya pubbenāparaṃ ajahitasaṃlakkhaṇā seyyāti katvā. Tenāha “ayaṃ hī”tiādi. Evanti “dakkhiṇena passenā”tiādinā vuttākārena seyyaṃ kappeti.

'The posture of the fourth jhāna': this refers to the fruition attainment based on the fourth jhāna. For the most part, Tathāgatas lie down only after having entered the fruition attainment. They say that for them this is entered after emerging from the fourth jhāna, and because the final quenching of the defilements has been accomplished. Here, the lion's posture is taken to be the posture of placing one foot over the other, characterized by not abandoning the perception of rising. Therefore he said: 'For this…,' and so on. 'Thus' means he adopts the posture in the way described by the words 'on the right side,' and so on.

Kathaṃ [V 2.0047] niddāyanto sato sampajāno hoti? Bhavaṅgacittena hi niddūpagamananti adhippāyo. Appahānenāti tadadhimuttatāya tadappahānaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Tathā tesu niddokkamanassa ādipariyosānesu ajahitasatisampajaññaṃ hoti. Tenāha – “niddaṃ okkamantopi sato sampajāno hotī”ti. Etaṃ panāti “niddaṃ okkamantopi sato sampajāno hotī”ti vuttanayaṃ vadati. Ñāṇadhātukanti na rocayiṃsūti niddokkamanepi jāgaraṇe pavattañāṇasabhāvamevāti na rocayiṃsu porāṇā. Nirantaraṃ bhavaṅgacittesu vattamānesu satisampajaññāsambhavoti adhippāyo. Purimasmiñhi naye satisampajaññassa asaṃvaro, na bhavaṅgassa. Indriyasaṃvaro, bhojane mattaññutā, jāgariyānuyogoti imehi tivaṅgikā, nimittānubyañjanaparivajjanādi sabbaṃ vipassanāpakkhikamevāti adhippāyo.

How is one mindful and clearly aware while falling asleep? The meaning is that falling asleep occurs with the life-continuum consciousness. 'By not abandoning' means its non-abandonment should be seen as due to being resolved on it. Similarly, at the beginning and end of entering sleep, mindfulness and clear comprehension are not abandoned. Therefore it is said: 'Even when entering sleep, one is mindful and clearly aware.' 'This, however,' refers to the method stated as: 'Even when entering sleep, one is mindful and clearly aware.' 'The element of knowledge': the ancients did not approve of this, meaning, they did not approve of the view that the very nature of knowledge that occurs in waking also occurs when entering sleep. The meaning is that mindfulness and clear comprehension are impossible while the life-continuum consciousness is occurring uninterruptedly. For in the former method, there is non-restraint of mindfulness and clear comprehension, not of the life-continuum. The meaning is that these three factors—restraint of the faculties, moderation in eating, and devotion to wakefulness—along with the avoidance of signs and details, etc., all belong to the side of insight.

Tānevāti vipassanākkhaṇe pavattāni indriyādīni. Yāva arahattā desanā vitthārato kathetabbā. Pāḷiyaṃ pana “yoni cassa āraddhā hoti āsavānaṃ khayāyā”ti saṅkhepena kathitā.

'Those very things' means the faculties and other factors that occur at the moment of insight. The teaching should be explained in detail up to arahantship. But in the Pāḷi text it is stated briefly as: 'And his well-spring is aroused for the destruction of the taints.'

Rathopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Simile of the Chariot Sutta is concluded.

3. Kummopamasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Kummopama Sutta

240. Aṭṭhikummoti piṭṭhiyaṃ tikhiṇaṭṭhiko kummo. Tantibandhoti byāvaṭo. Samugge viya attano kapāle pakkhipitvā. Samodahanto sammā odahanto ajjhattameva dahanto. Ārammaṇakapāleti ārammaṇakaṭāhe. Samodahantoti diṭṭhamattasseva ca gahaṇato manovitakke tattheva sammadeva odahanto. Tañhi visayavasena pavattituṃ adento. Pañca dhammeti “kālena vakkhāmī”tiādinā (pari. 362) vuccamāne.

240. 'Aṭṭhikummo' means a tortoise with sharp bones on its back. 'Tantibandho' means occupied. Like placing them into its own shell as into a casket. 'Samodahanto' means properly placing, placing right inside oneself. 'Ārammaṇakapāle' means in the bowl of the object. 'Samodahanto' means properly placing the mind’s thoughts right there upon what has just been seen, for it does not allow the mind to proceed in accordance with the sense field. 'The five things' refers to what is stated beginning with 'I will speak at the proper time' (Pari. 362).

Kummopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Kummopama Sutta is finished.

4. Paṭhamadārukkhandhopamasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the First Discourse on the Simile of the Log of Wood

241. Vilāsamāno [M 2.0332] viya sāgaraṃ patvā. Antosākhoti gaṅgāya tīrassa anto onatasākho. Temetīti tinto hoti. Tiriyaṃ patito daṇḍasetu viya ṭhitattā mahājanassa paccayo jāto, tathā mahantabhāvena gaṅgāsotaṃ otaraṇādi natthi.

241. Having reached the ocean as if playing. 'Antosākho' means a branch bent down within the bank of the Ganges. 'Temeti' means it becomes wet. Having fallen crosswise, it stood like a bridge of sticks and became a support for many people; thus, because of its great size, there is no descending into the current of the Ganges, and so on.

Ayaṃ [V 2.0048] hīti anantaraṃ vuttapuggalo. Ariyamaggaṃ oruyhāti catubbidhaṃ ariyamaggavīthiṃ otaritvā. “Cittaṃ nāmeta”ntiādinā cintetvā gihibandhanaṃ na vissajjetīti sambandho. Attano bhajamānaketi attano bhajante. Jīvikatthāya payujjitabbato payogo, khettavatthādi. Tato payogato uṭṭhitaṃ āyaṃ. Kiñcāpi bhikkhūnaṃ kāyasāmaggiṃ deti bhikkhatthāya.

'This indeed' means the person just mentioned. 'He descends upon the noble path' means having entered the fourfold course of the noble path. The connection is that having reflected, 'He should bend the mind,' and so on, he does not release the bonds of a householder. 'Attano bhajamānake' means those who attend on him. 'Prayogo' is what should be applied for the sake of a livelihood, that is, fields, land, etc. 'Āyaṃ' is the income that has arisen from that application. Although for the sake of alms he gives bodily requisites to the bhikkhus.

Muṇḍaghaṭanti bhinnoṭṭhaṃ ghaṭaṃ. Khandheti aṃse. Saṅghabhoganti saṅghasantakaṃ bhogagāmaṃ gantvā. Atthato evaṃ vadantīti tathā atthassa sambhavato evaṃ vadantā viya honti. Yāgumattake yāgunti yāguṃ pivitvā tāya aparipakkāya eva aññaṃ yāguṃ ajjhoharitvāti sambandho.

'Muṇḍaghaṭa' means a pot with a broken rim. 'Khandhe' means on the shoulder. 'Saṅghabhogaṃ' means having gone to a village of revenue belonging to the Sangha. 'They speak thus in meaning' means that because the meaning arises in that way, it is as if they are speaking thus. 'Gruel upon a mere measure of gruel': the connection is that having drunk gruel, while that is still undigested, he consumes another gruel.

Kilesānurañjitovāti yonisomanasikārassa abhāvena kilesānugatacitto eva. Akkhīni nīharitvāti kodhavasena akkhike karonto akkhīni nīharitvā vicarissati. Uddhato hoti avūpasanto.

'Tinged by defilements' means that his mind is accompanied by defilements due to the absence of wise attention. 'Having taken out his eyes' means that, overcome by anger, making his eyes into sockets, he will wander about having taken out his eyes. 'He is agitated' means he is unpacified.

Diṭṭhigatikoti sāsaniko evaṃ ayoniso ummujjitvā sāsanaṃ uddhammaṃ ubbinayaṃ katvā dīpento ariṭṭhasadiso. Tenāha “so hī”tiādi. Arūpabhave rūpaṃ atthi, aññathā tato cutassa kuto rūpakkhandhassa sambhavo. Asaññībhave cittaṃ atthīti etthāpi eseva nayo. Bahucittakkhaṇiko lokuttaramaggo “yo ime cattāro satipaṭṭhāne bhāveyya sattavassānī”tiādivacanato (dī. ni. 2.404; ma. ni. 1.137). Anusayo cittavippayutto, aññathā sāvajjānavajjadhammānaṃ ekajjhaṃ uppatti siyā. Te ca sattā sandhāvanti saṃsaranti, aññathā kammaphalānaṃ sambandho na siyāti. Iti vadanto evaṃ micchāvādaṃ paggayha vadanto.

'One gone to a view' is one who is nominally in the Dispensation but, having emerged unwisely, he is like Ariṭṭha, who makes the Teaching into non-Dhamma and the Discipline into non-Vinaya and proclaims it so. Therefore it is said: 'For he...,' etc. Form exists in the formless realm; otherwise, for one who has fallen from there, whence could the form aggregate arise? Mind exists in the realm of non-percipient beings—here too the same method applies. The supramundane path lasts for many mind-moments, as is clear from such statements as: 'Whoever would develop these four establishments of mindfulness for seven years...' (Dī. Ni. 2.404; Ma. Ni. 1.137). A latent tendency is dissociated from the mind; otherwise, faulty and faultless states would arise together. And those beings run on and transmigrate; otherwise, there would be no connection between kamma and its fruit. Thus speaking, he speaks upholding such a wrong view.

Temanarukkho viya…pe… dullabhadhammassavano ca puggalo saddhāsinehena atemanato. Katavinayo sikkhitavinayo yathā “katavijjo”ti. Dhammakathikānaṃ [M 2.0333] vicārethāti idaṃ deyyadhammaṃ dhammakathikānaṃ ayyānaṃ tassa tassa yuttavasena vicārethāti niyyātanavasena vatvā. Divākathiko sarabhāṇako sāyanhe katheti, purimayāmaṃ kathento rattikathiko bhāṇakapuggalo.

Like a Temana tree... and so on... and a person who hears the rare Dhamma, who is not moistened by the affection of faith. 'One who has completed the training' means one trained in the discipline, just as in 'one who has completed his knowledge.' 'He should allot it to the Dhamma speakers' means: having spoken with the intention of dedicating it, he should allot this gift to the venerable Dhamma speakers according to their individual suitability. A daytime speaker is a reciter who speaks in the evening; a nighttime speaker is a reciting person who speaks during the first watch of the night.

Nandanavanābhirāmeti nandanavanaṃ viya manorame. Assa bhārahārabhikkhūti assa kiccavāhakabhikkhū upajjhāyādayo. Sukhanisinnakathanti paṭisanthārakathāpubbakaṃ upanisinnakathaṃ sutvā.

'Delighting in the Nandana Grove' means in what is as delightful as the Nandana Grove. 'The bhikkhus who bear his burden' means the bhikkhus who carry out his duties, such as his preceptor and so on. 'The talk when comfortably seated' means having heard the talk when seated near, which was preceded by a friendly conversation.

Ṭhitasaṇṭhāneneva [V 2.0049] ṭhitākāreneva ṭhitaṃ, nappayuttanti attho. Cittakammamūlādīni ṭhitasaṇṭhāneneva ṭhitāti yojanā. Mudutāyāti muduhadayatāya sāpekkhatāya.

'By its very state of being established' means it is established by its very mode of being established; the meaning is that it is not applied. The application is that the roots of painted works, etc., are established by their very state of being established. 'By softness' means by softness of heart, by concern.

Paṭipadanti samathavipassanāpaṭipattiṃ. Dīpetvā pakāsetvā pākaṭaṃ katvā. Tatruppādoti tatra uppajjanakaāyuppādo. Khettaṃ sandhāya vadati. Tela…pe… hatthāti telaghaṭa-madhughaṭaphāṇitaghaṭādihatthā. Apakkamiṃsu dubbicāritattā. Pūresīti heṭṭhā ca tattha tattha atītāni kālavacanāni porāṇaṭṭhakathāya āgatattā kira vuttāni.

'The path' means the practice of serenity and insight. Having explained, illuminated, and made manifest. 'Arising there' means the arising of a lifespan that arises there. He says this in reference to a field. 'With oil... in hand' means with hands holding pots of oil, pots of honey, pots of molasses, etc. 'They departed' because of their misconduct. 'He filled': it is said that here and there below, past tense verbs are used because they have come from the ancient commentaries.

Upagamanānupagamanādīni orimassa pārimassa ca upagamanānupagamanāni ceva majjhe saṃsīdanāni ca. Paṭivijjhitunti jānituṃ. Etanti yathāvuttaṃ cakkhusabhāvaṃ. Domanassanti tasseva mandabhāvapaccayaṃ domanassaṃ. Āpajjantopi upagacchati nāma tannimittasaṃkilesassa uppāditattā. Tiṇṇaṃ lakkhaṇānanti hutvā abhāvato ādiantavantato tāvakālikato niccapaṭikkhepatotiādīnaṃ tiṇṇaṃ lakkhaṇānaṃ sallakkhaṇavasena.

'Approaching, not approaching, etc.' means the approaching and not approaching of the near shore and the far shore, and sinking in the middle. 'To penetrate' means to know. 'This' means the nature of the eye as stated. 'Grief' means the grief conditioned by its weak state. Even when falling into it, he is said to 'approach' because he has generated the defilement connected with that sign. 'Of the three characteristics' means by way of discerning the three characteristics, for reasons such as: ceasing after having come to be, having a beginning and an end, being temporary, and being the opposite of the permanent.

Vāmatoti micchā. Dakkhiṇatoti sammā. Yathā tassa tassa sattassa orabhāvattā ajjhattikāni āyatanāni orimaṃ tīraṃ katvā vuttāni, evaṃ nesaṃ parabhāvattā bāhirāni āyatanāni pārimaṃ vuttāni. Apāyamajjhe saṃsaraṇahetutāya nandīrāgova “majjhe saṃsādo”ti vutto.

'From the left' means wrongly. 'From the right' means rightly. Just as for each being, because they are on this side, the internal sense bases are called the near shore, so too, because they are on the other side, the external sense bases are called the far shore. Because it is a cause for transmigrating in the midst of the plane of misery, delight and lust is called 'sinking in the middle.'

Unnatoti “seyyohamasmī”tiādinā unnatiṃ upagato. Attukkaṃsane paṃsukūlikabhāvena attānaṃ dahanato aññākāratāgahetabbo. Pāsāṇo nu kho esa khāṇukoti gahetabbadārukkhandhasadiso vutto.

'Conceited' means one who has become haughty by thinking, 'I am better,' etc. In self-exaltation, he should be understood as having a different character, since he scorches himself with his status as a refuse-rag wearer. He is said to be like a log of wood that is to be taken as 'Is this a rock or a stump?'

Cuṇṇavicuṇṇaṃ [M 2.0334] hoti āvaṭṭavegassa balavabhāvato. Catūsu apāyesūti pañcakāmaguṇāvaṭṭe patitapuggalo manussalokepi guṇasarīrabhedanena dīgharattaṃ cuṇṇavicuṇṇaṃ āpajjatiyeva, tassa tathā āpannattā. Evañhi so apāyesu tādisesu jāyati.

It becomes crushed to powder because of the powerful force of the whirlpool. `In the four planes of misery`: a person who has fallen into the whirlpool of the five strands of sensual pleasure, even in the human world, is for a long time crushed to powder by the destruction of his virtue and his body, because he has fallen in that way. For thus he is born in such planes of misery.

Sīlassa duṭṭhaṃ nāma natthi, tasmā abhāvattho idha du-saddoti āha “nissīlo”ti. “Pāpaṃ pāpena sukara”ntiādīsu (udā. 48) viya pāpa-saddo nihīnapariyāyoti āha “lāmakadhammo”ti. Na sucīti kāyavācācittehi na suci. Saṅkāya vāti attano vā saṅkāya [V 2.0050] paresaṃ samācārakiriyaṃ sarati āsaṅkati. Tenāha “tassa hī”tiādi. Tāni kammāni pavisatīti tāni kammāni karontānaṃ antare pavisati. Guṇānaṃ pūtibhāvenāti guṇabhāvena gahitānaṃ sīladhammānaṃ saṃkiliṭṭhabhāvappattiyā. Kacavarajātoti abbhantaraṃ sañjātakacavaro, kacavarabhūto vā.

Virtue is not something that can be 'bad'; therefore the prefix `dus-` here has the sense of absence, hence it means `nissīlo` (unvirtuous). Just as in 'Evil is easy for the evil,' etc. (Ud 5.5), the word `pāpa` is a synonym for 'base,' thus he said `lāmakadhammo` (of base nature). `Not pure` means not pure in body, speech, and mind. `Or with suspicion` means he is suspicious and doubtful about his own or others' conduct. Therefore it is said: 'For him...,' etc. `He enters into those deeds` means he enters in among those who do those deeds. `Through the putridity of his qualities` means through the attainment of a defiled state by the virtuous qualities that were taken to be good qualities. `Become rubbish` means that rubbish has arisen within him, or he has become rubbish.

Aṇaṇā pabbajjāti aṇaṇasseva pabbajjā. Orimatīrādīnaṃ upagamanānupagamanādīnaṃ jotitattā vaṭṭavivaṭṭaṃ kathitaṃ.

`Going forth is for the debt-free` means that going forth is only for one who is free from debt. Because the approaching and not approaching, etc., of the near shore, etc., has been illuminated, the round and that which is not the round have been spoken of.

Paṭhamadārukkhandhopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Discourse on the Simile of the Log is finished.

5. Dutiyadārukkhandhopamasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Second Discourse on the Simile of the Log

242. Yā āpatti vuṭṭhānagāminī desanāgāminī, taṃ paṭicchāditakālato paṭṭhāya saṃkiliṭṭhā nāma antarāyikabhāvato. Āvīkatā pana anāpattiṭṭhāne tiṭṭhatīti asaṃkiliṭṭhā nāma. Tenāha – “āvīkatā hissa phāsu hotī”ti. Evarūpaṃ saṃkiliṭṭhanti paṭicchāditatāya vā duṭṭhullabhāvena vā saṃkiliṭṭhaṃ.

242. That offense which leads to rehabilitation or confession, from the time it is concealed, is called 'defiled' because of its obstructive nature. However, when disclosed, it stands in the state of being no offense and is thus called 'undefiled.' Therefore, it is said: 'For him who discloses it, there is ease.' Such a state is called 'defiled' either due to its concealment or due to its gross nature.

Dutiyadārukkhandhopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Second Discourse on the Simile of the Log is concluded.

6. Avassutapariyāyasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Discourse on the Infiltrations

243. Santhāgāranti saññāpanāgāraṃ. Tenāha “uyyogakālādīsū”tiādi. Ādi-saddena maṅgalamahādīnaṃ saṅgaho daṭṭhabbo. Santharantīti vissamanti[M 2.0335], parissamaṃ vinodentīti attho. Sahāti sannipātavasena ekajjhaṃ. Saha atthānusāsanaṃ agāranti tasmiṃ atthe ttha-kārassa ntha-kāraṃ katvā “santhāgāra”nti vuccatīti daṭṭhabbaṃ, paṭhamaṃ tattha sammantanavasena santharanti vicārentīti attho.

243. “Santhāgāra” means a council hall, a place for proclamations. Hence, it is said, “at the time of commencing activities, etc.” By the word “etc.,” gatherings for auspicious celebrations and great festivals should be understood. “Santharanti” means they rest, dispelling fatigue—this is the meaning. “Saha” means together, in the sense of gathering in one place. “Saha atthānusāsanaṃ agāra” means a hall for instruction on matters of importance; it should be understood that it is called “santhāgāra” by changing the ‘ttha’ to ‘ntha’ in that word. The meaning is that they first deliberate and discuss there by way of mutual consultation.

Tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ āgatameva bhavissatīti buddhavacanassa āgamanasīsena ariyaphaladhammānampi āgamanaṃ vuttameva. Tiyāmarattiṃ tattha vasantānaṃ phalasamāpattivaḷañjanaṃ hotīti. Tasmiñca bhikkhusaṅghe kalyāṇadhammā kalyāṇaputhujjanā vipassanaṃ ussukkāpentā hontīti ariyamaggadhammānampi tattha āgamanaṃ hotiyeva.

The Tipiṭaka, the word of the Buddha, has indeed come. By the coming of the word of the Buddha, the coming of the noble fruit-dharmas is also stated. For those dwelling there for the three watches of the night, the practice of fruition attainment occurs. And in that community of monks, those of good qualities and good ordinary people, keenly striving for insight, thus, the noble path-dharmas also come there.

Allagomayenāti [V 2.0051] acchena allagomayarasena. Opuñjāpetvāti vilimpetvā. Catujjātiyagandhehīti kuṅkumaturukkhayavanapupphatamālapattagandhehi. Nānāvaṇṇeti nīlādivasena nānāvaṇṇe, bhittivisesavasena nānāsaṇṭhānarūpe. “Mahāpiṭṭhikakojaveti hatthipiṭṭhiādīsu attharitabbatāya ‘mahāpiṭṭhikā’ti laddhasamaññe kojave”ti vadanti. Kuttake pana sandhāyetaṃ vuttaṃ, “caturaṅgulādhikapupphā mahāpiṭṭhikakojavā”tipi vadanti. Hatthattharaassattharā hatthiassapiṭṭhīsu attharitabbā hatthiassarūpavicittā ca attharakā. Sīhattharakādayo pana sīharūpādivicittā eva attharakā. Cittattharakaṃ nānāvidharūpehi ceva nānāvidhamālākammādīhi ca vicittaṃ attharakaṃ.

“Allagomayena” means with clear fresh cow-dung juice. “Opuñjāpetvā” means smearing. “Catujjātiyagandhehi” means with the scents of saffron, frankincense, jasmine flowers, and tamāla leaves. “Nānāvaṇṇe” means of various colors such as blue, etc., and of various shapes according to the different wall designs. “Mahāpiṭṭhikakojave” refers to large rugs spread on elephant backs, etc., hence called “mahāpiṭṭhikā”—a designation for rugs. However, this is said with reference to cushions, stating, “Cushions with flowers exceeding four fingers are called mahāpiṭṭhikakojavā.” Elephant and horse spreads are spreads to be spread on the backs of elephants and horses, and also patterned spreads with designs of elephants and horses. Lion spreads, etc., are spreads patterned with designs of lions and the like. A patterned spread is a spread patterned with various designs and various kinds of floral work, etc.

Upadhānanti apassayanaṃ. Upadahitvāti apassayayoggabhāvena ṭhapetvā. Gandhehi katamālā gandhadāmaṃ. Tamālapattādīhi katamālā pattadāmaṃ. Ādi-saddena hiṅgulatakkolajātiphalajātipupphādīhi katadāmaṃ saṅgaṇhāti. Pallaṅkākārena katapīṭhaṃ pallaṅkapīṭhaṃ. Tīsu passesu ekapasse eva vā saupassayaṃ apassayapīṭhaṃ. Anapassayaṃ muṇḍapīṭhaṃ. Yojanāvaṭṭeti yojanaparikkhepokāse.

“Upadhāna” means a support. “Upadahitvā” means having placed it in a way suitable for support. A garland made of fragrant substances is called a “gandhadāma.” A garland made of tamāla leaves and other materials is called a “pattadāma.” The word “ādi” includes garlands made of asafoetida, cubebs, nutmeg fruits, nutmeg flowers, and so on. A seat fashioned in the shape of a couch is called a “pallaṅkapīṭha.” A seat with a support on three sides or even on one side is called an “apassayapīṭha.” A seat without a support is called a “muṇḍapīṭha.” “Yojanāvaṭṭa” refers to a space within a yojana in circumference.

Saṃvidhāyāti antaravāsakassa koṇapadesaṃ itarapadesañca samaṃ katvā vidhāya. Tenāha – “kattariyā padumaṃ kantento viyā”ti, “timaṇḍalaṃ paṭicchādento”ti ca. Yasmā buddhānaṃ rūpasampadā viya ākappasampadāpi paramukkaṃsataṃ gatā, tasmā tadā bhagavā evaṃ sobheyyāti dassento “suvaṇṇapāmaṅgenā”tiādimāha. Tattha “asamena buddhavesenā”tiādinā [M 2.0336] tadā bhagavā buddhānubhāvassa niguhane kāraṇābhāvato tattha sannipatitadevamanussanāgayakkhagandhabbādīnaṃ pasādajananatthaṃ attano sabhāvapakatiyāva kapilavatthuṃ pāvisīti dasseti. Buddhānaṃ kāyapabhā nāma pakatiyā asītihatthamattapadesaṃ visaratīti āha “asītihatthaṭṭhānaṃ aggahesī”ti. Nīlapītalohitodātamañjiṭṭhapabhassarānaṃ vasena chabbaṇṇā buddharasmiyo.

“Saṃvidhāya” means arranging and making even the corner part of the inner robe and the other part. Hence it is said: “as if carving a lotus with a knife,” and “covering the three circles.” Because the Buddha’s physical perfection, like his perfection of deportment, reaches the highest excellence, therefore, to show how splendid the Blessed One appeared at that time, he said, “with golden radiance,” and so on. Here, the phrase “with the unequaled Buddha’s appearance” shows that since the Blessed One had no reason to conceal the Buddha’s power, he entered Kapilavatthu in his own natural state to inspire faith in the assembled devas, humans, nāgas, yakkhas, gandhabbas, and others. It is said that the Buddha’s body radiance naturally extends to an area of eighty cubits, as stated: “he occupied eighty cubits.” The Buddha’s rays are of six colors: blue, yellow, red, white, crimson, and luminous.

Sabbapāliphulloti mūlato paṭṭhāya yāva sākhaggā samantato phullo vikasito. Paṭipāṭiyā ṭhapitānantiādi parikappūpamā, yathā taṃ…pe… alaṅkataṃ aññaṃ virocati, evaṃ virocittha, samatiṃsāya pāramitāhi abhisaṅkhatattā evaṃ virocitthāti vuttaṃ hoti. “Pañcavīsatiyā gaṅgānanti satamukhā hutvā samuddaṃ paviṭṭhāya mahāgaṅgāya mahantamahantānaṃ gaṅgānaṃ pañcavīsatī”ti vadanti. Papañcasūdaniyaṃ (ma. ni. aṭṭha. 2.22) “pañcavīsatiyā nadīna”nti vuttaṃ, gaṅgādīnaṃ candabhāgāpariyosānānaṃ pañcavīsatiyā mahānadīnanti attho. Parikappavacanañhetaṃ. Sambhijjāti sambhedaṃ missībhāvaṃ patvā mukhadvāreti samuddaṃ paviṭṭhaṭṭhāne.

“Sabbapāliphullo” means fully opened and spread out from the roots to the tips of the branches all around. The phrase “arranged in order,” and so on, is a simile of imagination, just as… a decorated object shines, so it shone; because it was fashioned by the thirty perfections, it is said to have shone thus. They say, “twenty-five Ganges rivers,” meaning twenty-five of the great Ganges rivers, each having a hundred mouths, enter the ocean. In the Papañcasūdanī (Majjhima Nikāya Aṭṭhakathā 2.22), it is said: “twenty-five rivers,” meaning the twenty-five great rivers from the Ganges up to and including the Candabhāgā. This is an imaginative statement. “Sambhijjāti” means having attained a state of breaking up and mixing, at the mouth-door, at the place of entering the ocean.

Nāgasupaṇṇagandhabbayakkhādīnantiādi [V 2.0052] parikappavasena vuttaṃ. Sahassenāti padasahassena, bhāṇavārappamāṇena ganthenāti attho.

The phrase “nāgas, supaṇṇas, gandhabbas, yakkhas, and others” is said by way of imagination. “Sahassena” means by a thousand words, a text of recitation length—this is the meaning.

Kampayanto vasundharanti attano guṇavisesehi pathavīkammaṃ unnādento, evaṃbhūtopi aheṭhayanto pāṇāni. Sabbapadakkhiṇattā buddhānaṃ dakkhiṇaṃ paṭhamaṃ pādaṃ uddharanto. Samaṃ samphusate bhūmiṃ suppatiṭṭhitapādatāya. Yadipi bhūmiṃ samaṃ phusati, rajasā nupalippati sukhumattā chaviyā. Ninnaṭṭhānaṃ unnamatītiādi buddhānaṃ suppatiṭṭhitapādatāsaṅkhātamahāpurisalakkhaṇapaṭilābhassa nissandaphalaṃ. Nātidūre uddharatīti atidūre ṭhapetuṃ na uddharati. Naccāsanne ca nikkhipanti accāsanne ca ṭhāne anikkhipanto niyyāti. Hāsayanto sadevake loke tosento. Catūhi pādehi caratīti catucārī.

“Kampayanto vasundharaṃ” means making the earth resound with his own special qualities, yet he harms no living beings. Because all his movements are clockwise, the Buddha lifts his right foot first. He touches the ground evenly, because his feet are well-established. Although he touches the ground evenly, dust does not adhere to him due to the fineness of his skin. The rising of low places, etc., is the consequential result of the Buddha’s acquisition of the mark of a great man known as well-established feet. He does not lift his foot too far, to place it at too great a distance. And he does not place it too close, nor does he go forth placing it in an overly near position. Gladdening the world together with its gods, he brings joy. He walks with four feet—he is a quadruped.

Buddhānubhāvassa pakāsanavasena gatattā vaṇṇakālo nāma esa. Sarīravaṇṇe vā guṇavaṇṇe vā kathiyamāne dukkathitanti na vattabbaṃ. Kasmā? Appamāṇavaṇṇā hi buddhā bhagavanto, buddhaguṇasaṃvaṇṇanā jānantassa yathāraddhasaṃvaṇṇanaṃyeva [M 2.0337] anupavisati. Dukūlacumbaṭakenāti ganthitvā gahitadukūlavatthena.

This is called the time of praise because it is the occasion to proclaim the Buddha’s power. When describing the Buddha’s physical appearance or virtues, it should not be called ill-spoken. Why? The Blessed Buddhas are of immeasurable praise, and for one who understands the praise of the Buddha’s qualities, the description enters into a fitting account according to what was begun. “Dukūlacumbaṭakena” means with a fine cloth tied up and taken.

Nāgavikkantacāraṇoti hatthināgasadisapadanikkhepo. Satapuññalakkhaṇoti anekasatapuññābhinibbattamahāpurisalakkhaṇo. Maṇiverocano yathāti caturāsītisahassamaṇiparivārito ativiya virocamāno vijjotamāno maṇi viya. “Verocano nāma eko maṇī”ti keci. Mahāsālovāti mahanto sālarukkho viya, suddhaṭṭhito koviḷārādi mahārukkho viya vā. Padumo kokanado yathāti kokanadasaṅkhātaṃ mahāpadumaṃ viya, vikasamānapadumaṃ viya vā.

“Nāgavikkantacāraṇa” means the placing of the feet similar to that of a royal elephant. “Satapuññalakkhaṇa” means having the marks of a great man, produced by many hundreds of merits. “Maṇiverocano yathā” means just as a jewel, surrounded by eighty-four thousand jewels, shines exceedingly and is very bright. Some say, “Verocana is the name of a particular jewel.” “Mahāsālo” means like a great sāl tree, standing straight, or like other great trees such as the kovidāra. “Padumo kokanado yathā” means like a great lotus called kokanada, or like a blooming lotus.

Ākāsagaṅgaṃ otārento viyātiādi tassā pakiṇṇakakathāya aññesaṃ sudukkarabhāvadassanañceva suṇantānaṃ accantasukhāvahabhāvadassanañca. Pathavojaṃ ākaḍḍhanto viyāti nāḷiyantaṃ yojetvā mahāpathaviyā heṭṭhimatale pappaṭakojaṃ uddhaṃ mukhaṃ katvā ākaḍḍhanto viya. Yojanikanti yojanapamāṇaṃ. Madhubhaṇḍanti madhupaṭalaṃ.

“Ākāsagaṅgaṃ otārento viya” and so on—this miscellaneous talk of hers demonstrates both how very difficult it is for others and how it brings extreme happiness to those who listen. “Pathavojaṃ ākaḍḍhanto viya” means like one drawing up the earth’s essence, having joined the end of a tube to the great earth below, turning the essence of the crust upward, and drawing it up, as it were. “Yojanika” means a measurement of a yojana. “Madhubhaṇḍa” means a layer of honey.

Mahantanti vipulaṃ uḷārapuññaṃ. Sabbadānaṃ dinnameva hotīti sabbameva paccayajātaṃ āvāsadāyakena dinnameva hoti. Tathā hi dve tayo gāme piṇḍāya caritvā kiñci aladdhā āgatassapi [V 2.0053] chāyūdakasampannaṃ ārāmaṃ pavisitvā nhāyitvā paṭissaye muhuttaṃ nipajjitvā uṭṭhāya nisinnassa kāye balaṃ āharitvā pakkhittaṃ viya hoti. Bahi vicarantassa ca kāye vaṇṇadhātu vātātapehi kilamati, paṭissayaṃ pavisitvā dvāraṃ pidhāya muhuttaṃ nisinnassa visabhāgasantati vūpasammati, sabhāgasantati patiṭṭhāti, vaṇṇadhātu āharitvā pakkhittā viya hoti, bahi vicarantassa ca pāde kaṇṭakā vijjhanti, khāṇu paharati, sarīsapādiparissayā ceva corabhayañca uppajjati, paṭissayaṃ pavisitvā dvāraṃ pidhāya nisinnassa pana sabbepete parissayā na honti. Sajjhāyantassa dhammapītisukhaṃ, kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikarontassa upasamasukhaṃ uppajjati bahiddhā vikkhepābhāvato. Bahi vicarantassa ca kāye sedā muccanti, akkhīni phandanti, senāsanaṃ pavisanakkhaṇe mañcapīṭhādīni na paññāyanti, muhuttaṃ nisinnassa pana akkhipasādo āharitvā pakkhitto viya hoti, dvāravātapānamañcapīṭhādīni paññāyanti. Etasmiñca āvāse vasantaṃ disvā manussā catūhi paccayehi sakkaccaṃ upaṭṭhahanti. Tena vuttaṃ – “āvāsadānasmiñhi dinne sabbadānaṃ dinnameva hotī”ti. Bhūmaṭṭhaka…pe… na [M 2.0338] sakkāti ayamattho mahāsudassanavatthunā (dī. ni. 2.241 ādayo) dīpetabbo. Mātukucchi asambādhova hotīti ayamattho antimabhavikānaṃ mahābodhisattānaṃ paṭisandhivasena dīpetabbo.

Great means abundant, sublime merit. By ‘all is given’ is meant that the entire set of requisites is given by the donor of a dwelling. For when one has wandered for alms in two or three villages and gotten nothing, if he enters a monastery endowed with shade and water, bathes, lies down for a moment in his lodging, and then gets up and sits down, it is as if strength has been infused into his body. While wandering outside, the element of complexion in the body is afflicted by wind and sun; but when one enters a lodging, closes the door, and sits for a moment, the unsuitable continuity subsides, a suitable continuity is established, and it is as if the element of complexion has been infused. While wandering outside, thorns pierce one’s feet, one strikes against stumps, and dangers from reptiles and other creatures and the fear of thieves arise; but for one who enters a lodging, closes the door, and sits down, none of these dangers exist. For one reciting, the joy and happiness of the Dhamma arise; for one attending to a meditation subject, the happiness of peace arises, owing to the absence of external distraction. While wandering outside, sweat is emitted from the body and the eyes flicker, and on the occasion of entering the lodging, the bed and chair are not discerned; but after one has sat for a moment, it is as if clarity has been infused into the eye-faculty, and the doors, windows, beds, and chairs are discerned. Seeing him dwelling in this monastery, people attend on him respectfully with the four requisites. Therefore it is said: ‘When a dwelling is given, all is given.’ This point—‘On the ground floor… not possible’—should be illustrated by the story of Mahāsudassana (Dī. Ni. 2.241ff). This point—‘The mother’s womb is not constricted’—should be illustrated by way of the conception of the great Bodhisattas in their final existence.

Sītanti ajjhattaṃ dhātukkhobhavasena vā bahiddhā utuvipariṇāmavasena vā uppajjanakasītaṃ. Uṇhanti aggisantāpaṃ, tassa ca davadāhādīsu sambhavo daṭṭhabbo. Paṭihantīti paṭihanati. Yathā tadubhayavasena kāyacittānaṃ ābādho na hanati, evaṃ karoti. Sītuṇhabbhāhate hi sarīre vikkhittacitto bhikkhu yoniso padahituṃ na sakkoti. Vāḷamigānīti sīhabyagghādivāḷamige. Guttasenāsanañhi pavisitvā dvāraṃ pidhāya nisinnassa te parissayā na honti. Sarīsapeti ye keci sarantā gacchante dīghajātike sappādike aññe ca tathārūpe. Makaseti nidassanamattametaṃ, ḍaṃsādīnaṃ eteneva saṅgaho daṭṭhabbo. Sisireti sītakālavasena sattāhavaddalikādivasena ca uppanne sisirasamphasse. Vuṭṭhiyoti yadā tadā uppannā vassavuṭṭhiyo paṭihanatīti yojanā.

Cold: the cold that arises either internally through a disturbance of the elements or externally through a change of season. Heat: the heat of fire. Its occurrence should be seen in such things as forest fires. Wards off means it repels. It acts in such a way that affliction of body and mind due to both does not strike. For when the body is afflicted by cold and heat, a bhikkhu with a distracted mind is unable to strive wisely. Wild beasts: beasts such as lions and tigers. For one who enters a guarded lodging, closes the door, and sits down, these dangers do not exist. Creeping things: any long-bodied creatures that go by crawling, such as snakes and other such creatures. Mosquitoes: this is just an indication; gadflies and the like should be understood as included by this. In the winter: the touch of winter that arises during the cold season, such as during the seven-day cold spell. Rain: the construction is that it wards off the showers of rain that arise from time to time.

Vātātapo ghoroti rukkhagacchādīnaṃ ubbahanabhañjanādivasena pavattiyā ghoro sarajaarajādibhedo vāto ceva gimhapariḷāhasamayesu uppattiyā ghoro sūriyātapo ca. Paṭihaññatīti paṭibāhīyati. Leṇatthanti nānārammaṇato cittaṃ nivattitvā paṭisallāṇārāmatthaṃ. Sukhatthanti vuttaparissayābhāvena phāsuvihāratthaṃ. Jhāyitunti aṭṭhatiṃsāya ārammaṇesu yattha katthaci [V 2.0054] cittaṃ upanibandhitvā samādahanavasena jhāyituṃ. Vipassitunti aniccādivasena saṅkhāre sammasituṃ.

Fierce wind and sun: the wind that is fierce because it proceeds by way of uprooting and breaking trees and shrubs, and is of the kind with dust, without dust, etc.; and the sun’s heat that is fierce because it arises during the time of summer’s fever. Are warded off means are kept away. For the sake of a refuge: for the purpose of taking delight in seclusion after turning the mind away from various objects. For the sake of comfort: for the purpose of a comfortable abiding, due to the absence of the dangers mentioned. To practice jhāna: to practice jhāna by way of concentrating the mind, having bound it to any of the thirty-eight objects. To see insight: to comprehend formations as impermanent, etc.

Vihāreti paṭissaye. Kārayeti kārāpeyya. Rammeti manorame. Vāsayettha bahussuteti kāretvā pana ettha vihāresu bahussute sīlavante kalyāṇadhamme nivāseyya. Te nivāsento pana tesaṃ bahussutānaṃ yathā paccayehi kilamatho na hoti. Evaṃ annañca pānañca vatthasenāsanāni ca dadeyya ujubhūtesu ajjhāsayasampannesu kammaphalānaṃ ratanattayaguṇānañca saddahanena vippasannena cetasā. Idāni gahaṭṭhapabbajitānaṃ aññamaññūpakārataṃ dassetuṃ “te tassā”ti gāthamāha. Tattha teti bahussutā. Tassāti upāsakassa. Dhammaṃ desentīti sakalavaṭṭadukkhapanūdanaṃ [M 2.0339] niyyānikaṃ dhammaṃ kathenti. Yaṃ so dhammaṃ idhaññāyāti so puggalo yaṃ saddhammaṃ imasmiṃ sāsane sammāpaṭipajjanena jānitvā aggamaggādhigamena anāsavo hutvā parinibbāyati.

A dwelling: a lodging. Would have built: would cause to be built. Delightful: pleasing to the mind. And would settle the learned there: having had them built, he should settle in those dwellings the learned, the virtuous, those of good qualities. And while settling them, he should act so that those learned ones are not afflicted by a lack of requisites. Thus he should give food and drink, robes and lodgings, to those who are upright, endowed with good dispositions, with a confident mind, with faith in the fruit of kamma and the qualities of the Triple Gem. Now, to show the mutual assistance between householders and the renunciant, he states the verse beginning ‘They for him.’ Therein, they means the learned ones. For him means for the lay follower. They teach him the Dhamma means they speak the Dhamma that is emancipating, the dispeller of all the suffering of the round of existence. Having understood that Dhamma here: having known that good Dhamma in this Dispensation through right practice, that person, having become taintless through the attainment of the highest path, attains final Nibbāna.

Āvāseti āvāsadāne. Ānisaṃsoti udrayo. Pūjāsakkāravasena paṭhamayāmo khepito, satthu dhammadesanāya appāvaseso majjhimayāmo gatoti pāḷiyaṃ “bahudeva ratti”nti vuttanti āha “atirekataraṃ diyaḍḍhayāma”nti. Saṅgahaṃ nārohati vipulavitthārabhāvato. Buddhānañhi bhattānumodanāpi thokaṃ vaḍḍhetvā vuccamānā dīghamajjhimapamāṇāpi hoti. Tathā hi suphusitaṃ dantāvaraṇaṃ, jivhā tanukā, bhavaṅgaparivāso paritto, natthi vegāyitaṃ, natthi vitthāyitaṃ, natthi abyāvaṭamano, sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ samupabyūḷhaṃ, aparikkhayā paṭisambhidā.

Of a dwelling: in the gift of a dwelling. Benefit: advantage. The first watch was spent in offerings and honors; the middle watch passed with little remaining for the Teacher’s Dhamma discourse. Since it is said in the Pāli, ‘much of the night,’ he says, ‘for more than a watch and a half.’ It does not amount to a compendium because of its great extent. For even the Buddhas’ thanksgiving after a meal, when spoken with a slight extension, can be of long or medium length. For the row of his teeth is well-formed, his tongue is thin, the life-continuum is brief, there is no hastiness, no diffuseness, no inattentive mind, the knowledge of omniscience is fully marshalled, and the analytical knowledges are inexhaustible.

Sandassetvātiādīsu sandassetvā āvāsadānapaṭisaṃyuttaṃ dhammiṃ kathaṃ katvā. Tato paraṃ, mahārāja, itipi sīlaṃ, itipi samādhi, itipi paññāti sīlādiguṇe tesaṃ sammā dassetvā hatthena gahetvā viya paccakkhato pakāsetvā. Samādapetvāti evaṃ sīlaṃ samādātabbaṃ, sīle patiṭṭhitena evaṃ samādhipaññā bhāvetabbāti yathā te sīlādiguṇe sammā ādiyanti, tathā gaṇhāpetvā. Samuttejetvāti yathāsamādinnaṃ sīlaṃ suvisuddhaṃ hoti, samathavipassanā ca bhāviyamānā yathā suṭṭhu visodhitā uparūpari visesāvahā honti, evaṃ samuttejetvā nisāmanavasena vodāpetvā. Sampahaṃsetvāti yathānusiṭṭhaṃ ṭhitasīlādiguṇehi sampati paṭiladdhaguṇānisaṃsehi ceva upariladdhabbaphalavisesehi ca cittaṃ sampahaṃsetvā laddhassāsavasena suṭṭhu tosetvā. Evametesaṃ padānaṃ attho veditabbo. Sakyarājāno yebhuyyena bhagavato dhammadesanāya sāsane laddhassādā laddhappatiṭṭhā ca.

In regard to having instructed, etc.: Having instructed means having given a Dhamma talk connected with the gift of a dwelling. Then, ‘Great king, such is virtue, such is concentration, such is wisdom’—having thus properly shown them the qualities of virtue, etc., and having revealed them directly as if taking them by the hand. Having roused: having made them grasp the qualities of virtue, etc., properly, so that they might think: ‘Thus should virtue be undertaken; being established in virtue, thus should concentration and wisdom be developed.’ Having incited: having incited them by way of sharpening, that is, by cleansing them, so that the virtue they have undertaken becomes well purified, and so that serenity and insight, being developed, become well cleansed and lead to successively higher distinctions. Having gladdened: having gladdened their minds with the benefits of the qualities of virtue, etc., in which they are established as instructed, with the benefits of the qualities now attained, and with the special fruits to be attained in the future; having pleased them well by way of the confidence they have gained. Thus the meaning of these terms should be understood. The Sakyan princes, for the most part, gained a taste for and a footing in the Blessed One’s Dispensation through his Dhamma teaching.

Upasaggasaddānaṃ [V 2.0055] anekatthattā abhi-saddo ati-saddena samānatthopi hotīti vuttaṃ “abhikkantāti atikkantā”ti.

Because the words that are prefixes have many meanings, it is said that the word abhi- can also have the same meaning as the word ati-, thus: ‘Excellent means surpassed.’

Tatra kirātiādi kecivādoti baddhopi na hoti. Tenāha “akāraṇameta”ntiādi. Kāyacittalahutādayo uppajjantīti idaṃ kāyikacetasikaaññathābhāvassa kāraṇavacanaṃ, lahutādiuppanne savanānuttariyapaṭilābhena laddhabbadhammatthavedasamadhigamato. Vuttañhetaṃ –

The view of some, beginning 'It is said that...', is not even established. Therefore he said, ‘This is not the reason,’ etc. ‘Lightness of body and mind, etc., arise’: this is the reason for the alteration of bodily and mental states, because when lightness, etc., have arisen, one acquires the meaning of the Dhamma and the experience of the Dhamma through the attainment of the excellence of hearing. For this has been said:

“Yathā[M 2.0340], yathāvuso, bhikkhuno satthā vā dhammaṃ deseti aññataro vā garuṭṭhāniko sabrahmacārī, tathā tathā so tasmiṃ dhamme labhati atthavedaṃ, labhati dhammavedaṃ, labhati dhammūpasaṃhitaṃ pāmojja”ntiādi (dī. ni. 3.322, 355; a. ni. 5.26).

‘Friend, in whatever way the Teacher teaches the Dhamma to a bhikkhu, or some other fellow monastic in the position of a teacher does so, in that same way he gains an experience of the meaning in that Dhamma, he gains an experience of the Dhamma, he gains the joy connected with the Dhamma,’ etc. (D III 222, 255; AN 5.26).

Piṭṭhivāto uppajji upādinnasarīrassa tathārūpattā saṅkhārānañca aniccatāya dukkhānubandhattā. Akāraṇaṃ vā etanti yenādhippāyena vuttaṃ, tameva adhippāyaṃ vivarituṃ “pahotī”tiādi vuttaṃ. Ekapallaṅkena nisīdituṃ pahoti yathā taṃ veluvagāmake. Ettake ṭhāneti ettake ṭhāne ṭhānaṃ nipphannanti yojanā. Tañca khoti vuṭṭhānasaññaṃ citte ṭhapanaṃ. Dhammakathaṃ suṇamāno dhammagāravena.

A pain in the back arose because of the nature of the grasped body and because formations are impermanent and followed by suffering. Or, ‘This is not the reason’: this was said with a certain intention, and to explain that same intention, ‘he is able,’ etc., was said. He is able to sit in a single cross-legged posture, as at Veluvagāmaka. At just that place: the construction is, ‘the sitting was accomplished at just that place.’ And that is indeed the establishing in the mind of the perception of rising, while listening to the Dhamma talk out of reverence for the Dhamma.

Avassutassāti avassutabhāvassa rāgādivasena. Avassutassa kāraṇanti tintabhāvakāraṇaṃ. Kilesādhimuccanenāti kilesavasena paripphanditavasena. Nibbāpanaṃ viyāti vūpasamo viya. Nibbisevanānanti paripphandanarahitānaṃ.

Of one who is corrupt: because of the state of being corrupt through lust, etc. The cause of corruption means the cause of being stained. By being intent on the defilements means through the defilements, through agitation. Like an extinguishing means like a calming. Of those who are unagitated means of those devoid of agitation.

Avassutapariyāyasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Discourse on the Corrupt Person is concluded.

7. Dukkhadhammasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Explanation of the Discourse on States Pertaining to Suffering

244. Dukkhadhammānanti dukkhakāraṇānaṃ. Tenāha “dukkhasambhavadhammāna”ntiādi. Tattha kiṃ dukkhaṃ, kā dukkhadhammāti tadubhayaṃ dassetuṃ “pañcasu hī”ti vuttaṃ. Teti pañcakkhandhā. Dukkhasambhavadhammattāti dukkhuppattikāraṇattā. Assāti tena. Karaṇe hetaṃ sāmivacanaṃ. Kāmeti vatthukāme kilesakāme ca. Puna assāti sāmiatthe eva sāmivacanaṃ. Cāranti cittācāraṃ. Vihāranti pañcadvārappavatticāravihāraṃ. “Ekaṭṭhā”ti ca vadanti. Teneva hi “anubandhitvā carantaṃ”icceva vuttaṃ. Anubandhitvāti ca vīthicittappavattito paṭṭhāya yāva tatiyajavanavārā anu [V 2.0056] anu bandhitvā. Pakkhandanādīti ādi-saddena kasigorakkhādivasenapi kāmānaṃ pariyesanadukkhaṃ saṅgaṇhāti.

244. Of states pertaining to suffering means of the causes of suffering. Therefore it is said, ‘of states that are the origin of suffering,’ and so on. Here, to show both what suffering is and what the states of suffering are, it is said, ‘in the five.’ Those are the five aggregates. Because they are states that are the origin of suffering means because they are the cause for the arising of suffering. ‘Assā’ means by that. This is a genitive form used in the sense of an instrument. ‘Kāmeti’ refers to both sensual pleasures as objects and sensual desire as a defilement. Again, ‘assā’ is a genitive form, indeed in the sense of ownership. ‘Cāra’ means the movement of the mind. ‘Vihāra’ means the movement and dwelling occurring through the five sense doors. And some say they have one meaning. For that very reason it is said, ‘following closely.’ And ‘following closely’ means from the arising of the cognitive series up to the third impulsion-moment, following again and again. ‘Pakkhandanādi’—the word ‘ādi’ includes the suffering of seeking sensual pleasures through activities such as farming and cattle-herding.

Dāyatīti [M 2.0341] dāyo, vanaṃ. Tenāha “aṭavi”nti. Kaṇṭakagabbhanti ovarakasadisaṃ vanaṃ. Nāmapadaṃ nāma kiriyāpadāpekkhanti “vijjhī”ti vacanasesena kiriyāpadaṃ gaṇhāti.

“Dāya” means a forest. Hence it is said, “aṭavi” (forest). “Kaṇṭakagabbha” means a forest like a covered or enclosed space. A noun depends on a verb; thus, the verb 'vijjhī' (he pierced) is supplied to complete the sentence.

Dandhāyitattaṃ uppannakilesānaṃ avaṭṭhānaṃ. Tenāha “uppannamattāyā”tiādi. Tāyāti satiyā. Kāci kilesāti cuddasavidhe cittassa kicce javanakicce eva cittakilesānaṃ uppattiṃ katvā tathā vuttaṃ. Niggahitāva honti pavattituṃ appadānavasena. Tenāha “na saṇṭhātuṃ sakkontī”ti. Cakkhudvārasminti pāḷiyaṃ tassa paṭhamaṃ gahitatāya vuttaṃ, tena nayena sesadvārānipi gahitāneva honti. Rāgādīsu uppannesu paṭhamajavanavāre. Satisammosena “kilesā me uppannā”ti ñatvā tathā paccāmāsasatiyā labbhanato. Tenāha – “anacchariyaṃ ceta”nti. Āvaṭṭetvāti ayoniso āvaṭṭetvā. Āvajjanādīsūti tato eva ayoniso āvajjanādīsu uppannesu iṭṭhārammaṇassa laddhattā paccayasiddhiyā sampattaṃ pavattanārahaṃ. Nivattetvāti dutiyajavanavārepi kilesuppattiṃ nivattetvā. Kathaṃ panassa evaṃ laddhuṃ sakkāti āha “āraddhavipassakānaṃ hī”tiādi. Bhāvanāpaṭisaṅkhāneti bhāvanāyaṃ paṭisaṅkhāne ca yogino patiṭṭhitabhāvo. Tassa ayamānisaṃso – yaṃ paccayalābhena uppajjituṃ laddhokāsāpi kilesā pubbe pavattabhāvanānubhāvena vikkhambhitā tathā tathā niggahitā eva hutvā nivattanti, kusalā dhammāva laddhokāsā uparūpari vaḍḍhanti.

“Dandhāyitatta” is the non-persistence of arisen defilements. Hence it is said, “uppannamattāya,” and so on. “Tāya” means by mindfulness. “Kāci kilesā” (some defilements) is stated thus because it refers to the arising of mental defilements specifically in the javana function, among the fourteen types of mental functions. They are indeed suppressed from persisting due to lack of opportunity. Hence it is said, “they are unable to persist.” “At the eye-door” is mentioned in the Pāli because it is the first to be grasped; in this way, the other sense-doors are also included. When lust, etc., arise in the first javana moment, despite a lapse of mindfulness, by knowing that “defilements have arisen in me,” and then obtaining mindfulness that reflects in such a way, it is said: “this is no wonder.” “Āvaṭṭetvā” means having adverted unwisely. “Āvajjanādīsu” (in attention, etc.) means that when unwise attention, etc., arise from that very thing, because the desired object is obtained, it becomes fit to proceed, having reached the fulfillment of conditions. “Nivattetvā” means turning back the arising of defilements even in the second javana moment. But how is it possible for this to be achieved? It is said: “for those who have undertaken insight,” and so on. “Bhāvanāpaṭisaṅkhāne” means the meditator is established in development and in reflection.
