This is the benefit for them: even though defilements gain the opportunity to arise due to the obtaining of conditions, they are suppressed by the power of prior development, restrained in various ways, and thus turn back; wholesome states alone gain the opportunity and progressively increase.

Abhihaṭṭhunti abhiharitvā. Anudahantīti anudahantā viya honti. Anusentīti etthāpi eseva nayo. Anāvaṭṭanteti anivattante sāmaññatoti adhippāyo. Vipassanābalameva dīpitaṃ maggaphalādhigamassa ajotitattā.

“Abhihaṭṭhuṃ” means having brought forward. “Anudahanti” means they are like those who burn in accordance. “Anusenti”—here too, the same method applies. “Anāvaṭṭante” means not turning back from the state of being a recluse is the meaning. The power of insight alone is illuminated, because the attainment of the path and fruit is not shown.

Dukkhadhammasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Discourse on States Pertaining to Suffering is concluded.

8. Kiṃsukopamasuttavaṇṇanā

8. Explanation of the Discourse on the Simile of the Kiṃsuka Tree

245. Catunnaṃ ariyasaccānaṃ pariññābhisamayādivasena vividhadassananti kiccavasena nānādassanaṃ hotīti vuttaṃ, “dassananti paṭhamamaggassetaṃ adhivacana”nti[M 2.0342]. Tayidaṃ uparimaggesu bhāvanāpariyāyassa niruḷhattā paṭhamamaggassa paṭhamaṃ nibbānadassanato. Tenāha “paṭhamamaggo hī”tiādi[V 2.0057]. Koci yathāvuttaṃ aviparītaṃ atthaṃ ajānanto ñāṇadassanaṃ nāma ārammaṇakaraṇassa vasena atippasaṅgaṃ āsaṅkeyyāti taṃ nivattetuṃ “gotrabhū panā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Na dassananti vuccatīti ettha rājadassanaṃ udāharanti. Cattāropi maggā dassanameva yathāvuttenatthena, bhāvanāpariyāyo pana upari tiṇṇaṃ maggānaṃ paṭhamamaggaupāyassa bhāvanākārena pavattanato. Dassanaṃ visuddhi etthāti dassanavisuddhikaṃ, nibbānaṃ. Phassāyatanaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ assa atthīti phassāyatanakammaṭṭhāniko. Esa nayo sesesupi padesu.

245. Regarding the four noble truths, it is said that there are diverse insights by way of full comprehension, realization, and so on; thus, there are various insights based on their function. 'Insight' (dassana) is a designation for the first path. This is because for the higher paths the mode of cultivation is established, whereas for the first path there is the first seeing of Nibbāna. Hence, it is stated, 'For the first path...' and so on. Lest someone, not knowing the correct, unerring meaning as stated, might suspect an over-application of the term 'knowledge and insight' simply by way of taking an object, to prevent this it is said, 'But the change-of-lineage...' and so on. To show that it is 'not called insight,' they cite the example of seeing a king. All four paths are indeed insight in the sense explained, but the mode of cultivation pertains to the three higher paths because they proceed by way of cultivating the means of the first path. That wherein insight is purification is the purification of insight (dassanavisuddhikaṃ), which is Nibbāna. One for whom the base of contact is the meditation subject is a 'meditator on the base of contact' (phassāyatanakammaṭṭhāniko). This same method applies to the remaining passages.

Padesasaṅkhāresūti saṅkhārekadesesu. Heṭṭhimaparicchedena pathaviādike dhammamatte diṭṭhe rūpapariggaho, cakkhuviññāṇādike taṃsahagatadhammamatte diṭṭhe arūpapariggaho ca sijjhatīti vadanti.

'In partial formations' (padesasaṅkhāresu) means in parts of the formations. They say that with the lower delimitation, the comprehension of form is accomplished when mere phenomena such as the earth element are seen; and the comprehension of the formless is accomplished when mere phenomena such as eye-consciousness and the states associated with it are seen.

Adhigatamaggameva kathesīti yena mukhena vipassanābhinivesaṃ akāsi, tamevassa mukhaṃ kathesi. Ayaṃ panāti kammaṭṭhānaṃ pucchanto bhikkhu. Imesanti phassāyatanakammaṭṭhānikapañcakkhandhakammaṭṭhānikānaṃ vacanaṃ. “Aññamaññaṃ na sametī”ti vatvā tamevatthaṃ pākaṭaṃ karoti “paṭhamenā”tiādinā. Pañcakkhandhavimuttassa saṅkhārassa abhāvā “nippadesesū”ti vuttaṃ. Tathevāti yatheva phassāyatanakammaṭṭhānikaṃ, tatheva taṃ pañcakkhandhakammaṭṭhānikaṃ pucchitvā.

'He spoke only of the path he had attained' means he spoke of that very approach by which he had applied himself to insight. 'This, however' (ayaṃ pana) refers to the monk questioning about the meditation subject. 'Of these' (imesaṃ) refers to the statement of the meditators on the bases of contact and the meditators on the five aggregates. Having said, 'They do not accord with each other,' he clarifies that meaning with 'by the first,' and so on. Because there is no formation that is liberated from the five aggregates, it is said, 'without a foothold' (nippadesesu). 'Similarly' (tatheva) means: just as he questioned about the meditation subject of the bases of contact, so too he questioned about the meditation subject of the five aggregates.

Samappavattā dhātuyoti rasādayo sarīradhātuyo samappavattā, na visamākārasaṇṭhitā ahesuṃ. Tenāha “kallasarīraṃ balappatta”nti. “Atītā saṅkhārā”tiādi vipassanābhinivesavasena vuttaṃ. Sammasanaṃ sabbatthakameva icchitabbaṃ. Cāribhūminti gocaraṭṭhānaṃ.

'The elements occurred evenly' (samappavattā dhātuyo) means the bodily elements, such as the humors, occurred evenly; they were not established in an uneven state. Therefore, it is said, 'a healthy body, having attained strength.' 'Past formations,' and so on, is stated by way of application to insight. Comprehension (sammasana) should be understood as being applicable everywhere. 'The ground for wandering' (cāribhūmi) means the alms-resort.

Kārakabhāvanti bhāvanānuyuñjanabhāvaṃ. Paṇḍurogapurisoti paṇḍurogī puriso. Ariṭṭhanti suttaṃ. Bhesajjaṃ katvāti bhesajjapayogaṃ katvā. Karissāmīti bhesajjaṃ karissāmi. Jhāmathuṇo viyāti daḍḍhathuṇo viya khārakajālanaddhattā taruṇamakulasantānasañchannattā.

'The state of being an agent' (kārakabhāva) is the state of being devoted to cultivation. 'A man with jaundice' (paṇḍurogapurisa) means a jaundiced man. 'Ariṭṭha' means a thread. 'Having made medicine' (bhesajjaṃ katvā) means having applied medicine. 'I will make' (karissāmi) means I will make medicine. 'Like a burnt stump' (jhāmathuṇo viya) means like a burnt pillar, because of being bound with a net of salt-crystal and covered by a series of young buds.

Dakkhiṇadvāragāmeti dakkhiṇadvārasamīpe gāme. Lohitakoti lohitavaṇṇo. Ocirakajātoti jātaolambamānacirako viya. Ādinnasipāṭikoti [M 2.0343] gahitaphalapotako. Sandacchāyoti bahalacchāyo. Yasmā tassa rukkhassa sākhā aviraḷā ghanappattā aññamaññaṃ saṃsanditvā ṭhitā, tasmā chāyāpissa tādisīti vuttaṃ “sandacchāyo nāma saṃsanditvā ṭhitacchāyo”ti, ghanacchāyoti attho. Tatthātiādi upamāsaṃsandanaṃ.

'In the southern gate village' (dakkhiṇadvāragāme) means in a village near the southern gate. 'Lohitaka' means of a red color. 'Ocirakajāta' means like a grown, hanging strip of bark. 'Ādinnasipāṭika' means a fruit-pod that has been taken. 'Dense shade' (sandacchāyo) means thick shade. Because the branches of that tree are not sparse but have thick leaves and stand intertwined with one another, its shade is also like that. Therefore it is said, 'dense shade means shade that stands intertwined,' the meaning being 'thick shade.' 'There' (tattha), and so on, is the connection of the simile.

Yena [V 2.0058] yenākārena adhimuttānanti chaphassāyatanādimukhena yena yena vipassanābhinivesena vipassantānaṃ nibbānañca adhimuttānaṃ. Suṭṭhu visuddhaṃ pariññātisamayādisiddhiyā. Tena tenevākārenāti attanādhimuttākārena. Idāni taṃ taṃ ākāraṃ upamāya saddhiṃ yojetvā dassetuṃ “yathā hī”tiādi āraddhaṃ. Taṃ suviññeyyameva.

'Of those inclined in whatever manner' (yena yenākārena adhimuttānaṃ) means: of those who are contemplating through the approach of the six contact-bases and so on, with whatever application to insight they are inclined, and who are also inclined toward Nibbāna. 'Perfectly purified' (suṭṭhu visuddhaṃ) refers to being purified by the accomplishment of full understanding, realization, and so on. 'By that very manner' (tena tenevākārena) means by the manner of one's own inclination. Now, to show that manner connected with a simile, the passage beginning 'Just as...' is introduced. That is easily understood.

Idanti nagaropamaṃ. Taṃ sallakkhitanti kiṃsukopamadīpitaṃ atthajātaṃ sace sallakkhitaṃ. Assa bhikkhuno. Dhammadesanatthanti yathāsallakkhitassa atthassa vasena laddhavisesassa upabrūhanāya. Tassevatthassāti tassa dassanavisuddhisaṅkhātassa atthassa. Corāsaṅkā na honti majjhimadesarajjassa pasannabhāvato. Tipurisubbedhānīti ubbedhena tipurisappamāṇāni nānābhittivicittāni thambhānaṃ upari vividhamālākammādivicittadhanurākāralakkhitāni manoramāni. Tenāha “nagarassa alaṅkārattha”nti. Nagaradvārassa thirabhāvāpādanavasena ṭhapetabbattā vuttaṃ “coranivāraṇatthānipi hontiyevā”ti. Piṭṭhasaṅghātassāti dvārabāhassa. “Ime āvāsikā, ime āgantukā, tatthāpi ca imehi nagarassa nagarasāmikassa ca attho. Imesaṃ vasena anattho siyā”ti jānanañāṇasaṅkhātena paṇḍiccena samannāgato. Aññātanivāraṇe paṭubhāvasaṅkhātena veyyattiyena samannāgato. Ṭhānuppattikapaññāsaṅkhātāyāti tasmiṃ tasmiṃ atthakicce taṅkhaṇuppajjanakapaṭibhānasaṅkhātāya.

'This' (idaṃ) is the simile of the city. 'If that is well marked' (taṃ sallakkhitaṃ) means: if that collection of meanings, illuminated by the simile of the kiṃsuka tree, is well marked. 'Of that monk' (assa bhikkhuno). 'For the purpose of teaching the Dhamma' (dhammadesanatthaṃ) means for the strengthening of the special attainment obtained by means of the meaning that has been well marked. 'Of that very meaning' (tassevatthassa) means of that meaning designated as the purification of view. There is no fear of thieves due to the peaceful state of the kingdom in the middle country. 'Three persons in height' (tipurisubbedhāni) means delightful structures measuring three persons in height, adorned with various walls, and marked by bow-shapes on top of pillars that are adorned with diverse garland-works and so on. Thus he said, 'for the purpose of ornamenting the city.' Because they must be placed in a way that establishes the firmness of the city gate, it is said, 'they also serve to ward off thieves.' 'Of the back-joint' (piṭṭhasaṅghātassa) means of the door-post. Endowed with the sagacity designated as the knowledge of recognizing, 'These are residents, these are visitors; by these there is benefit for the city and the city's lord, but by these others harm could arise.' Endowed with the competence designated as the skill to prevent the unknown. 'Designated as ready-witted wisdom' (ṭhānuppattikapaññāsaṅkhātāya) means designated as the readiness of mind that arises at that very moment for each and every task.

Raññā āyutto niyojito rājāyutto, tattha tattha rañño kātabbakicce ṭhapitapuriso. Katipāheyevāti katipayadivaseyeva akiccakaraṇena tassa ṭhānaṃ vibbhamo jātoti katvā vuttaṃ – “sabbāni vinicchayaṭṭhānādīni hāretvā”ti.

A king's official (rājāyutto) is one commissioned (āyutto) or appointed (niyojito) by the king; a person appointed to the king's duties in various places. 'In just a few days' (katipāheyeva) means that in just a few days, due to neglecting his duties, he fell from his position. Thus it is said: 'having removed all the places of judgment and so on.'

Sīsamassa chindāhīti sīsabhūtaṃ uttamaṅgaṭṭhāniyaṃ tattha tassa rājakiccaṃ chindāti attho. Aññathā tassa pākatike atthe gayhamāne pāṇātipāto [M 2.0344] āṇatto nāma siyā. Na hi cakkavattirājā tādisaṃ āṇāpeti, aññesampi tato nivārakattā. Atha vā chindāhīti mama āṇāya assa sīsaṃ chindanto viya attānaṃ dassehi, evaṃ so tatthovādapaṭikarattapatto odameyyāti, tathā ceva upari paṭipatti āgatā. Tatthāti etasmiṃ paccantimanagare.

'Cut off his head' means: cut off his royal duty, which is like the head, occupying the position of the highest limb. Otherwise, if its ordinary meaning were taken, it would be a command for the destruction of life. A wheel-turning monarch does not give such a command, as he prevents others from it as well. Alternatively, 'cut off' means: 'By my command, show yourself as if you are cutting off his head.' In this way, having become receptive to advice there, he would be subdued. And the subsequent procedure is given accordingly. 'There' (tattha) means in this border town.

Uppannenāti samathakammaṭṭhāne uppannena.

'Having arisen' (uppannena) means having arisen in the meditation subject of serenity.

Tassevāti sakkāyasaṅkhātassa nagarassa “dvārānī”ti vuttānīti ānetvā sambandho. “Sīghaṃ [V 2.0059] dūtayuga”nti vuttāti yojanā. Hadayavatthurūpassa majjhe siṅghāṭakabhāvena gahitattā “hadayavatthussa nissayabhūtānaṃ mahābhūtāna”nti vuttaṃ. Yadi evaṃ vatthurūpameva gahetabbaṃ, tadevettha aggahetvā kasmā mahābhūtaggahaṇanti āha “vatthurūpassa hī”tiādi. Yādisoti sammādiṭṭhiādīnaṃ vasena yādiso eva pubbe āgatavipassanāmaggo. “Ayampi aṭṭhaṅgasamannāgatattā tādiso evā”ti vatvā ariyamaggo “yathāgatamaggo”ti vutto. Idaṃ tāvetthāti ettha etasmiṃ sutte dhammadesanatthaṃ ābhatāya yathāvuttaupamāya idaṃ saṃsandanaṃ.

'Of that very' (tasseva): the connection is made by supplying the words 'of the city, designated as personal identity' and 'which were called 'gates'. The construction is that 'a swift pair of messengers' was said. Because the physical heart-base is taken as a crossroads in the middle, it is said 'of the great elements which are the support for the heart-base.' If so, the physical base itself should be taken. Why then, not taking that here, are the great elements taken? To this, he says, 'For the physical base...' and so on. 'Of what kind' (yādiso) refers to the path of insight that came before, by way of right view and so on. Having said, 'This too is of the same kind because it is endowed with eight factors,' the noble path is called 'the path as it has come.' 'This here' (idaṃ tāvettha) means: here in this sutta, this is the correlation with the aforesaid simile, which was introduced for the purpose of teaching the Dhamma.

Idaṃ saṃsandananti idāni vakkhamānaṃ upamāya saṃsandanaṃ. Nagarasāmiupamā pañcakkhandhavasena dassanavisuddhipattaṃ khīṇāsavaṃ dassetuṃ ābhatā. Siṅghāṭakūpamā catumahābhūtavasena dassanavisuddhipattaṃ khīṇāsavaṃ dassetuṃ ābhatāti yojanā. “Catusaccameva kathita”nti vatvā tāni saccāni niddhāretvā dassetuṃ “sakalenapi hī”tiādi vuttaṃ. Idha nagarasambhāro chadvārādayo. Tena hi chaphassāyatanādayo upamitā. Te pana dukkhasaccapariyāpannāti vuttaṃ “nagarasambhārena dukkhasaccameva kathita”nti.

'This connection' means the connection with the simile that is about to be explained. The simile of the city lord is introduced to show the arahant who has attained purification of vision by way of the five aggregates. The simile of the crossroads is introduced to show the arahant who has attained purification of vision by way of the four great elements. The application is thus. Having said, 'Only the four truths are spoken,' in order to determine and show those truths, what begins with 'For indeed, by the entirety...' was said. Here, the city's equipment is the six gates and so on. By that, the six sense bases and so on are compared. But these are included in the truth of suffering, so it is said, 'By the city's equipment, only the truth of suffering is spoken.'

Kiṃsukopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Kiṃsukopama Sutta is concluded.

9. Vīṇopamasuttavaṇṇanā

9. Commentary on the Vīṇopama Sutta

246. Bhikkhussa vā bhikkhuniyā vāti kāmaṃ pāḷiyaṃ parisādvayameva gahitaṃ, sesaparisānaṃ pana tadaññesampi devamanussānanti sabbasādhāraṇovāyaṃ dhammasaṅgahoti imamatthaṃ upamāpubbakaṃ katvā dassetuṃ “yathā nāmā”tiādi [M 2.0345] āraddhaṃ. Yajantoti dadanto. Vinditabboti laddhabbo, adhigantabboti attho.

246. 'Of a monk or a nun'—although in the Pāli text only the two assemblies are mentioned, this collection of Dhamma is common to all, including the other assemblies and others besides them, devas and humans. To show this meaning by means of a preceding simile, what begins with 'Just as...' was started. 'Sacrificing' means giving. 'To be obtained' means to be acquired; that is the meaning.

Chandoti taṇhāchando. Tenāha – “dubbalataṇhā so rañjetuṃ na sakkotī”ti. Pubbuppattikā ekasmiṃ ārammaṇe paṭhamaṃ uppannā. Sā hi anāsevanattā mandā. Soti chando. Rañjetuṃ na sakkoti laddhāsevanattā. Doso nāma cittadūsanattā. Tānīti daṇḍādānādīni. Tammūlakāti lobhamūlakā tāva māyāsāṭheyyamānātimānadiṭṭhicāpalādayo, dosamūlakā upanāhamakkhapalāsaissāmacchariyathambhasārambhādayo, mohamūlakā ahirika-anottappa-thinamiddhavicikicchuddhacca-viparītamanasikārādayo, saṃkilesadhammā gahitāva honti taṃmūlakattā. Yasmā pana sabbepi saṃkilesadhammā dvādasākusalacittuppādapariyāpannā eva, tasmā tesampettha gahitabhāvaṃ dassetuṃ “chando rāgoti vā”ti vuttaṃ.

'Desire' means desire based on craving. Therefore it is said, 'Weak craving is unable to color him.' 'Previously arisen' means first arisen in a single object. For it is weak due to non-cultivation. That is desire. It is unable to color because cultivation has not been acquired. It is called 'hate' because it corrupts the mind. 'Those' means the taking of sticks and so on. 'Rooted in that': those rooted in greed are deceit, craftiness, conceit, excessive conceit, wrong view, restlessness, and so on; those rooted in hate are animosity, denigration, envy, stinginess, obstinacy, rivalry, and so on; those rooted in delusion are shamelessness, lack of moral dread, sloth and torpor, doubt, agitation, wrong attention, and so on. Defiled states are indeed included because they are rooted in that. And since all defiled states are included in the twelve unwholesome consciousness-arisings, to show their inclusion here, it is said, 'Desire or lust...'

Bhāyitabbaṭṭhena [V 2.0060] sabhayo. Bheravaṭṭhena sappaṭibhayo. Kusalapakkhassa vikkhambhanaṭṭhena sakaṇṭako. Kusalaanavajjadhammehi duravagāhaṭṭhena sagahano. Bhavasampattibhavanibbānānaṃ appadānabhāvato ummaggo. Duggatigāmimaggattā kummaggo. Iriyanāti vattanā paṭipajjanā. Duggatigāmitāya kileso eva kilesamaggo. Na sakkā sampattibhavaṃ gantuṃ kuto nibbānagamananti adhippāyo.

By the aspect of being something to be feared, it is 'sabhaya' (with fear). By the aspect of being dreadful, it is 'sappaṭibhaya' (with terror). By the aspect of suppressing the wholesome side, it is 'sakaṇṭaka' (with thorns). By the aspect of being hard to fathom by means of wholesome and faultless qualities, it is 'sagahana' (with thickets). Because of not giving rise to prosperity in existence or to Nibbāna, it is 'ummagga' (a wrong path). Because it is a path leading to a bad destination, it is 'kummagga' (an evil path). 'Going' is conducting oneself, practicing. Because it leads to a bad destination, defilement itself is the path of defilements. It is not possible to go to a prosperous existence; how much less to Nibbāna? This is the intention.

Asubhāvajjanādīhīti ādi-saddena aniccamanasikārādīnampi saṅgaho daṭṭhabbo. Cittaṃ nivattati sarāgacittaṃ na uppajjati paṭipakkhamanasikārena vinoditattā. Majjhattārammaṇeti aññāṇupekkhaṭṭhāniye ārammaṇe. Uddesa…pe… āvajjantassāti uddisāpanavasena uddesaṃ, paripucchāpanavasena paripucchaṃ, garūnaṃ santike vasanavasena garuvāsaṃ āvajjantassa. Cittanti gambhīrañāṇacariya-paccavekkhaṇa-paññavanta-puggalasevanavasena tadadhimuttisiddhiyā aññāṇacittaṃ nivattati.

By 'contemplation of foulness and so on,' through the word 'and so on,' the inclusion of attention to impermanence and so on should also be understood. The mind turns away; a lustful mind does not arise, because it has been dispelled by attention to the opposite. 'In a neutral object' means in an object that is a basis for equanimity based on ignorance. 'For one reflecting on recitation... etc.': for one reflecting on recitation by way of causing recitation, on questioning by way of causing questioning, on dwelling with a teacher by way of dwelling in the presence of teachers. 'The mind': by way of associating with persons possessing deep knowledge, conduct, and wisdom, and by way of reviewing, the mind of ignorance turns away through the accomplishment of resolve towards that.

Yathā “pujjabhavaphalaṃ puñña”nti vuttaṃ “evamidaṃ puññaṃ pavaḍḍhatī”ti (dī. ni. 3.80), evaṃ kiṭṭhasambhavattā “kiṭṭha”nti vuttanti āha “kiṭṭhanti kiṭṭhaṭṭhāne uppannasassa”nti.

Just as it is said, 'Merit is the fruit of a worshipful state,' and 'Thus this merit grows,' so, because it arises from tillage, it is called 'tillage.' He says, ''Tillage' means the crop that has arisen in the place of tillage.'

Ghaṭāti [M 2.0346] siṅgayugaṃ idhādhippetanti āha “dvinnaṃ siṅgānaṃ antare”ti. Ghaṭāti goṇādīnaṃ siṅgantaraṭṭhassa samaññāti vadanti. Nāsārajjuketi nāsārajjupātaṭṭhāne.

'Ghaṭā' means the pair of horns intended here; he says, 'between the two horns.' 'Ghaṭā' is a designation for the space between the horns of oxen and the like, they say. 'Nāsārajjuke' means at the place where the nose-rope is placed.

Dameti puthuttārammaṇato nivāreti. Nanti cittaṃ. Yaṃ suttaṃ subhāsitaṃ mayā. Tadassāti tadā assa bhikkhuno. Ārammaṇeti kammaṭṭhānārammaṇe.

'He tames' means he restrains it from diverse objects. 'Naṃ' means the mind. 'The sutta well-spoken by me.' 'Tadassāti' means 'then for that monk.' 'Ārammaṇeti' means 'in the meditation object.'

Sudujitanti nibbisevanabhāvakaraṇena jitaṃ. Sutajjitanti suṭṭhu dūrakaraṇena jitaṃ, tathābhūtañca tajjitaṃ nāma hotīti tathā vuttaṃ. Gocarajjhattanti ajjhattabhūto gocaro. Kammaṭṭhānārammaṇañhi bahiddhārūpādiārammaṇavidhuratāya ajjhattanti vuccati. Samatho anurakkhaṇaṃ etassāti samathānurakkhaṇaṃ. Yathā indriyasaṃvarasīlaṃ samathānurakkhaṇaṃ hoti, tathā kathitanti attho. Yathā hi indriyasaṃvarasīlaṃ samathassa paccayo, evaṃ samathopi tassa paccayoti.

'Sudujitaṃ' means conquered by the act of non-association. 'Sutajjitaṃ' means well conquered by making it distant; and because such a state is called 'tajjita,' it is said thus. 'Gocarajjhattaṃ' means an internal domain. For the meditation object is called 'internal' because it is devoid of external objects such as form. 'Samathānurakkhaṇaṃ' is 'serenity is its protection.' The meaning is that it is said in such a way that the virtue of sense restraint has serenity as its protection. For just as the virtue of sense restraint is a condition for serenity, so too is serenity a condition for it.

Vādiyamānāya vīṇāya. Cittaṃ rañjetīti rajjanena. Avissajjanīyatāya cittaṃ bandhatīti [V 2.0061] bandhanīyo. Veṭṭhaketi tantīnaṃ āsajjanaveṭṭhake. Koṇanti kavaṇato vīṇāya saddakaraṇato koṇanti laddhanāmaṃ dārudaṇḍaṃ siṅgādīsu yena kenaci kataṃ ghaṭikaṃ. Tenāha “caturassaṃ sāradaṇḍaka”nti.

Of the lute being played. Because it delights the mind, it is 'delighting' (rajjanīya). Because it binds the mind by its nature of not being let go, it is 'binding' (bandhanīya). 'In the wrapping device' (veṭṭhake) means in the device for fastening and wrapping the strings. 'Koṇa' is the name given to the wooden rod—a plectrum (ghaṭika) made of anything whatsoever, such as horn—because it strikes (kavaṇato), because it makes the sound of the lute. Therefore he says, 'a four-sided rod of heartwood.'

Yasmā so rājā rājamahāmatto vā saddaṃ yathāsabhāvato na aññāsi, tasmiṃ tassa ajānanākārameva dassetuṃ “saddaṃ passissāmī”tiādi vuttaṃ.

Because that king or royal minister did not understand sound according to its true nature, what begins with 'I will see the sound' was said, in order to show just that manner of his not knowing.

Asatī kirāyanti pāḷiyaṃ liṅgavipallāsena vuttanti yathāliṅgameva vadanto “asā”ti āha. “Asatīti lāmakādhivacana”nti vatvā tattha payogaṃ dassetuṃ “asā lokitthiyo nāmā”ti vuttaṃ, loke itthiyo nāma asatiyoti attho, tattha kāraṇamāha “velā tāsaṃ na vijjatī”ti. Pakatiyā loke jeṭṭhabhātā kaniṭṭhabhātā mātulotiādikā velā mariyādā tāsaṃ na vijjati. Kasmā? Sārattā ca pagabbā ca sabbesampi sambhogavasena viniyogaṃ gacchanti. Kathaṃ? Sikhī sabbaghaso yathā. Tenevāha –

They say that 'asatī' is used in the Pāli with a reversal of gender; speaking according to the correct gender, he says 'asā.' Having said, ''Asatī' is a designation for one who is base,' to show its usage there, it is said, 'asā lokitthiyo nāma,' the meaning of which is, 'in the world, women are indeed unchaste.' There he states the reason: 'for them, there is no boundary.' By nature, in the world, for them there exists no boundary or limit such as elder brother, younger brother, maternal uncle, and so on. Why? Because being impassioned and bold, they are available for the enjoyment of all. How? Like fire, the all-consumer. Therefore he says—

“Sabbā [M 2.0347] nadī vaṅkagatī, sabbe kaṭṭhamayā vanā;

Sabbitthiyo kare pāpaṃ, labhamāne nivātake”ti. (jā. 2.21.308);

All rivers have a winding course, all forests are made of wood; all women would do evil, finding a private place.

Aññampi tantibaddhaṃ caturassaambaṇavāditādīni. Vīṇā viya pañcakkhandhā anekadhammasamūhabhāvato. Rājā viya yogāvacaro tappaṭibaddhadhammagavesakattā. Assāti yogāvacarassa.

Other things bound by strings are also the four-sided `ambaṇa`-drum and other instruments. The five aggregates are like a lute, because of being a collection of many phenomena. The meditator is like a king, because of being one who seeks out the phenomena connected with that. 'Of that one' (assa) means 'of the meditator.'

Nirayādito aññasmimpi gati-saddo vattati. Tato visesanatthaṃ “gatigatī”ti vuttaṃ “dukkhadukkhaṃ, rūparūpa”nti ca yathā, gatisaññitaṃ pavattiṭṭhānanti attho. Tenāha – “etthantare saṃsarati vattatī”ti. Sañjāyanapadeso eva gatīti sañjātigati.

The word 'gati' (destination) is used for other states as well, starting from hell. Therefore, for the purpose of specification, 'gatigati' is said, just like 'dukkhadukkhaṃ' (suffering upon suffering) and 'rūparūpaṃ' (form upon form). The meaning is a place of occurrence designated as a 'gati.' Therefore he says: 'In this interval one wanders in saṃsāra, one revolves.' 'Sañjātigati' (destination of birth) means the place of birth itself is the 'gati.'

Taṃ pana gatiṃ sattānaṃ saṃvegavatthubhūtassa paccakkhassa gabbhāsayassa vasena dassetuṃ “ayamassa kāyo”tiādi vuttaṃ. Rūpadhammassa salakkhaṇaṃ gati niṭṭhā, tato paraṃ aññaṃ kiñci natthīti salakkhaṇagati. Abhāvo accantābhāvo. Santānavicchedo vibhavagati taṃniṭṭhānabhāvā. Bhedoti khaṇanirodho, idhāpi taṃniṭṭhānatāyeva pariyāyo. Yāva bhavaggāti yāva sabbabhavaggā. Salakkhaṇavibhavagatibhedagatiyo “eseva nayo”ti imināva pakāsitāti na gahitā. Tassa khīṇāsavassa na hoti aggamaggena samucchinnattā.

To show that destination of beings, which is a basis for spiritual urgency, by means of the directly perceived womb, it is said: 'This is his body,' etc. The own-characteristic of the phenomenon of form is its destination, its finality; beyond that, there is nothing else—thus, the destination of its own-characteristic. Non-existence is absolute non-existence. The cutting off of the continuum is the destination of annihilation, its conclusion being non-existence. 'Breaking' means momentary cessation; here too, it is merely a synonym for that state of conclusion. 'Up to the peak of existence' means as far as the highest point of all existence. The destinations of own-characteristic, annihilation, and breaking are revealed by 'this very method,' so they are not taken up separately. For that one whose taints are destroyed, it does not exist, because it is completely cut off by the supreme path.

Sīlaṃ [V 2.0062] kathitaṃ rūpādīsu chandādinivāraṇassa kathitattā. Majjhe samādhibhāvanā kathitā “ajjhattameva santiṭṭhati…pe… samādhiyatī”ti jotitattā. Pariyosāne ca nibbānaṃ kathitaṃ “yampissa…pe… na hotī”ti vacanato.

Virtue is spoken of, because the prevention of desire and so forth in regard to forms, etc., is spoken of. In the middle, the development of concentration is spoken of, as it is illuminated by the statement, 'He becomes inwardly settled... and is concentrated.' And at the conclusion, Nibbāna is spoken of, according to the text, 'That which for him... is not.'

Vīṇopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Vīṇopama Sutta is concluded.

10. Chappāṇakopamasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Chappāṇakopama Sutta

247. Vaṇasarīroti vaṇitasarīro. Pakkattāti kuthitattā. Saradaṇḍesu sarasamaññāti kaṇḍa-saddo sarapariyāyoti āha – “saravananti kaṇḍavana”nti. Arugatto…pe… veditabbo guṇasarīrassa khaṇḍachiddasīlādīhi heṭṭhā majjhe uparibhāge ca bhedavisamacchinnavikāradosattā. Ettha [M 2.0348] kusā “kaṇṭakā”ti adhippetā kaṇṭakasadisattā, kusatiṇānaṃ eva vā vuttākārapadeso “kusakaṇṭako”ti vutto.

247. 'Vaṇasarīro' means a wounded body. 'Pakkattā' means because it is rotten. In 'saradaṇḍesu,' the word 'kaṇḍa' is a synonym for 'sara,' hence it is said: 'saravana means a kaṇḍavana,' a forest of reeds. 'A body with sores'... should be understood as the body of virtue, due to the fault of being split, unevenly cut, and distorted in its lower, middle, and upper parts by such things as broken and perforated virtue. Here, 'kusā' (kusa grass) is intended as 'thorns' because they resemble thorns; or, a part of the kusa grass of the kind described is called 'kusakaṇṭako' (kusa-thorn).

Gāmavāsīnaṃ vijjhanaṭṭhenāti nārahova hutvā tesaṃ kārānaṃ paṭiggahaṇavasena pīḷanaṭṭhena.

'In the sense of piercing the village-dwellers' means: in the sense of oppressing them by accepting their services while being unworthy.

Pakkhinti hatthiliṅgasakuṇaṃ. Tassa kira hatthisoṇḍasadisaṃ mukhaṃ, tasmā “hatthisoṇḍasakuṇa”nti vuttaṃ. Vissajjeyyāti rajjuyā yathābaddhaṃ eva vissajjeyya.

'Pakkhin' refers to the elephant-marked bird. Indeed, its beak resembles an elephant's trunk, therefore it is called 'hatthisoṇḍasakuṇa' (elephant-trunk bird). 'Vissajjeyya' means one should release it with the rope, just as it was bound.

Bhogehīti attano sarīrabhogehi. Maṇḍalaṃ bandhitvāti yathā sarīraṃ maṇḍalākārena tiṭṭhati, evaṃ katvā. Supissāmīti niddaṃ okkamissāmi. Ḍetukāmoti uppatitukāmo. Disā disanti disato disaṃ.

'Bhogehī' means by its own bodily coils. 'Maṇḍalaṃ bandhitvā' means having acted in such a way that the body remains in a circular shape. 'Supissāmi' means 'I will fall asleep.' 'Ḍetukāmo' means 'desiring to fly up.' 'Disā disaṃ' means from direction to direction.

Cha pāṇakā viya cha āyatanāni nānajjhāsayattā, nānajjhāsayatā ca nesaṃ nānāvisayaninnatāya daṭṭhabbā. Daḷharajju viya taṇhā tesaṃ bandhanato. Majjhe gaṇṭhi viya avijjā bandhanassa dubbinimmocanahetuto. Ārammaṇaṃ balavaṃ hoti manuññabhāvena ceva tattha taṇhābhinivesassa daḷhabhāvena ca.

The six sense bases are like six creatures due to their different dispositions, and their different dispositions should be seen as their inclination toward different objects. Craving is like a strong rope, because it binds them. Ignorance is like a knot in the middle, because it is the cause for the bond being difficult to release. An object is strong due to its agreeable nature and the firmness of craving's adherence to it.

Sarikkhakena vā upamāya āharaṇapakkhe. Appanāti saṃsandanā. Pāḷiyaṃyeva appitā upamā “evameva kho”tiādinā. Rūpacittādivisamaninnattā cakkhussa visamajjhāsayatā. Esa nayo sesesupi.

Or, in the case of adducing a comparison by way of a simile. 'Application' means comparison. The simile is applied in the Pāli itself by means of 'Even so...' and so on. Because of its uneven inclination towards forms, mind-states, etc., the eye's disposition is uneven. This is the method for the remaining ones as well.

Kaṇṇacchiddakūpaketi [V 2.0063] kaṇṇacchiddasaññite āvāṭe. Tassāti sotassa. Paccayo hotīti upanissayo hoti tena vinā saddaggahaṇassa abhāvato. “Ajaṭākāsopi vaṭṭati evā”ti vatvā tassa paccayabhāvaṃ dassetuṃ “antoleṇasmi”ntiādi vuttaṃ. Dhātuparamparā ghaṭṭentoti bhūtaparamparāsaṅghaṭṭento.

'In the little well of the ear-hole' means in the cavity designated as the 'ear-hole.' 'Of that' means of the ear-organ. 'It is a condition' means it is a decisive support condition, because without it, the grasping of sound is impossible. Having said, 'Even unobstructed space is suitable,' to show its nature as a condition, 'inside the cave,' etc., is said. 'A succession of elements striking' means a succession of primary elements colliding.

Evaṃ santeti evaṃ bhūtaparamparāvasena sadde sotapathamāgacchante. Sampattagocaratā hoti sotassa. Ghānādīnaṃ viya “dūre saddo”ti jānanaṃ na sambhaveyya sampattagāhibhāvato. Tathā tathā jānanākāro hoti manoviññāṇassa gahaṇākāravisesato. Sotaviññāṇappavatti pana ubhayattha samānāva. Dūre ṭhitopi saddo tādise ṭhāne [M 2.0349] paṭighosādīnaṃ paccayo hoti ayokanto viya ayocalanassāti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Dhammatāti dhammasabhāvo, saddassa so sabhāvoti attho. Tato tato savanaṃ hoti ākāsasaññitassa upanissayassa labbhanato. Yadi pana sotaṃ sampattagāhī siyā, cittasamuṭṭhānasaddo sotaviññāṇassa ārammaṇapaccayo na siyā. Paṭṭhāne ca avisesena “saddāyatanaṃ sotaviññāṇassa ārammaṇapaccayena paccayo”ti vutto, bahiddhā ca cittajassa saddassa sambhavo natthi. Atha vā cittajo saddo dhātuparamparāya sotapasādaṃ ghaṭṭeti, na so cittajo saddo, yo paramparāya pavatto. Utujo hi so, tasmā yathuppanno saddo, tattha ṭhitova sotapasādassa āpāthaṃ āgacchatīti niṭṭhamettha gantabbaṃ. Tena vuttaṃ “asampattagocarameveta”nti.

'This being so' means: when sound thus comes into the path of the ear by way of a succession of primary elements. For the ear-organ, there is the state of having an object that has arrived. As with the nose and other senses, knowing 'the sound is far away' would not be possible, because it grasps what has arrived. The mode of knowing varies according to the specific mode of grasping of mind-consciousness. The process of ear-consciousness, however, is the same in both cases. Even a sound situated far away can be a condition for echoes and so on in such a place, just as a magnet can be for the movement of iron—this should be understood. 'Dhammatā' means the intrinsic nature of a phenomenon; the meaning is that this is the intrinsic nature of sound. Hearing occurs from various locations because the decisive support condition designated as space is obtainable. But if the ear-organ were to grasp only what has arrived, a sound originating from consciousness would not be an object-condition for ear-consciousness. In the Paṭṭhāna, it is stated without distinction: 'The sound base is a condition for ear-consciousness by way of the object-condition.' And externally, the occurrence of a sound born of consciousness is not possible. Alternatively, a sound born of consciousness strikes the ear-sensitivity by means of a succession of elements, but that which has arisen through the succession is not the mind-produced sound itself. For it is produced by temperature. Therefore, the conclusion to be reached here is that a sound, as it arises, comes into the range of the ear-sensitivity while remaining in its own place. Hence, it is said, 'This indeed has an object that is not arrived at.'

Tadā ekaggacittataṃ āpajjati parissayānaṃ abhāvato. Nāsacchiddasaṅkhātaākāsasannissaye vattanato ghānaṃ ākāsajjhāsayaṃ vuttaṃ. Vātena vinā gandhagahaṇassa asambhavato vātūpanissayagandhagocaraṃ. Tenāha “tathā hī”tiādi.

Then it attains one-pointedness of mind due to the absence of dangers. The nose is said to have a disposition for space because it functions in dependence on the space designated as the nostril. Because the grasping of an odor is impossible without air, the nose has odor as its object, which has air as its decisive support. Therefore, he said: 'For thus...' etc.

Gāmato laddhabbaṃ āhāraṃ gāmaṃ, tanninnatāya gāmajjhāsayatā vuttā. Tathā hi jīvitanimittaṃ raso jīvitaṃ, tasmiṃ ninnatāya taṃ avhāyatīti jivhā. Na sakkā kheḷena atemitassa rasaṃ jānituṃ, tasmā āposannissitarasārammaṇā jivhāti.

Food to be obtained from the village is 'the village'; because of inclination towards that, the disposition for the village is spoken of. For taste is the cause of life, it is life; because of its inclination towards that, it calls to it, thus 'jivhā' (tongue). It is not possible to know the taste of something not moistened by saliva; therefore, the tongue has taste, which is dependent on the water element, as its object.

Āmakasusānato bahi. Upādiṇṇakajjhāsayoti upādiṇṇakaninno. Kāyapasādasannissayabhūtāya pathaviyā phoṭṭhabbārammaṇe ghaṭite eva tattha viññāṇuppatti, na aññathāti vuttaṃ “pathavīsannissitaphoṭṭhabbārammaṇo”ti. Tathā hītiādi kāyassa upādiṇṇakajjhāsayatāya sādhakaṃ[V 2.0064]. Ajjhattikabāhirāti ajjhattikā bāhirā vā. Assāti kāyassa. Susanthatassātiādi tassa pathaviyā paccayabhāvadassanaṃ.

Outside the charnel ground for raw corpses. 'Upādiṇṇakajjhāsayo' means inclined towards what is clung to. Consciousness arises there only when a tangible object comes into contact with the earth element, which is the support for the body's sensitivity, and not otherwise. Thus it is said: 'having a tangible object that is dependent on earth.' 'For thus,' etc., is proof of the body's disposition for what is clung to. 'Internal-and-external' means internal or external. 'Of it' refers to the body. 'Of one well-laid out,' etc., is a demonstration of the earth element's nature as a condition for it.

Nānajjhāsayoti nānārammaṇaninno. Tena manassa makkaṭassa viya anavaṭṭhitataṃ dasseti. Tenāha “diṭṭhapubbepī”tiādi. Mūlabhavaṅgaggahaṇena piṭṭhibhavaṅgaṃ nivatteti. Assāti manassa. Evaṃ kiriyamayaṃ viññāṇaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Nānattanti bhedo.

'Nānajjhāsayo' means being inclined towards diverse objects. By this, he shows the unsteadiness of the mind, like that of a monkey. Hence, he says, 'even in regard to what was previously seen,' etc. By grasping the root bhavaṅga, one turns back the subsequent bhavaṅga. 'Assāti' refers to the mind. Thus, functional consciousness should be understood. 'Diversity' means difference.

Yathārucippavattiyā [M 2.0350] nivāraṇavasena baddhānaṃ. Nibbisevanabhāvanti lolatāsaṅkhātaparipphandassa abhāvaṃ. Nākaḍḍhatīti savisaye rūpārammaṇe cittasantānaṃ, taṃsamaṅginaṃ vā puggalaṃ nākaḍḍhati. Pubbabhāgavipassanāva kathitā āyatanamukhena “evañhi vo, bhikkhave, sikkhitabba”nti vuttattā.

Of those who are bound, by way of preventing their proceeding according to their liking. 'The state of non-indulgence' means the absence of the agitation known as greed. 'It does not draw' means: it does not draw the continuum of consciousness towards a form-object in its own domain, nor does it draw the person endowed with it. It is preliminary insight that is spoken of through the entrance of the sense bases, because it was said, 'Thus, monks, should you train'.

Chappāṇakopamasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Chappāṇakopama Sutta is concluded.

11. Yavakalāpisuttavaṇṇanā

11. Commentary on the Yavakalāpi Sutta

248. Yavapuñjo suparipakkayavasamudāyo. Kājahatthāti daṇḍahatthā. Potheyyunti yathā yavasaṅkhātaṃ dhaññaṃ vaṇṭato muccati, evaṃ potheyyuṃ. Yave sāvetvāti yavasīsato yave pothanena mocetvā vivecetvā.

248. A heap of barley is a collection of fully ripe barley. 'Kājahatthā' means 'with sticks in their hands.' 'Potheyyuṃ' means they would beat it in such a way that the grain called barley is freed from the stalk. 'Having processed the barley' means having freed the barley grains from the barley-head by beating, and having separated them.

Catumahāpatho viya cha āyatanāni ārammaṇadaṇḍakehi hananaṭṭhānattā. Yavakalāpī viya satto tehi haññamānattā. Cha byābhaṅgiyo viya sabhāvato chadhāpi paccekaṃ iṭṭhāniṭṭhamajjhattavasena aṭṭhārasa ārammaṇāni yavakalāpaṭṭhāniyassa sattassa hananato. Bhavapatthanāya aparāparuppattiṃ sandhāya “bhavapatthanā kilesā”ti bahuvacananiddeso. Bhavapatthanā ca tassa paccayabhūtā kilesā cāti bhavapatthanākilesāti evaṃ vā ettha attho daṭṭhabbo. Yasmā sattānaṃ vaṭṭadukkhaṃ nāma sabbampi taṃ bhavapatthanāmūlakaṃ, tasmā vuttaṃ “evaṃ sattā”tiādi. Bhavapatthanakilesāti ca bhavapatthanāmūlakaṃ kilesaṃ.

The six sense bases are like a great crossroads, as they are a place of striking with the staffs that are sense objects. A being is like a sheaf of barley, being struck by them. The eighteen objects—which are sixfold by nature, and individually pleasant, unpleasant, or neutral—are like six executioners, because they strike the being who stands in the place of a sheaf of barley. Regarding the aspiration for existence, referring to repeated arising, the designation 'the defilements of aspiration for existence' is in the plural. Or the meaning here should be understood thus: 'the defilements of aspiration for existence' means the aspiration for existence and the defilements that are its conditions. Since all the suffering of beings' round of existence is rooted in the aspiration for existence, it is therefore said, 'Thus beings,' and so on. And 'the defilements of aspiration for existence' means the defilement rooted in the aspiration for existence.

Bhummanti samīpatthe bhummaṃ. Tenāha “sudhammāya dvāre”ti. Na katanti dukkhuppādanaṃ na kataṃ. Navagūthasūkaraṃ viyāti navagūthabhakkhasūkaraṃ viya. Cittenevāti yo bajjhati, tassa citteneva[V 2.0065]. Tasmāti yasmā vepacittibandhanassa bandhanamuccanaṃ viya, tasmā “vepacittibandhana”nti vuttaṃ. Ñāṇamokkhaṃ bandhananti ñāṇena muccanaṃ bandhanaṃ.

The locative `bhummaṃ` is used in the sense of proximity. Therefore, he said, 'at the door of the Sudhammā Hall.' 'Not done' means the production of suffering is not done. 'Like a fresh-dung pig' means like a pig that eats fresh dung. 'By the mind alone' means: whoever is bound, it is by their mind alone. 'Therefore' means: because it is like the binding and releasing of the Vepacitti-bond, it is therefore called the 'Vepacitti-bond.' 'The bond of liberation by knowledge' means the bond from which one is freed by knowledge.

Maññamānoti parikappitataṇhāvasena “etaṃ mamā”ti, diṭṭhivasena “eso me attā”ti, mānavasena “esohamasmī”ti maññanto. Khandhavinimuttassa maññamānavatthuno abhāvā “khandhe maññanto”ti vuttaṃ. Etanti “mārassā”ti [M 2.0351] etaṃ sāmivacanaṃ. Kilesamārena baddhoti kilesamārena teneva kilesabandhanena baddho. Muttoti etthāpi eseva nayo.

'Conceiving' means conceiving by way of speculative craving as 'This is mine,' by way of view as 'This is my self,' and by way of conceit as 'This I am.' Because there is no object for conceiving apart from the aggregates, it is said 'conceiving in the aggregates.' Regarding the word `etaṃ`, `mārassā` is a genitive expression. 'Bound by the Māra of defilements' means bound by the Māra of defilements, by that very bond of defilements. This same method applies also to 'freed'.

Taṇhāmaññanāya sati diṭṭhimānamaññanānaṃ pasaṅgo eva natthīti yathā “asmī”ti padena diṭṭhimānamaññanā vuttā honti, evaṃ taṇhāmaññitāpīti vuttaṃ “asmīti padena taṇhāmaññitaṃ vutta”nti. Ayamahamasmīti padena diṭṭhimaññitanti etarahi labbhamānadiṭṭhivatthuvaseneva. Bhavissanti anāgatadiṭṭhivatthuparikappanavaseneva diṭṭhimaññitaṃ. Yebhuyyena hi anāgatāliṅganā sassatadiṭṭhi. Ucchedavasena diṭṭhimaññitamevāti ānetvā sambandho. Rūpītiādīni padāni. Sassatassevāti sassatagāhasseva pabhedadīpanāni. Yasmā maññitaṃ ābādhavasena rogo, antodosavasena gaṇḍo, aṅganikantavasena sallaṃ, tasmā imehi taṇhādīhi kilesehi pākaṭacalanavasena iñjanti ceva, apākaṭasañcalanavasena phandanti ca. Papañcitaṃ saṃsāre cirāyanaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ, khandhasantānassa vā vitthāraṇaṃ. Pamattākārappattā mucchanākārappattā. Tesanti taṇhādiṭṭhikilesānaṃ. Ākāradassanatthanti pavattiākāradassanatthaṃ.

Just as conceiving by view and conceit are expressed by the word 'I am,' so too is conceiving by craving. Therefore it is said, 'conceiving by craving is expressed by the word "I am,"' because when there is conceiving by craving, there is no occasion for conceiving by view and conceit. Conceiving by view, expressed by the words 'This I am,' is based only on a presently obtained object of view. Conceiving by view as 'I will be' is based only on the speculation about a future object of view. For the most part, clinging to the future is the eternalist view. The connection should be made by supplying 'it is simply conceiving by view in the sense of annihilation.' The terms 'material,' etc., are distinctions illustrating only the varieties of eternalist grasping. Because conceiving is a disease in the sense of affliction, a boil in the sense of internal corruption, and a dart in the sense of piercing pain, therefore, through these defilements of craving and so on, they are stirred in the sense of manifest movement and they tremble in the sense of latent movement. Proliferation should be seen as lingering long in saṃsāra, or as the extension of the continuity of the aggregates. They have reached a state of heedlessness, they have reached a state of infatuation. 'Of them' refers to the defilements of craving, view, and so on. 'For the purpose of showing the mode' means for the purpose of showing the mode of occurrence.

Māno nāma “seyyohamasmī”tiādinā majjanākārappavatti. Taṇhāya sampayuttamānavasenāti kasmā vuttaṃ? Nanu sabbo māno taṇhāsampayutto? Sati hi byabhicāre visesanaṃ icchitabbanti. Saccametaṃ, taṇhā pana atthi mānassa paccayabhūtā, atthi mānassa appaccayabhūtā, yato māno aniyato vuccati. Tathā hi paṭṭhāne “saṃyojanaṃ dhammaṃ paṭicca saṃyojano dhammo uppajjati hetupaccayā”ti ettha saṃyojanāni saṃyojanehi yathālābhaṃ yojetvā dassitayojanāya “kāmarāgasaṃyojanaṃ paṭicca mānasaṃyojanaṃ avijjāsaṃyojana”nti vatvā “kāmarāgasaṃyojanaṃ paṭicca avijjāsaṃyojana”nti, “mānasaṃyojanaṃ paṭicca bhavarāgasaṃyojanaṃ avijjāsaṃyojana”nti ca vatvā “bhavarāgasaṃyojanaṃ paṭicca avijjāsaṃyojana”nti vuttaṃ. Ettha ca kāmarāgabhavarāgasaṃyojanehi mānassa aniyatabhāvo pakāsito. Tattha yā taṇhā balavatī, taṃ sandhāya idaṃ vuttaṃ “taṇhāya sampayuttamānavasenā”ti. Balavataṇhāsampayutto hi māno sayampi balavā hutvā asmīti savisesaṃ majjanavasena pavattatīti.

Conceit (māna) is the occurrence in the mode of intoxication, such as 'I am superior.' Why is it said, 'by way of conceit associated with craving'? Is not all conceit associated with craving? For a qualification is desired only when there is an exception. This is true. However, sometimes craving is a condition for conceit, and sometimes it is not a condition for conceit, for which reason conceit is called 'variable.' For instance, in the Paṭṭhāna, it is stated: 'Dependent on a fetter-state, a fetter-state arises as a root condition.' Here, in the explanation where fetters are connected with fetters as they are found, it is said: 'Dependent on the fetter of sensual lust, the fetter of conceit and the fetter of ignorance arise,' and 'Dependent on the fetter of sensual lust, the fetter of ignorance arises'; and also 'Dependent on the fetter of conceit, the fetter of craving for existence and the fetter of ignorance arise,' and 'Dependent on the fetter of craving for existence, the fetter of ignorance arises.' And here the variable nature of conceit in relation to the fetters of sensual lust and craving for existence is shown. In that context, this was said, 'by way of conceit associated with craving,' with reference to that craving which is strong. For conceit associated with strong craving, having itself become strong, occurs in the mode of intoxication with the specific thought 'I am.'

Diṭṭhivasenāti [M 2.0352][V 2.0066] mānamūlakadiṭṭhivasena. “Seyyohamasmī”tiādinā hi bahulamānupetassa puggalassa rūpādīsu ekaṃ uddissa ayamahamasmīti diṭṭhiyā uppannāya mānassa appahīnattā mānopi tattha tattheva uppajjati. Iti mānamūlakaṃ diṭṭhiṃ sandhāyāha “ayamahamasmīti diṭṭhivasena vutta”nti. Codako pana imamatthaṃ ajānanto anupalabbhamānameva sampayogatthaṃ gahetvā “nanu cā”tiādinā codeti. Itaro “āma natthī”ti tamatthaṃ sampaṭicchitvā “mānassa panā”tiādinā parihāramāha. Tassattho vutto eva.

'By way of view' means by way of a view rooted in conceit. For a person much given to conceit—thinking 'I am superior,' and so on—when a view arises concerning one among form and other phenomena, thinking 'This I am,' because conceit has not been abandoned, conceit also arises right there. Thus, with reference to a view rooted in conceit, he says, 'it is said by way of the view "This I am."' The questioner, however, not understanding this meaning and assuming a sense of association that is not found, objects with 'But surely...' and so on. The other, accepting that point with, 'Yes, there is not,' offers a rebuttal with 'But as for conceit...' Its meaning has already been stated.

Yavakalāpisuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Yavakalāpi Sutta is finished.

Āsīvisavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Āsīvisa Chapter is completed.

Catuttho paṇṇāsako.

The Fourth Fifty.

Saḷāyatanasaṃyuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Connected Discourses on the Six Sense Bases is concluded.

2. Vedanāsaṃyuttaṃ

2. The Connected Discourses on Feeling

1. Sagāthāvaggo

1. The Section with Verses

1. Samādhisuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Samādhi Sutta

249. Vedanā [M 2.0353][V 2.0067] ca pajānātīti saccābhisambodhavasena vuccamānavedanānaṃ pajānanaṃ sātisayasamādhānapubbakanti bhagavatā “samāhito”ti vuttanti āha “upacārena vā appanāya vā samāhito”ti. Vedanāti tissopi vedanā. Dukkhasaccavasenāti dukkhasaccabhāvena, parijānanavasenāti attho. Sambhavanti samudayaṃ taṇhāvijjākammaphassādippabhedaṃ uppattikāraṇaṃ. Tenāha “samudayasaccavasena pajānātī”ti. Yatthāti yaṃnimittaṃ, yaṃ āgamma kāmataṇhāvijjādinirodhā vedanānirodho, tesaṃ ayaṃ nirodho. Nibbānaṃ ārabbha ariyamaggappavattiyā hi nibbānaṃ vedanānirodhoti vutto. Khayaṃ gametīti khayagāmī, taṃ khayagāminaṃ. Chātaṃ vuccati taṇhā kāmānaṃ pātukāmatāvasena pavattanato, accantameva samucchinnattā natthi etasmiṃ chātanti nicchāto. Tenāha “nittaṇho”ti. Sammasanacāravedanāti sammasanūpacāravedanā. Dvīhi padehīti “samāhito sampajāno”ti imehi dvīhi. “Sato”ti pana idaṃ sampajānapadasseva upabrūhananti adhippāyo. Sesehi catūhi catusaccaṃ kathitaṃ, itarehi pana dvīhi catusaccabujjhanameva kathitaṃ. Sabbasaṅgāhikoti sabbasabhāvadhammānaṃ saṅgaṇhanako. Tenāha “catubhūmakadhammaparicchedo vutto”ti.

249. Regarding 'and he understands feeling': because the understanding of feelings—spoken of in terms of the full awakening to the truths—is preceded by superior concentration, the Blessed One said 'concentrated.' Therefore, the commentary says, 'concentrated either by access or by absorption.' 'Feeling' refers to all three kinds of feeling. 'By way of the truth of suffering' means by its being the truth of suffering; the meaning is 'by way of full understanding.' 'They arise' refers to the origin, the cause of arising, which is distinguished as craving, ignorance, kamma, contact, and so on. Therefore, it is said, 'he understands by way of the truth of origin.' 'Where' means that ground; relying on which, through the cessation of sensual craving, ignorance, and so on, there is the cessation of feeling—this is their cessation. For by the occurrence of the noble path with Nibbāna as its object, Nibbāna is called the cessation of feeling. 'He brings to an end' means 'ending'; it refers to that which is ending. Craving is called 'hunger' because it occurs by way of desiring the appearance of sensual pleasures. Since it is utterly eradicated, there is no hunger in him, hence 'unhungry.' Therefore, it is said, 'without craving.' 'Feeling in the range of comprehension' means feeling in the access-range of comprehension. 'By two terms' refers to these two: 'concentrated' and 'clearly comprehending.' The intention is that 'mindful,' however, is an amplification of the term 'clearly comprehending.' By the remaining four terms, the four truths are stated; but by the other two, only the awakening to the four truths is stated. 'All-encompassing' means that which gathers all intrinsic phenomena. Therefore, it is said, 'the delimitation of the phenomena of the four planes is stated.'

Samādhisuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Samādhi Sutta is concluded.

2. Sukhasuttavaṇṇanā

2. Commentary on the Sukha Sutta

250. Dukkhaṃ na hotīti adukkhaṃ, sukhaṃ na hotīti asukhaṃ, ma-kāro padasandhikaro. Adukkhamasukhanti vedayitasaddāpekkhāya napuṃsakaniddeso. Saparasantānagate sandhāya ajjhattabahiddhāgahaṇanti āha “attano ca parassa cā”ti. Tena sabbampi vedayitaṃ gahitanti daṭṭhabbaṃ[V 2.0068]. Nassanasabhāvanti ittarakhaṇatāya [M 2.0354] bhaṅgato uddhaṃ apassitabbasabhāvaṃ. Paloko bhedo etassa atthīti palokinaṃ. Tenāha “bhijjanasabhāva”nti. Ñāṇena phusitvā phusitvāti pubbabhāge vipassanāñāṇena aniccā pabhaṅgunoti ārammaṇato, uttarakālaṃ asammohato maggaparamparāya phusitvā phusitvā vayaṃ passanto. Virajjatīti maggavirāgena virajjati. Sammasanacāravedanā kathitā āraddhavipassakānaṃ vasena desanāti. Lokiyalokuttarehi ñāṇehi yāthāvato parijānanaṃ paṭivijjhanaṃ ñāṇaphusanaṃ.

250. "It is not pain" (dukkhaṃ na hoti) means "not-painful" (adukkhaṃ); "it is not pleasure" (sukhaṃ na hoti) means "not-pleasant" (asukhaṃ). The letter 'ma' serves as a euphonic conjunction. "Neither-painful-nor-pleasant" (adukkhamasukhaṃ) is a neuter designation with reference to the word "feeling" (vedayita). Referring to what pertains to one's own and others' continuities, the inclusion of 'internal' and 'external' is stated as "of oneself and of another." Thus, it should be understood that all feeling is included. "Of a perishing nature" (nassanasabhāvaṃ) means, due to its momentary nature, it has a nature that is not to be seen after its breaking up. "Having dissolution" (palokinaṃ) means it has dissolution (paloka), which is breaking (bheda). Therefore, it is said, "of a breaking nature" (bhijjanasabhāvaṃ). "Touching again and again with knowledge" (ñāṇena phusitvā phusitvā) means, in the earlier stage, with insight knowledge, from the perspective of the object, as impermanent and fragile; and in the later stage, seeing its passing away again and again without delusion through the succession of paths. "He becomes dispassionate" (virajjati) means he becomes dispassionate through the dispassion of the path (maggavirāgena). The feeling associated with the practice of investigation (sammasanacāravedanā) is explained as a teaching for those who have undertaken insight practice. Fully understanding as it really is, penetration, and touching with knowledge are accomplished by means of mundane and supramundane knowledges.

Sukhasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Sukha Sutta is concluded.

3. Pahānasuttavaṇṇanā

3. Commentary on the Pahāna Sutta

251. Sabbampi aṭṭhasatapabhedaṃ taṇhaṃ chindi samucchedapahānavasena pajahi. Tenāha “samucchindī”ti. Yadaggena taṇhā sabbaso samucchinnā, tadaggena sabbānipi saññojanānīti āha “dasavidhampī”tiādi. Sammāti suṭṭhu. Pahānañca nāma upāyena ñāyena pahānanti āha “hetunā kāraṇenā”ti. Dassanābhisamayāti asammohapaṭivedhā. Arahattamaggo hi uppajjamānova sabhāvapaṭicchādakaṃ mohaṃ viddhaṃsento eva pavattati, tena māno yāthāvato diṭṭho nāma hoti, ayamassa dassanābhisamayo. Yathā hi sūriye uṭṭhite andhakāro viddhaṃsiyamāno vihato, evaṃ arahattamagge uppajjamāne so sabbaso pahīno eva hoti, na tasmiṃ santāne patiṭṭhaṃ labhati, ayamassa pahānābhisamayo. Tena vuttaṃ “arahattamaggo hī”tiādi. Kiccavasenāti asammohapaṭivedhasaṅkhātassa dassanakiccassa anipphādanavasena.

251. He cut off all craving, divided into one hundred and eight categories, and abandoned it by way of eradication-abandonment (samucchedapahāna). Therefore, it is said, "he cut off completely" (samucchindī). To the extent that craving is entirely cut off, to that extent all fetters are also cut off—thus he said, "the tenfold" and so on. "Rightly" (sammā) means thoroughly. And abandonment (pahāna) is indeed abandonment by means (upāyena) and by method (ñāyena)—thus he said, "by cause and by reason" (hetunā kāraṇena). "Realization by seeing" (dassanābhisamaya) means penetration without delusion (asammohapaṭivedha). For the path of arahantship, upon arising, indeed proceeds by destroying the delusion (moha) that conceals the true nature; thereby conceit (māna) is seen as it really is—this is his realization by seeing. Just as when the sun rises, darkness is destroyed and dispelled, so too when the path of arahantship arises, it is entirely abandoned and does not find footing in that continuum—this is his realization by abandonment (pahānābhisamaya). Therefore, it is said, "for the path of arahantship" and so on. "By way of function" (kiccavasena) means by way of the non-accomplishment of the function of seeing (dassanakicca), which is known as penetration without delusion.

Ye ime cattāro antāti sambandho. Mariyādantoti mariyādasaṅkhāto anto. Esa nayo sesattayepi. Tesūti catūsu antesu. Aduṃ catutthakoṭisaṅkhātaṃ sabbasseva vaṭṭadukkhassa antaṃ paricchedaṃ arahattamaggena mānassa diṭṭhattā pahīnattā ca akāsīti yojanā. Samussayo attabhāvo.

"These four extremes" (ye ime cattāro antā)—this is the connection. "The boundary-extreme" (mariyādanto) means the extreme known as the boundary. This method applies to the remaining three as well. "Among them" (tesu) means among the four extremes. The construction is that he made an end and a limit (antaṃ paricchedaṃ) of all the suffering of the round (vaṭṭadukkha), designated as the fourth category, because conceit (māna) was seen and abandoned by the path of arahantship. "The compound" (samussayo) is individual existence (attabhāvo).

Na riñcatīti na viveceti na vissajjeti. Tenāha “sampajaññaṃ na jahatī”ti, sampajaññaṃ na pariccajatīti attho. Saṅkhyaṃ nopetīti imassa “diṭṭhadhamme anāsavo”ti [M 2.0355] imassa vasena atthaṃ vadanto “ratto duṭṭho”ti avoca saupādisesanibbānavasena. Saṅkhyaṃ nopetīti vā diṭṭhe-dhamme anāsavo dhammaṭṭho vedagū kāyassa bhedā manusso devoti vā paññattiṃ na upetīti anupādisesanibbānavasenapi attho vattabbo. Ettha ca sukhādīsu vedanāsu yathākkamaṃ [V 2.0069] rāgānusayādayo kathitāti āha – “imasmiṃ sutte ārammaṇānusayo kathito”ti. Yo hi rāgādi paccayasamavāye atiiṭṭhādīsu uppajjanāraho maggena appahīno, so “rāgānusayo”tiādinā vutto, appahīnattho so maggena pahātabbo, na pariyuṭṭhānābhibhavatthoti.

"He does not relinquish" (na riñcati) means he does not separate from (na viveceti) or abandon (na vissajjeti). Therefore, it is said, "he does not abandon clear comprehension" (sampajaññaṃ na jahati); the meaning is he does not relinquish clear comprehension. Explaining the meaning of "He does not go to a reckoning" (saṅkhyaṃ nopeti) by way of "in this very life, he is without taints" (diṭṭhadhamme anāsavo), he spoke of "one who is lustful or hateful" (ratto duṭṭho) in terms of Nibbāna with residue remaining. Alternatively, the meaning of "He does not go to a reckoning" (saṅkhyaṃ nopeti) is also to be stated in terms of Nibbāna without residue remaining as: "in this very life, without taints, established in Dhamma, a knower of the Vedas," he does not go to the designation "after the breakup of the body, a human or a deva." Here, regarding pleasant and other feelings, the underlying tendencies of lust and so on are explained in due order—thus he said, "in this discourse, the object-based underlying tendency is explained." For that lust, etc., which is capable of arising in regard to extremely desirable objects and so on, when the conditions are met, and which is not yet abandoned by the path, is called "the underlying tendency of lust" (rāgānusayo) and so on; that which has not yet been abandoned is to be abandoned by the path, not for the purpose of merely overcoming its manifestation.

Pahānasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Pahāna Sutta is concluded.

4. Pātālasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Pātāla Sutta

252. Yattha patiṭṭhā natthi ekantikāti ekantikassa mahato pātassa alanti ayamettha attho adhippetoti āha “natthi ettha patiṭṭhā”ti. Asambhūtatthanti na sambhūtatthaṃ, musāti attho. Soti assutavā puthujjano. Yaṃ taṃ udakaṃ patatīti yojanā. Yasmā samuddapiṭṭhe antarantarā verambhavātasadiso mahāvāto uṭṭhahitvā mahāsamudde udakaṃ uggacchāpeti, taṃ kadāci cakkavāḷābhimukhaṃ, kadāci sinerupādābhimukhaṃ gantvā taṃ patihanati, tasmā vuttaṃ “balavāmukhaṃ mahāsamuddassā”tiādi. Vegena pakkhanditvāti mahatā vātavegena samuddhataṃ teneva vegena pakkhandantañca hutvā. Mahānarakapapāto viyāti yojanāyāmavitthāragambhīrasobbhapapāto viya hoti. Tathārūpānanti tattha vasituṃ samatthānaṃ. Asantanti abhūtaṃ. Atthavasena hi vācā abhūtaṃ nāma.

252. Where there is no footing is 'absolute' (ekantika); the meaning intended here is that this is sufficient for a great, absolute fall. Thus it is said, "There is no footing here." "Unreal" (asambhūtattaṃ) means not a real thing; the meaning is 'false.' "He" refers to an uninstructed worldling. The construction is: "that water which falls." Because on the surface of the ocean, from time to time, a great wind like the Verambha wind arises and causes the water of the great ocean to rise up. Sometimes it moves toward the world-rim, sometimes toward the foot of Mount Sineru, and strikes it. Therefore it is said, "The powerful mouth of the great ocean," etc. "Rushing swiftly" means, having been lifted by the great force of the wind, it surges and rushes forward with that same force. "Like the great abyss of hell" is like a chasm a yojana in length, width, and depth. "Of such a kind" (tathārūpānaṃ) refers to those capable of dwelling there. "Non-existent" (asantaṃ) means unreal. For, in terms of meaning, speech is called unreal.

Na patiṭṭhāsīti patiṭṭhaṃ na labhi. Anibaddhanti anibandhanatthaṃ yaṃkiñci. Dubbalañāṇoti ñāṇabalarahito. “Assutavā puthujjano”ti vatvā “sutavā ariyasāvako”ti vuttattā “sotāpanno dhura”nti vuttaṃ. Itaresu ariyasāvakesu vattabbameva natthi. Tesañhi vedanā supariññātā. Balavavipassako…pe… yogāvacaropi vaṭṭati mattaso vedanānaṃ pariññātattā.

"He did not find a footing" (na patiṭṭhāsi) means he did not obtain a footing. "Unbound" (anibaddhaṃ) means whatever is for the sake of non-binding. "Weak in knowledge" (dubbalañāṇo) means lacking the strength of knowledge. Having stated "an uninstructed worldling," and then "an instructed noble disciple" is stated, hence it is said, "a stream-enterer is foremost." Among the other noble disciples, there is nothing further to say. For them, feelings are fully understood. A strong insight meditator...and even a practitioner is suitable, because feelings are understood to a certain extent.

Pātālasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Pātāla Sutta is concluded.

5. Daṭṭhabbasuttavaṇṇanā

5. Commentary on the Daṭṭhabba Sutta

253. Vipariṇāmanavasena [M 2.0356] dukkhato daṭṭhabbāti kiñcāpi sukhā, pariṇāmadukkhatā pana sukhavedanāya ekantikāti. Vinivijjhanaṭṭhenāti pīḷanavasena attabhāvassa vijjhanaṭṭhena. Hutvāti paccayasamāgamena uppajjitvā. Tena pākabhāvapubbako attalābho vutto. Abhāvākārenāti bhaṅgupagamanākārena. Tena viddhaṃsabhāvo vutto. Ubhayena udayabbayaparicchinnatāya sikhappattaṃ aniccataṃ [V 2.0070] dasseti. Svāyaṃ hutvā abhāvākāro itarāsupi vedanāsu labbhateva, adhiko ca pana dvinnaṃ dukkhasabhāvo. Dukkhatāvasena purimānaṃ vedanānaṃ daṭṭhabbatāya dassitattā pacchimāya vedanāya evaṃ daṭṭhabbatā dassitā. Addāti ñāṇagatiyā sacchikatvā adakkhi. Ñāṇagamanañhetaṃ, yadidaṃ dukkhato dassanaṃ. Santasabhāvaṃ sukhadukkhato upasantarūpattā.

253. "To be seen as suffering by way of change" means that although it is pleasant, the suffering due to change is certain for pleasant feeling. "By the meaning of piercing through" means by the meaning of piercing individual existence by way of oppression. "Having been" means having arisen due to the coming together of conditions. Thus, the acquisition of existence is said to be preceded by a state of ripening. "In the mode of non-existence" means in the mode of going to dissolution. Thus, the nature of destruction is stated. By both, it shows impermanence, defined by rise and fall, reaching its peak. This mode of "having been and then non-existence" applies to the other feelings as well, but the suffering nature of the two is more prominent. Since the 'to-be-seen-ness' of the former feelings was shown by way of their suffering nature, the 'to-be-seen-ness' of the latter feeling is shown in the same way. "He saw" (addā) means he saw (adakkhi), having realized it through the course of knowledge. For this is the path of knowledge, namely, seeing as suffering. "Of a peaceful nature" because its form is more tranquil than pleasure and pain.

Daṭṭhabbasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Daṭṭhabba Sutta is concluded.

6. Sallasuttavaṇṇanā

6. Commentary on the Salla Sutta

254. Dvīsu janesūti sutavantaassutavantesu dvīsu janesu. Anugatavedhanti pubbe pavattavedhassa anugatavedhaṃ. Balavatarā diguṇā viya hutvā daḷhatarapavattiyā. Evamevāti yathā viddhassa purisassa anuvedhanā balavatarā, evameva. Samādhimaggaphalāni nissaraṇaṃ vikkhambhanasamucchedapaṭippassaddhivasena. Samādhisīsena hettha jhānañca gahitaṃ. So na jānāti anadhigatattā. Nissaraṇanti jānāti anissaraṇameva. Tāsaṃ samadhigatānaṃ sukhadukkhavedanānaṃ. Na vippayutto appahīnakilesattā. Saṅkhātadhammassāti saṅkhāya paññāya pariññātacatukkhandhassa. Tenāha “tulitadhammassā”ti. Imasmimpi sutte purimasutte viya ārammaṇānusayova kathito, so pana tattha vuttanayeneva veditabbo. Anurodhavirodhapahānassa vuttattā khīṇāsavo dhuraṃ. Anāgāmīpi vaṭṭati, tassa virodhappahānaṃ labbhati.

254. Among two types of people—the learned and the unlearned. 'Following the piercing' means the following piercing of what was initially pierced. Stronger, like being doubled, with a firmer momentum. Just as the subsequent feeling of a pierced man is stronger, so too. The escape through concentration, path, and fruition is by way of suppression, eradication, and tranquillization. Here, by 'concentration' as the heading, jhāna is also included. He does not know because it has not been attained. He knows what is not an escape as 'the escape'. Of those feelings of pleasure and pain that have been fully comprehended. Not dissociated, because the defilements have not been abandoned. 'One who has discerned the Dhamma' means one whose four aggregates have been fully known through discerning wisdom. Therefore, it is said, 'one who has weighed the Dhamma.' In this sutta, as in the previous one, the underlying tendency regarding the object is discussed, but it should be understood in the same way as explained there. Because the abandoning of attraction and aversion is stated, the one whose cankers are destroyed is foremost. The non-returner also applies, as the abandoning of aversion is attainable for him.

Sallasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Salla Sutta is concluded.

7. Paṭhamagelaññasuttavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the First Sickness Sutta

255. Saddahitvā [M 2.0357] gilāne upaṭṭhātabbe maññissantīti yojanā. Tatthāti tasmiṃ ṭhāne. Kammaṭṭhānānuyogo sappāyo yesaṃ te kammaṭṭhānasappāyā, satipaṭṭhānaratāti attho. Aniccataṃ anupassantoti kāyaṃ paṭicca uppannāya vedanāya ca aniccataṃ anupassanto. Vayaṃ anupassantotiādīsupi eseva nayo. Vayanti pana tāya eva khayasaṅkhātaṃ. Virāganti virajjanaṃ. Paṭinissagganti pariccāgapaṭinissaggaṃ, pakkhandanapaṭinissaggampi vā.

255. 'Thinking, 'They should tend to the sick out of faith''—this is the connection. 'There' means in that place. 'Suitable for meditation practice' means those for whom the application of meditation subjects is suitable; the meaning is delighting in the establishments of mindfulness. 'Contemplating impermanence' means contemplating the impermanence of the feeling that has arisen in dependence on the body. In 'contemplating vanishing,' and so on, the same method applies. 'Vanishing,' however, means its destruction. 'Fading away' means dispassion. 'Relinquishment' means relinquishment as giving up, or also relinquishment as abandoning.

Āgamanīyapaṭipadāti ariyamaggassa āgamanaṭṭhāniyā pubbabhāgapaṭipadā. Pubbabhāgāyeva na lokuttarā. Sampajaññaṃ pubbabhāgiyameva. Tisso anupassanā pubbabhāgāyeva vipassanāpariyāpannattā[V 2.0071]. Missakāti lokiyalokuttaramissakā. Bhāvanākālo dassito “nirodhānupassino viharato, paṭinissaggānupassino viharato rāgānusayo pahīyatī”ti vuttattā.

'The way of approach' is the preliminary practice that is the place of approach to the noble path. It is preliminary, and not supramundane. 'Clear comprehension' is preliminary. The three kinds of contemplation are preliminary, because they are included within insight. 'Mixed' means mixed mundane and supramundane. The time for cultivation is shown, for it was stated: "When one dwells contemplating cessation, dwells contemplating relinquishment, the underlying tendency to lust is abandoned."

Paṭhamagelaññasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Sickness Sutta is finished.

8-9. Dutiyagelaññasuttādivaṇṇanā

8-9. Commentary on the Second Sickness Sutta and others

256-257. Phassaṃ paṭiccāti ettha phassasīsena phassāyatanānaṃ gahaṇaṃ. Na hi phassāyatanehi vinā phassassa sambhavo. Tenāha “kāyova hi ettha phassoti vutto”ti, phassasīsena vuttoti adhippāyo. Navamaṃ uttānameva heṭṭhā vuttanayattā.

256-257. Regarding 'dependent on contact,' here by 'contact' as the heading, the contact bases are included. For without the contact bases, contact cannot arise. Therefore, it is said, 'Indeed, the body is called contact here'—the meaning is that it is stated by way of 'contact' as the heading. The ninth sutta is clear, since the method has been stated previously.

Dutiyagelaññasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Second Sickness Sutta and others is concluded.

10. Phassamūlakasuttavaṇṇanā

10. Commentary on the Sutta Rooted in Contact

258. Sukhavedanāya hitaṃ sukhavedaniyaṃ. So panesa hitabhāvo paccayabhāvenāti āha “sukhavedanāya paccayabhūta”nti. Imasmimpi suttadvayeti [M 2.0358] imasmiṃ navame dasame ca suttepi. Sammasanacāravedanāti sammasanīyavedanā eva kathitā, na lokuttarāti adhippāyo.

258. What is beneficial for pleasant feeling is 'pleasant-feeling-producing' (sukhavedaniya). And this beneficial nature exists by way of being a condition, hence it is said, 'a condition for pleasant feeling.' This also applies to these two suttas—that is, in this ninth and tenth sutta. By 'feeling for thorough comprehension' (sammasanacāravedanā) is meant only feelings that are to be thoroughly comprehended, not supramundane ones—this is the intended meaning.

Phassamūlakasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Phassamūlaka Sutta is concluded.

Sagāthāvaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Chapter with Verses is concluded.

2. Rahogatavaggo

2. The Chapter on Solitude

1. Rahogatasuttavaṇṇanā

1. Commentary on the Rahogata Sutta

259. Yaṃkiñci vedayitanti “sukhaṃ dukkhaṃ adukkhamasukha”nti vuttaṃ vedayitaṃ dukkhasmiṃ antogadhaṃ, dukkhanti vattabbataṃ labhati pariyāyenāti attho. Tenāha “taṃ sabbaṃ dukkhanti attho”ti. Yā esātiādīsu yo saṅkhārānaṃ aniccatāsaṅkhāto hutvā abhāvākāro, yā khayasabhāvatā vinassanasabhāvatā jarāya maraṇena cāti dvidhā vipariṇāmanasabhāvatā, etaṃ sandhāya uddissa sabbaṃ vedayitaṃ dukkhanti mayā vuttanti ayaṃ saṅkhepattho. Sāti saṅkhārānaṃ aniccatā[V 2.0072]. Vedanānampi aniccatā eva saṅkhārasabhāvattā. Tāsaṃ aniccatā ca nāma maraṇaṃ bhaṅgoti katvā tato uttari dukkhaṃ nāma natthīti sabbā vedanā “dukkhā”ti vuttā, yathā “yadaniccaṃ taṃ dukkha”nti ca, “pañcupādānakkhandhā dukkhā”ti ca vuttaṃ. Idaṃ suttapadaṃ. Cattāro āruppāti catasso arūpasamāpattiyo. Etthāti ca etasmiṃ paṭippassaddhivāre.

259. Whatever is felt—that is, the feeling spoken of as 'pleasant, painful, or neither-painful-nor-pleasant'—is included within suffering, and by implication, it obtains the designation 'suffering'; this is the meaning. Hence it is said, 'All that is suffering'—this is the meaning. In phrases such as 'that which is,' the concise meaning is this: 'With reference to and concerning that which is the state of non-being, designated as the impermanence of formations; that which is the nature of destruction; the nature of perishing; and the twofold nature of change through aging and death—I have said that all feeling is suffering.' That is the impermanence of formations. The impermanence of feelings is also the same, because they have the nature of formations. And considering that their impermanence—namely, death and dissolution—is such that there is no suffering beyond it, all feelings are called 'suffering,' just as it is said, 'What is impermanent is suffering,' and 'The five aggregates subject to clinging are suffering.' This is a sutta passage. By 'the four formless states' (āruppa) are meant the four formless attainments. And 'here' refers to this instance of stilling.

Rahogatasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Rahogata Sutta is concluded.

4. Agārasuttavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Sutta on the Householder

262. Puratthimāti vibhattilopena niddeso, nissakke vā etaṃ paccattavacanaṃ. Paṭhamajjhānādivasenāti paṭhamadutiyajjhānavasena. Anussativasenāti nidassanamattaṃ aññassapi upacārajjhānaggahitassa savitakkacārassa nirāmisassa sukhassa labbhanato. Tassapi vā paṭhamajjhānādīti ettha ādi-saddena saṅgaho daṭṭhabbo. Kāmahetu dukkhappattānaṃ dukkhavedanā kāmāmisena [M 2.0359] sāmisā vedanā. Anuttaravimokkhā nāma arahattaphalaṃ. “Kudāssu nāmāhaṃ tadāyatanaṃ upasampajja viharissāmī”ti evaṃ pihaṃ upaṭṭhāpayato tasmiṃ anijjhamāne pihappaccayā uppannadomanassavedanā. Nekkhammanissitā upekkhāvedanā nirāmisā adukkhamasukhā nāma. Savisesaṃ pana dassetuṃ “catutthajjhānavasena uppannā adukkhamasukhavedanā”ti vuttaṃ.

262. The term 'puratthimā' is a directive with an elided case ending, or it is a personal pronoun in the nominative case. 'By means of the first jhāna, etc.' means by means of the first and second jhānas. 'By way of recollection' is merely an illustration, since non-sensual happiness associated with initial and sustained thought can be obtained even by one who has attained access concentration. Or, in 'his first jhāna, etc.,' the word 'etc.' should be understood as inclusive of that too. The painful feeling of those who have attained suffering due to sensual pleasures is a sensual feeling, tainted by sensuality. The unsurpassed liberation is the fruition of arahantship. For one who establishes a longing such as, 'When, indeed, shall I attain and dwell in that sphere?' when that is not accomplished, a feeling of grief arises conditioned by that longing. The equanimous feeling based on renunciation is called the non-sensual, neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling. But to show a specific case, it is said: 'The neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling that has arisen by means of the fourth jhāna.'

Agārasuttavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Agāra Sutta is concluded.

5-8. Paṭhamaānandasuttādivaṇṇanā

5-8. Commentary on the First Sutta to Ānanda and others

263-266. Heṭṭhā kathitanayāneva, veneyyajjhāsayato pana tesaṃ desanāti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Etthāti etesu catūsu suttesu. Purimāni dve “paṭhamaṃ jhānaṃ samāpannassa vācā paṭippassaddhā hotī”tiādinā nayena yāva khīṇāsavassa kilesapaṭippassaddhi, tāva desanāya pavattattā “paripuṇṇapassaddhikānī”ti vuttāni. Tāva paripūraṃ katvā adesitattā “pacchimāni upaḍḍhapassaddhikāni, desanāyā”tiādi vuttaṃ.

263-266. The method is the same as explained previously, but it should be understood that the teaching is given according to the disposition of those to be trained. Here, in these four suttas, the first two are called 'those with complete stilling' because the teaching proceeds in the manner beginning, 'For one who has attained the first jhāna, speech is stilled,' all the way up to the stilling of the defilements of one whose cankers are destroyed. The latter two are called 'those with partial stilling' because they are not taught completely up to that point.

Paṭhamaānandasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the First Sutta to Ānanda and others is concluded.

9-10. Pañcakaṅgasuttādivaṇṇanā

9-10. Commentary on the Pañcakaṅga Sutta and others

267-268. Daṇḍamuggaraṃ aggasoṇḍamuggaraṃ. Kāḷasuttapakkhipanaṃ kāḷasuttanāḷi. Vāsiādīni [V 2.0073] pañca aṅgāni assāti pañcakaṅgo. Vatthuvijjāya vuttavidhinā kattabbanissayāni ṭhapetīti thapati. Paṇḍitaudāyitthero, na lāḷudāyitthero. Dvepānandāti aṭṭhakathāya paduddhāro kato – “dvepi mayā ānandā”ti pana pāḷiyaṃ. Pasādakāyasannissitā kāyikā, cetosannissitā cetasikā. Ādhipaccaṭṭhena sukhameva indriyanti sukhindriyaṃ. Upavicāravasenāti rūpādīni upecca vicaraṇavasena. Atīte ārammaṇe. Paccuppanneti addhāpaccuppanne. Evaṃ aṭṭhādhikaṃ sataṃ aṭṭhasataṃ.

267-268. Daṇḍamuggaraṃ means a chief elephant's goad. Kāḷasuttapakkhipanaṃ means a black thread tube. One who has five components (aṅga), such as an adze (vāsi), is called pañcakaṅga (five-component). One who establishes the supports to be made according to the method stated in the science of architecture is called a thapati (master builder). It is the Elder Paṇḍitaudāyi, not the Elder Lāḷudāyi. The phrase 'dvepānandā' is an extraction of the word made by the commentary, but in the Pāli text it is 'both by me, Ānanda.' Those dependent on the sensitive body are bodily; those dependent on the mind are mental. In the sense of dominance, pleasure itself is a faculty; hence 'pleasure-faculty' (sukhindriya). 'By way of investigating' (upavicāravasena) means by way of approaching and investigating forms and so on. In a past object. 'In the present' means in the temporal present. Thus, one hundred plus eight is one hundred and eight (aṭṭhasataṃ).

Pāṭiyekko anusandhīti pucchānusandhiādīhi visuṃ tehi asammisso eko anusandhi. Ekāpi vedanā kathitā “tatra, bhikkhave, sutavā ariyasāvako”ti āharitvā “phassapaccayā vedanā”ti. Yasmā bhagavā [M 2.0360] catutthajjhānupekkhāvedanaṃ vatvā – “atthānanda, etasmā sukhā aññaṃ sukha”ntiādiṃ vadanto thapatino vādaṃ upatthambheti nāma. Tena hi upekkhāvedanā “sukha”nteva vuttā santasabhāvattā. Abhikkantataranti ativiya kantataraṃ manoramataraṃ. Tenāha “sundaratara”nti. Paṇītataranti padhānabhāvaṃ nītatāya uḷārataraṃ. Tenāha “atappakatara”nti. Sukhanti vuttā paṭipakkhassa suṭṭhu khādanena, sukaraṃ okāsadānamassāti vā. Nirodho sukhaṃ nāma sabbaso udayabbayābhāvato. Tenāha “niddukkhabhāvasaṅkhātena sukhaṭṭhenā”ti.

Pāṭiyekko anusandhī means a single connection, distinct and unmixed with other connections such as the question-and-answer connection. Even a single feeling is discussed, as when it is introduced by 'Here, monks, a learned noble disciple,' and then 'feeling arises dependent on contact.' Since the Blessed One, having spoken of the equanimous feeling of the fourth jhāna, declares, 'There is, Ānanda, a happiness other than this happiness,' and so on, he thereby supports the view of the thapati. Therefore, the equanimous feeling is called 'happiness' precisely because of its peaceful nature. Abhikkantataraṃ means exceedingly more delightful, more pleasing to the mind. Hence it is said, 'more beautiful.' Paṇītataranti means more elevated due to its leading position, more surpassing. Hence it is said, 'more untroubled.' It is called 'happiness' because it thoroughly consumes its opposite, or because it easily grants opportunity. Cessation is called happiness because it is entirely free from arising and passing away. Hence it is said, 'by the meaning of happiness, in the sense of the state of being free from suffering.'

Sukhasmiṃyevāti sukhamicceva paññapeti. Nirodhasamāpattiṃ sīsaṃ katvāti nirodhasamāpattidesanāya sīsaṃ uttamaṃ katvā. Desanāya uddesādhimutte uṭṭhāpetvā vitthāritattā “neyyapuggalassa vasenā”ti vuttaṃ. Dasamaṃ anantarasutte vuttanayattā uttānatthameva.

'In happiness alone' means it is conceived as happiness itself. 'Having made cessation attainment the head' means having made cessation attainment the foremost in the teaching. Because it was explained in detail after raising it from the summary and the inclination towards the teaching, it is said 'by means of a person to be guided.' The tenth sutta, being in accordance with the method stated in the immediately preceding sutta, has a plain meaning.

Pañcakaṅgasuttādivaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Pañcakaṅga Sutta and others is concluded.

Rahogatavaggavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The commentary on the Rahogata Vagga is concluded.

3. Aṭṭhasatapariyāyavaggo

3. The Chapter on the Discourse of One Hundred and Eight

1. Sīvakasuttavaṇṇanā

1. The Commentary on the Sīvaka Sutta
